Ezra reported it twice — "When leaving a project for a split second there's a black bar at the bottom", then "Black bar again" with the band under the + on home. Measured live: <html> painted a flat #060c0f with background-image:none while <body> carried the glass theme's two radial gradients. Once <html> has a background of its own, <body>'s stops propagating to the document CANVAS — and the canvas paints the whole web view, which under viewport-fit=cover is taller than the layout viewport. So any strip the page did not cover was the ground colour with none of the theme's light, sitting directly under an area that had it: same colour, different brightness, a flat band along the bottom edge. This is the RESIDUAL of v6.85, which matched the canvas colour and stopped there. The gradients live on <html> now and <body> is transparent so they cannot double-paint. The durable part is the test: it asserts, in BOTH themes, that the canvas is never black against a non-black ground (the v6.85 bar), never flat against a lit page (this one), and never doubled. Three passes at this family have each fixed one property of the canvas; the invariant is "html paints what the page paints" and it is now pinned. Mutation-checked by putting the light back on body. Suite 247/247.
- v7.03 — open paths curve all the way to their ends (#158). Ezra: "Spiral shapes last little bit is straight instead of round." It was never the spiral's data. Each point's tangent is the Catmull-Rom (next − prev)/6; on a CLOSED path both neighbours exist, but on an OPEN one the missing neighbour was clamped to the endpoint ITSELF, so at the last point `next` was p and the tangent collapsed to (p − prev)/6 — half length, aimed down the chord. Invisible on a closed shape; on the spiral's final wide sweep at maximum radius it is a straight tail. Reflecting the missing neighbour across the endpoint (2p − prev, the standard phantom point) restores (p − prev)/3, the magnitude that carries the curve out to its end. It fixes every open path at once — the spiral, and freehand and vector drawings, whose first and last strokes had been flattening for the same reason with nobody connecting the two. Asserted as geometry rather than by eye: the end handle must be ~chord/3 not ~chord/6, the start handle likewise, a CLOSED path's control points must not move at all, and the real spiral's last segment must bow off its chord. Mutation-checked. Suite 248/248.
- v7.04 — the Freehand Drawing icon is a pencil (#161), and Vector Drawing stops being one too. Ezra: "Make the free hand drawing icon a pencil." Freehand's mark was a squiggle with a nib, which says "a wavy line" rather than naming the tool; it is a pencil now, drawn with a ferrule band and a tip band because a bare angled rectangle at 22px is just a stick. The second half was not asked for and is not scope creep but the other side of the same change: Vector Drawing's icon was ALREADY a pencil silhouette with two anchor dots, so doing only what was asked would have left two pencils side by side in one list — the exact fault the Elements cube note in this file records, "at 22px the two were nearly the same mark and neither told you what it opened". Vector is now a curve between two anchor squares, which is what vector drawing is and shares nothing with a pencil at any size. Flagged in REQUESTS.md in case he would rather Vector kept its old mark. Both photographed at 380px. Suite 248/248.
- v7.05 — the loading screen is pure black again (#162). Ezra: "Loading screen isn't fully black anymore", with a screenshot of the splash video's black frame sitting inside a navy surround. It was deliberate and the reasoning had expired: queue 143 changed the surround from #000 to the app ground because a pure-black splash left the UNCOVERED canvas beside it standing out as a bar — the first ever sighting of that bug — and its note claimed "#060c0f against a black video edge is imperceptible at this size". His screenshot disproves the claim, and v7.02 removed the reason behind it, since the canvas now paints what the page paints. So the splash is #000 again AND the canvas goes black with it for as long as it is up: without that second half the bands would just move outward, because the splash covers the layout viewport while the web view under viewport-fit=cover is taller. The mark is dropped when the splash is removed, putting the theme's light straight back. Suite 248/248.
- v7.06 — a freehand stroke stops doubling when you let go (#164). Ezra: "When I do freehand drawing and finish a stroke it will for some reason make the stroke thicker when I let go of drawing." It was exactly 2x, and the cause is that the compositor draws an OPEN path twice: a border under-stroke at lw*2, so half shows on each side of the line, and then the line itself at lw in layer.fill. addPathLayer was handing every freehand stroke a border that was ENABLED and the SAME COLOUR as the line — so the outline was invisible as an outline and did nothing but make the mark 2x wide, while the live preview strokes at lw. Border off by default; the width still comes from the same place, since the compositor reads stk.width whether or not the border is enabled. Turning the border on in Border & Shadow now does what it says: an outline around the line in whatever colour you choose. The test measures RENDERED THICKNESS rather than the stored numbers, because the bug was a factor of two in pixels while every stored value looked correct. Suite 249/249.
- v7.07 — a freehand drawing is ONE layer again (#167), which also removes the cause of #166. Ezra: "when you draw with free hand drawing it creates multiple layers, it should all be inside the one drawing you just made not keep creating more" — his screenshot showed nine Freehand rows from a single drawing. commitStroke() called addPathLayer per stroke, by design ("a stroke is committed and the tool stays armed for the next one"), and the renderer never needed that: layer.subs has been a multi-subpath field all along with nothing ever writing more than one into it. Now the first stroke creates the layer and every stroke after it is appended, with the layer re-fitted around the union — the box grows and all strokes are re-normalised into it, because subs live in [0,1] of the layer's own box and a stroke drawn outside the old box would otherwise land outside the drawing. Id, stack position and selection are preserved; only geometry moves. Reset on startDraw as well as stop(), since without that a second drawing would silently append to the first one's layer. This is also why the timeline would not scroll (#166): a row per stroke left no empty lane to start a vertical swipe on. The test asserts one layer, two subs, no stale layer.points, a box that grew to hold both, and every point inside [0,1] of it. Suite 250/250.
- v7.08 — a project on a custom frame rate can finally be exported at it (#141, part 2). Ezra: "if you made a custom fps or other things etc there's no way to export at that." He was exactly right, and the code said so out loud: the export dialog's frame-rate list was a fixed ladder of 15/25/30/50/60/120 whose own comment read "there is no Custom on this dialog", so a project built at 48fps had no route to a 48fps render. It now leads with "Same as project — N fps", labelled with the project's real rate at the moment the dialog opens (built there rather than in the markup, for the same reason the resolution list builds "Full — W×H": it depends on the project), and it selects itself automatically whenever the project's rate is not one of the rungs. A standard 30fps project still lands on its own rung, so the ordinary path is untouched. Mutation-checked by deleting the option. Suite 251/251.
- v7.09 — custom export sizes, so a project can be rendered at a ratio that is not its own (#141, part 1). Ezra: "there's no way to do custom export ratios, or fps." Every rung in the resolution list was a uniform SCALE of the project, which is precisely why the dialog could only ever produce the project's own aspect; the list now ends with "Custom size…", revealing width and height fields that hand the exporter explicit dimensions. The frame is CONTAINED in whatever is typed and never cropped — exporting 9:16 work as 1:1 must letterbox, and cover/crop would silently discard part of what someone made, which is not a size option. Bars take the project's background colour, or stay transparent on a transparent export. Bitrate is computed from the REAL output rather than project×scale, or a large custom render would be encoded at the project's bitrate and look starved. The fit maths lives in FM.exportFitRect, pure and exposed, so it can be tested without running an encoder: an export is the one operation you cannot casually re-run to see. The test checks containment, aspect preservation, centring, that one axis is always filled (no needless shrinking), and that a same-aspect export does not report letterboxing; mutation-checked by switching contain to cover. Suite 252/252.
- v7.10 — the project notepad, with export reminders (#139). Ezra: "put a little note pad icon and make it so you can add notes about the project and reminders, make it so you can tick wether it will remind you to do these things when you press the export button." Notes live on the project so they travel and save with it, and the tick is the whole design: unticked it is a note and never interrupts, ticked it is shown once before the export dialog with Back and Export anyway. Back is deliberately the louder button — a reminder exists because you probably meant to do the thing first. Nothing ticked means no popup at all, and a ticked-but-empty note does not count, since it would interrupt with nothing to say. The gate lives inside showExportDialog, the one funnel every export route already goes through (top bar, phone bar, home ⋯ → Export video), so it covers all of them and cannot drift when a fourth appears. On the phone it lives in Settings rather than the top bar, and that was forced: a bar button was built first and the suite caught a real hazard — any extra control in that group shifts the settings cog into the spot the delete bin takes in select mode, "a thumb going where it has always gone hits delete". Desktop gets a dedicated notepad button beside Export with a dot when something is ticked. Suite 253/253 at both widths.
- v7.11 — the cog is now genuinely the source of truth for export: the export dialog no longer remembers your frame rate and resolution, so it inherits both from Canvas settings every time and an export-time change is a one-off. Format and quality are still remembered (nothing else owns them). Before this, a resolution picked once outranked the cog forever — change the project to 48fps and export still opened on last week's 60.
- v7.12 — the Faves pull-down can be CANCELLED by pulling back up, from wherever you got to, and the cancel sticks so shoving back down cannot re-arm it; the hint now says which of the three things letting go will do (Faves / Release to open / Cancelled) and the block glides home under your finger when you call it off. Everything that said "All favourites" or "Favourites" now says "Faves", audio effects included.
- v7.12 — the notepad is PAPER: a yellow glued top edge and a warm white sheet, ruled feint lines, writing on the page instead of in dark boxes. The pre-export reminder card matches — and every reminder on it now has a tick, so you can deal with one on the spot instead of going Back → notepad → untick → export again. Ticked rows stay, struck through, so the list never jumps under your finger mid-decision; once nothing is outstanding the card says "All clear" and the button is just "Export".
- v7.12 — the notes button moved to the left of the settings cog on both bars, and on the phone it leaves whenever a selection owns the bar — it is in exactly the same hide-lists as the cog, so the delete bin can never land on a slot your thumb learned as something else.
- v7.13 — the export resolution list now says "Same as project — 1080×1920" instead of "Full", matching the words the frame rate above it already used; quality defaults to High, and a Medium remembered from an older build no longer outranks that default (it is dropped once, then your own choice is kept).
- v7.13 — the notes button sits properly between the version chip and the cog (both gaps 24px optically; the chip has no internal padding and the icon buttons do, which is why an even 4px gap looked shoved left), and its icon is a little sheet of paper — white page, yellow edge, dark rules — like the panel it opens.
- v7.14 — Loop playback is out of the settings cog; the ⛶ view bar's loop button is its one home now. Reset project is gone entirely — the button, its confirm, and the 24 lines of teardown behind it, which nothing else called.
- v7.15 — the Classic theme option is gone; Liquid is the look. (The Classic|Studio LAYOUT row is a different setting that shares the word, and it stays.) A "classic" saved before this is ignored on load rather than honoured, so nobody is left on a look with no way back.
- v7.15 — the layer ⋯ says "Save layer's effects as preset…" instead of a bare "Save as preset…", which also tells it apart from an individual effect's own Save as preset.
- v7.16 — you can swipe the timeline up and down again with a full layer list: a vertical drag that starts ON a clip now scrolls the track list instead of scrubbing. It used to work only from empty lane, which is exactly what runs out when you have a lot of layers. Horizontal still scrubs and wins ties.
- v7.17 — the add-menu shape icons now match the shapes they add. Three faults, all in the icon renderer and none in the shape data: it squashed every shape into a square box instead of its real aspect (a banner shown at 1.84:1 arrived at 4.08:1), it filled each subpath separately so holes filled in solid (a clock was a plain disc), and it drew smooth outlines as straight lines. Across all 54: 2 plainly wrong and 14 drifting became 0 and 6.
- v7.17 — the notes button reads right in the bar: its page glyph was drawn a third narrower than the cog beside it, so the same gap looked wider around it. The glyph fills its box now and the extra nudge came off.
- v7.18 — finishing a vector drawing now ends the drawing session BEFORE adding the layer, so the inspector can no longer open and measure itself while `body.drawing` is hiding it. That is a real fault on the path Ezra reported (#179), but the harness would not reproduce the full-screen panel he photographed, so it is a likely fix rather than a proven one — flagged as such in REQUESTS.md.
- v7.19 — FreeMotion actually works offline now. sw.js had been a ZERO-BYTE file, so the app registered a service worker that did nothing while calling itself an installable PWA. The new one is built around the update mechanism rather than against it: the page is always fetched from the network when there is one (so a new build can never be hidden behind a cached index.html), while `?v=`-versioned scripts and styles are served from cache, since that query already guarantees their bytes never change. Proven with the network cut AND Chrome's own cache disabled, so only the worker could answer. The second launch is the one that works offline — the cache fills as the app loads.
- v7.20 — the effect browser's preview tiles can no longer come up permanently blank. When the sample photographs finish decoding, every tile is invalidated and re-mounted — but the re-mount only found tiles already in the document, while the clear-down wiped the queue, so any tile mounted-but-not-yet-inserted was dropped with nothing to bring it back. The browser sits right in that window: tiles are mounted while still detached and appended afterwards. In-flight tiles are now carried across the invalidation.
- v7.21 — Fill Behind's Blur works on a phone. The blur was never the problem: the effect was skipping the fill entirely, because its "this layer already covers the frame, do nothing" test read a deliberately loose bounding box as if it were exact. A layer leaving a small margin looked edge-to-edge — and since that margin is measured in device pixels, it vanished as the preview quality tier dropped, so the fill quietly stopped drawing on a phone while every test at full scale passed. Coverage is now decided by the exact opaque core.
- v7.22 — Film Grain has a Grain shape control: Square or Round, defaulting to Round, so the effect browser's tile shows the round version too. Real grain is a particle rather than a tile, so a round grain fades to nothing at the rim of each cell and the field stops reading as a grid. Projects saved before this keep square grain exactly as it was.
- v7.23 — the phone bar's right-hand run is finally evenly spaced: refresh · notes · cog · Export now sit 24.1px apart by the eye, where they were 19 / 28 / 15. The two earlier attempts measured tap boxes, which are all 42px and told a comforting lie; these four controls present different edges — the version chip and Export are painted to their borders while the icons sit ~11px inside their targets — so equal gaps look unequal. Measured on real ink this time.
- v7.23 — the floating "Editing group" pill is gone; the top-left back button already steps out of a group before it leaves the project, on both phone and desktop.
- v7.24 — the Import buttons in Media and Audio no longer end in "…". It looked like a truncated word on a phone; it was actually the desktop convention for "this opens a picker", which says nothing when every tile opens something. (Checked it was not CSS truncation first — no label in any tab is actually cut.)
- v7.24 — Project notes is out of App settings. It only lived there while the notepad had no phone button; it has one on both bars now, so the settings row was a second door to the same panel.
- v7.25 — grouping now visibly puts the layers inside the group. Nothing was ever being duplicated: a new group simply opened expanded, so its members stayed listed on the top-level timeline underneath it, which looks exactly like copies left outside — and with nested groups every level was listed at once. New groups start closed; press the chevron or go inside to look.
- v7.25 — the little arrow on a group row no longer shoves the rest of the row sideways. It is the expand/collapse toggle, and only group rows had one, so their eye and name started 16px further right than everything else. Every row reserves the slot now, and the arrow's tooltip says what it does.
- v7.26 — the + button carries the home screen's own light: the same three pools (teal, violet, blue) drifting on mismatched clocks, rebuilt for a 64px circle rather than shrunk — stronger colour, tighter radii and four times the relative travel, because a background's 6% drift is four invisible pixels at this size. Transform-only so it composites rather than repaints over the timeline, and it stops under reduced-motion. The glow it throws is now teal and violet-blue to match what the orb actually contains.
- v7.27 — follow-up on the + button after a second look found two faults in what shipped: the white + had lost contrast against its own brighter face (3.2:1, at the floor), and the violet pool was still drifting off the disc so the orb read as two hues for about a quarter of its cycle. The shade under the glyph now sits over the pools rather than beneath them, and the violet stays on the face. Measured after: 4.5–4.9:1 contrast at every phase, three hues visible throughout.
- v7.28 — the top-bar run is spaced 18 / 24 / 18 by the eye: the notes↔cog gap Ezra called perfect is untouched, and the two gaps either side of it — against the version chip and the Export button — are brought in to match each other. Equal distances were not equal appearance: a gap bounded by a hard painted edge reads wider than the same gap between two soft icons.
- v7.28 — the + button has its glass rim back. The coloured aura is opaque and the rim is an inset shadow, so the aura had buried it and the orb was lit from below, which is what makes something read as a printed disc rather than a lens. It also no longer sits frozen for the first two seconds after the editor opens, and it no longer relies on anything WebKit is known to break.
- v7.29 — Add ▸ Audio has a Sound effects menu: sixteen effects across Movement, Impact, Build, Interface and Texture, every one generated in the app rather than sampled — so there is no licence question, nothing extra to download, and any of them can be re-tuned by changing a number. Tap a name to hear it before you commit; Add drops it in as an ordinary audio clip that trims, fades and exports like any import.
- v7.30 — a clip that is still decoding now says so: a small rotating arc appears bottom-left, naming the clip, and goes the moment the first frame is available. Measured on a real import — for about half a second after adding a 1280×720 video (much longer on a phone) the layer exists and the canvas can only draw nothing, which is exactly the "layers I add are invisible" and "video seemingly broken" reports. Nothing was wrong with the import; the app never said it was working.
- v7.30 — the version shown in the header is now checked against POLISH-LOG by the test suite. It had silently fallen two releases behind: v7.28 and v7.29 both shipped while the header still read v7.27, because bumping it is a find-and-replace and a find that matches nothing fails quietly.
- v7.31 — Extract Audio now produces something that looks like audio: the extracted twin draws a waveform on the timeline instead of a filmstrip of invisible frames identical to the clip it came from. The waveform machinery was already there and already handled trim, speed and reverse — it was simply unreachable for a layer that still carried a picture, which the twin does.
- v7.32 — Wiggle no longer eats a layer that is part-way off screen. It worked on a frame-sized plate, so the off-frame half of a layer had already been discarded before Wiggle saw it: displacing it back toward the middle revealed nothing and the layer could only ever lose ink — measured, a layer half off the corner lost a third of itself at amount 90. It now sources from a plate that reaches past the frame, so that content comes back. Layers away from the edges take the same cheap path as before, byte-identically.
- v7.33 — pressing Play no longer starts with a pitched-up catch-up. The transport used to start the instant you pressed it while the audio took about 200ms to actually begin, and rather than waiting it dragged the sound up to the playhead by speeding it up — measured, the rate sat pinned at its +10% ceiling for 55 decisions in a row at the start of every play. It now holds the picture for those couple of hundred milliseconds and starts in step with the sound, with a deadline so a silent or blocked clip can never freeze playback.
- v7.34 — a song no longer goes silent partway through. The app trusts a decode over the file's own header, because plenty of files lie about their length — but playback was still gating on the header's shorter claim, so everything past it was treated as "off the end of the source" and held silent while the playhead ran on. A file claiming 11 seconds but actually running 26 played only its first 11. Even well-formed files had a 60ms dead tail at the end.
- v7.35 — the drawing and Add Text screens no longer shove the canvas to the bottom of the phone. One CSS rule collapsed the layout to a single row while the top bar and the stage are placed on separate ones, so the stage went content-sized and every spare pixel piled up above it. It hid for months because it depends on your project's shape: measured, a 9:16 project showed a 22px band (invisible) while a square one showed 314px and a landscape one 477px. Both screens now centre the canvas.
- v7.36 — the timeline ruler's notches no longer vanish when you zoom in on a long project. They were thinned according to the whole project's length, so a long project started coarse and zooming in only spread that coarse step further apart — measured, a 30-minute project showed a single notch 1577px away at full zoom. The ruler now draws only the stretch you can see, so density follows the zoom and a 30-minute project looks exactly like a 15-second one. It also stops every timeline rebuild paying to lay out the whole timeline: around 1,050 elements on a 5-minute project, now at most ~112.
- v7.37 — hold any button on the canvas view rail and it tells you what it is. The rail is icon-only with no room for captions — it is 46px wide and already scrolls because it is full — and on a phone a tooltip does nothing, so there was no way to find out what a control was short of pressing it. Holding shows its name and swallows the press, so asking never also toggles the thing. The two timeline-zoom buttons keep their own hold meaning.
- v7.38 — the effect stack stops trusting the file it came from (queue 113, step 1 of the filters plan). Six more type-keyed render tables were still inheriting from Object.prototype — TEXT_FX was the dangerous one, because its lookup CALLS what it finds, so a saved project naming an effect "valueOf" threw out of the render and killed the frame. The effects browser had a worse version keyed off localStorage: a junk id in your recents list built a tile out of a prototype function and took the browser down on open. And layer.effects, alone among the layer's sub-structures, had NO validation on the way in — not on import, and not on the autosave load that every project takes on every open. It has one now, written so a valid effect comes out exactly as it went in: absent params stay absent (they fall back to a legacy value, not the schema default, so filling them would have restyled every old project). Fixed on the way past: keyframe curves shaped by hand were losing their tangents and parameterised eases on every reload.
- v7.39 — one walker over the effect stack, so nothing goes missing inside a filter (queue 113, step 2 of the filters plan). Seven places walked a layer's effects exactly one level deep, and every one of them fails SILENTLY once an effect can hold other effects: no keyframe diamonds on the timeline for anything inside a filter, keyframes you can see and cannot delete, keyframe copy/paste landing on a different parameter than the one you copied, an audio link driving the wrong effect, and a Luma Matte / Compound Blur / Match Grade / Displacement Map keeping a DEAD layer id after a duplicate, import or template insert — rendering plain with no error to tell you. They all go through FM.eachFx now, and the three separate keyframe address grammars share one parser that reads both the old form and the nested one, so every saved project and every existing audio link keeps working. Nothing creates a filter yet; this is the groundwork landing before anything can depend on it.
- v7.40 — a filter now actually renders, and its Strength fades the whole group (queue 113, step 3 of the filters plan — the risky one). A filter is one effect in the stack that holds other effects, and Strength cross-fades between the picture with those effects and the picture without. Deliberately NOT done by scaling the children's settings, which is the obvious approach and wrong three ways over: half a colour is meaningless, half a "mode" switch corrupts the look rather than weakening it, and scaling a child that has keyframes would rewrite them permanently. Both ends are exact rather than close — at Strength 1 a filter is byte-for-byte the same picture as its effects sitting in the stack directly, and at Strength 0 it is byte-for-byte the same as the filter not being there. The mix in between is a true blend, not the "draw one over the other" shortcut used elsewhere in the compositor: that shortcut is only correct where the top image is fully opaque, so it would have gone visibly wrong on soft edges and on any filter containing a key or a matte. Nothing in the UI creates a filter yet — that is the next step.
- v7.41 — you can now make a filter and see what is in it (queue 113, step 4 of the filters plan — the first bit you can actually touch). "+ Add Filter" sits under "+ Add Effect" in the Effects panel and drops in an empty one, already open, with its own "+ Add effect to this filter" button inside. The effects you put in it appear as normal rows indented under it — same open/close, eye, delete, swipe and drag — with the filter's Strength above them, because that is the control acting on all of them. Everything inside a filter is properly scoped to the filter: deleting a child removes it from the FILTER rather than from whatever unrelated effect sits at that position in the layer's list, and opening a child no longer closes the filter holding it. One bug caught only by the phone screenshot, with every DOM check green: the three closed effects inside an open filter were all drawing the OPEN arrow, because the rule that turns the arrow was matching every chevron underneath an open row instead of that row's own.
- v7.42 — a filter is judged by what is inside it (queue 113, §8). The plan predicted that copying a look containing a filter, or saving it as a preset, would silently delete the filter. Measured instead of assumed, and it turned out not to: making the container a real registry entry had already closed that. What WAS open: nothing could ask whether a FILTER suits a layer. The existing check is asked about an effect TYPE, and the container type has no restrictions of its own — so a filter full of text effects landed happily on a shape, where not one of them could run, as a row that looks like a look and does nothing. A filter now applies where its contents apply. And pasting fits the look to the layer instead of throwing it away: a child that does not suit is dropped from inside the filter, the filter is kept, and the toast says how many were left behind rather than handing you a look that quietly differs from the one you copied.
- v7.43 — the effects list stops pretending you can reorder things that cannot be reordered (queue 113, §6). Nine effects — blur, brightness, contrast, saturation, hue, greyscale, sepia, invert and glow — are applied together before everything else, so dragging them up or down has never changed the picture. That was survivable while it was one list; filters handed you a second list to drag things around in, which is exactly the thing the plan said must not ship. Those rows now say "always first". Measured rather than asserted: all nine render byte-identically either way round against an order-dependent partner, with a control proving the test can detect ordering at all — the first version of that control used a plain rectangle, which is too uniform to reveal ordering, and quietly proved nothing.
- v7.44 — 16 ready-made filters, in four sections (queue 113, step 6 — the "lots of people will not want to spend time making filters themselves" half). "+ Add Filter" now opens a picker: Cinematic, Retro & Analogue, Light & Glow, Stylised — or Empty filter to build your own. Teal & Orange, Bleach Bypass, Cross Process, Faded Film, VHS Tape, CRT Monitor, Super 8, Old Film, Dreamy Bloom, Golden Hour, Light Leak, Neon Night, Comic Ink, Poster Print, Thermal Camera, Night Vision. Every one is an ORDINARY filter, not a locked preset: open it, retune any effect inside, throw one out, add your own, or pull the Strength down to blend it back toward the original. Anything in a filter that cannot run on the layer you are adding it to is dropped from inside it and the rest is kept, with a note saying so, rather than the whole look being refused. The library is validated against the real effect list on load, so a typo in a definition costs one dropped control instead of a broken render — and the suite checks all 16 against it, which is what caught the ranges while they were being written.
- v7.45 — Colouring gets an "✦ Add a Filter" button at the top, which was the last piece of the original filters ask still outstanding besides the browser tab. Ezra's words: "have a button at the top of the colouring section as a shortcut to it." It opens the same picker as the one in Effects — one filter menu in the app rather than two that drift apart — and it belongs in Colouring, because a filter IS a colour decision most of the time, and that is the panel you are already in when you decide the clip needs a look rather than a slider.
- v7.46 — Filters is now a third subsection next to Effects and Audio (queue 113, step 5). Your words: "now I want a third subsection for filters. It'll work the same as the others." All 16 looks, under their section headings, each one saying in plain words what it does AND what it is built from — Teal & Orange reads "Contrast · Saturation · Teal & Orange · Vignette". That last line is deliberate: the whole point of a filter here is that it is not a black box, so the list tells you what is inside before you commit, and those are the same names you will see when you open it up. Tap one and it lands on the layer, open, with the panel back on your effect stack rather than leaving you staring at the list wondering whether the tap did anything. No picture previews on the tiles yet — the thumbnail system is built around one effect per tile, so filters need their own path through it; that is its own release and the words do the job in the meantime.
- v7.47 — the last two "I pressed play and nothing happened" bugs (queue 96). First: holding the Play button for more than half a second used to toggle Loop AND eat the press, so the transport did not start and a setting you never asked for flipped silently — on a phone that is most presses. Play just plays now; Loop stays where you asked for it, on the view rail. Second: the FIRST clip you add landed at 0 and set the project length, but the playhead kept whatever value it was left at — so a song added to an empty project could start from the middle for no reason, or from past the end, where pressing play genuinely does nothing. The playhead now follows the first clip the way it already followed every later one.
- v7.48 — the filters section, corrected to what he actually asked for, plus two small ones. The Effects card's three tabs are now **Visual · Filters · Audio** — the card is Effects, so calling the first tab Effects too was odd, and his line settled it: "I want all the tabs to be classified as effects, so you go into the effects tab, then you have, visual, filters, and then audio." There is no "+ Add Filter" button in the Visual tab any more — one door, and the door is the Filters tab, which now carries "Empty filter" at the top so building your own moved rather than disappeared. And the Colouring shortcut GOES to the Filters tab instead of opening a menu where it stood: he asked for "a shortcut button to go to filters", which is a place to look before you choose, not a button that adds one. Also: Delete is gone from the layer ⋯ menu — the bin is in the top bar two inches away, and a second door to the most destructive action in the app, at the end of a long scrolling list under the colour swatches, is exactly where a mis-tap lands.
- v7.49 — the Filters section is a proper browser now, with a picture of every look (queue 220 point 1). His words: "With the filters I wanted them to have a section like how effects and audio does, not how it currently is." So it is a grid of tiles — same four columns, same picture size, same rhythm as the Effects and Audio browsers — and each tile is that filter actually rendered on the sample photo, so you pick Bleach Bypass by seeing it rather than by reading about it. Sixteen tiles, sixteen different pictures, which the suite checks: sixteen IDENTICAL ones is what you get if a preview quietly falls back to the plain sample, and that looks like a working grid while telling you nothing. What each filter is MADE of has not gone away — it moved to the tile's tooltip, because a picture cannot tell you a filter is a group of ordinary effects you can open and retune, and that is the whole point of them. "Empty filter" keeps its full-width row: it is an action, not a look, and an empty picture in the middle of a grid of real ones would just be confusing.
- v7.50 — answered the half of "transform blur can't smear effect-driven motion" that could be answered without you (queue 31b). The app HAS a blur that smears effect motion — Motion Blur (Footage) — and it does it well: an Orbit goes from 41px wide to 80px with it on. The two blurs read different things and between them cover both cases: Object smears the layer's own position/scale/rotation keyframes and genuinely cannot see Orbit; Footage smears Orbit and does nothing for plain keyframed movement. Nothing needed building — what was missing was the app SAYING so, which is why "why doesn't my orbit smear" had no answer. Both now say which motion they read, and Object points at Footage for effect or camera motion. Also measured its speed limit, because it has one: Footage smears up to about 73px per frame and gives up somewhere before 103, since it works by comparing one frame to the next. That is the whole reason a fast Wiggle looked like it was being ignored — at 168px per frame it was simply outrunning it.
- v7.51 — an export in progress can no longer be thrown away by a stray gesture (queue 47, the cheap half). A render lives entirely in the page until the very last moment, so a refresh, a back swipe or a closed tab part-way through destroyed minutes of work with no warning whatsoever. The browser now asks before letting you leave, but ONLY while something is actually rendering — a browser that questions every reload would be a worse bug than the one being fixed, and the suite checks both directions. This is not crash-RESUME, which is a much bigger job for a separate release; it is the commonest way a render actually dies, which is not a crash but a hand.
- v7.52 — PC: the side rail is gone and everything lives on the row with the play button (queue 168). Left to right it now reads: back · undo · redo · skip-back · PLAY · skip-forward · duplicate · delete · bind · group · … · version-refresh · settings · export · view options. Delete, bind and group only appear when they apply, as asked — the row grows and shrinks with what you have selected instead of showing three dead buttons. The buttons were MOVED rather than copied, so there is still one of each with one click handler; a second copy is how two things end up disagreeing about whether a button is on screen. Play cannot drift as the row fills, which was the specific worry: the transport is a 1fr/auto/1fr grid, so the two sides stay equal by construction however many buttons land on either end.
- v7.53 — An export now survives an actual crash, not just a stray gesture (queue 47, the second half). v7.51 stopped a refresh or a back swipe from throwing a render away; it could do nothing about the way a long render on a phone really dies, which is the tab being OOM-killed or discarded. Now the encoded chunks are written to IndexedDB as they come out of the encoder, so if the export dies, starting it again picks up at the seam instead of at frame zero — a crash costs about two seconds of footage rather than the whole render. **This is not the "segmented export" redesign the entry had been told it needed.** That conclusion came from a true fact — you cannot reopen a half-written MP4 and keep muxing into it — plus a wrong inference, that the muxer therefore had to be resumable. It doesn't: muxing is a byte copy, not an encode, so the fix is to throw the half-written file away and re-mux the whole thing from the saved chunks in milliseconds. No demuxer, no new dependency, no segment joining, and the resumed file is assembled from exactly the chunks an uninterrupted run would have contained. Two things it deliberately refuses to do: it will not resume a project you have edited since (the signature covers the scene document, the output size, fps, bitrate, codec, range and whether there is an audio track — a mismatch discards the lot rather than splicing old frames into a new file), and it stops recording past 512 MB rather than filling your storage with the leftovers of a render nobody is coming back for. Seven tests in the suite cover the pieces; tests/_xresume.html does it for real — kills a genuine 150-frame export at frame 100, resumes it, and counts the samples in the finished MP4.
- v7.54 — Crash-resume actually survives a crash now. v7.53 shipped the feature and an adversarial review of it found the one thing every test had missed: `FM.projects.pruneOrphans()` sweeps the media store at every boot, deleting anything no layer, project or media-library entry points at — and the saved export chunks are pointed at by none of those. So they were deleted at the first boot after a crash, which is precisely the boot on which they exist to be used. Resume worked inside a page session (a thrown error mid-export, which is what the end-to-end probe exercises) and would never once have worked for a real OOM kill. The tests could not have caught it because none of them ran the boot path at all; a feature about surviving a reload has to be tested across the reload's clean-up, not just across the crash. Fixed in two halves, because the obvious half alone would trade one bug for another: `xr:` keys are now exempt from the generic sweep, and — since nothing generic is looking after them any more — `FM.exportResume.sweep()` reaps them on its own terms at boot instead: an abandoned render older than three days goes, a capped one goes, and parts left behind by a torn write go whatever the job's age. Two new tests, one of which calls the real `pruneOrphans()` rather than a stand-in; both mutation-checked, and reverting the exemption reproduces the original bug exactly.
- v7.55 — The last two things the review of crash-resume found, both of which only bite the people it was built for. **(1) It no longer loads the whole saved render into memory to resume it.** The first cut read every saved chunk into one array — up to 512 MB — and held it for the rest of the export: an out-of-memory risk inside the one feature whose entire job is surviving an out-of-memory kill, and the worse version of that is that each retry after a crash would be likelier to die than the attempt before it. It now replays one ~3 MB batch at a time and releases each before reading the next, so peak memory is flat however long the render. Feeding is all-or-nothing per batch, because half-feeding a batch is the subtle disaster: those frames would count towards the resume point, the next batch written would overwrite that slot with different chunks, and the resume after that would have a hole in the middle of it. A failed replay now also bins the saved render and fails that one export rather than carrying on into a muxer it has already half-filled — one failed export that then self-heals, instead of a resume that fails forever. **(2) The join no longer flashes on projects with time-based effects.** Motion Blur (Content), Frame Stutter, the temporal denoiser and the time warp each build frame N out of frame N-1 — the echo-trail style out of the whole preceding second. An export wipes that state before it starts (so a render never inherits what you had been previewing), which is right, but a resume then began in the middle with it cleared: a built-up echo trail would vanish for a single frame and ramp back in, mid-file, in a file you would have no way of knowing was different. Now a resume re-renders the 25 frames leading up to the join without encoding them, purely to rebuild that history — and only for a project that actually carries one of those four effects, since a pre-roll is real render time and there is no reason to charge it to everybody. Two more tests, four more mutations, all caught.
- v7.56 — Drag a clip to the edge of the screen and the timeline comes to meet you (queue 115), the same way a trim and the multi-select paint drag already did. Ezra asked for this a long time ago; it was built and backed out **three** times, and each attempt turned the same three unrelated tests red — two text-editor geometry checks and an edit-points snap. Each attempt guessed at the mechanism from those three names and each guess was wrong: the scroller width, the scroll position, a leaked drag, the scene. All four were measured this time and all four were innocent. **The actual cause: the auto-scroll loop called `FM.requestRender()` every frame.** That feeds `noteMotion()` — the adaptive-quality heuristic that decides the app is in motion and calls `resizeCanvas()` to drop the preview to a lower resolution, snapping back when you stop. Three tests measuring canvas geometry were measuring a canvas that had been resized underneath them. It was invisible module state, which is exactly why three rounds of looking at the timeline found nothing. The loop now updates the clip's position in the timeline without repainting the canvas; the picture catches up on the next finger movement and on release, and while you are edge-scrolling you are looking at the timeline anyway. Two smaller defects fixed on the way: the growth limit was derived from the clip's LIVE position, so the loop pushed the clip right, which pushed the limit right, which made room to scroll further — it now comes from where the clip started, so it terminates by construction; and the loop is cancelled on every gesture-end path rather than only on pointerup. Found with two new tools that stay in the repo: `tests/_fmdiff.py` captures and diffs the FULL pass/fail list between two suite runs to find the first test that changes, and `tests/_only.html?items=…` runs an arbitrary subset to ask "does this fail on its own, or only after something earlier ran?". Proven working, not just green: `tests/_q115b.html` drives a real pointer drag to the edge at both desktop and 380px phone width and checks the timeline scrolls, the clip travels with it, and releasing stops it.
- v7.57 — Three fixes for "major lag when scrolling, with barely any layers" (queue 125) — and the first one explains why three previous profiling passes all came back clean. **(1) The app could not see its own lag.** The adaptive quality ladder decides whether to soften the preview using one number: the time our JavaScript spends inside render(). Canvas `filter` effects are done by the GPU after we return, and video decode happens off-thread, so neither ever reaches that clock. Measured: six Gaussian blurs plus six glows on a 1080×1920 comp at 6× CPU throttle reported **1.1 ms a frame** — a tenth of budget — so the ladder never shed a single rung on exactly the scenes it exists for. It now also watches the interval between consecutive frames, which sees everything, and the same scrub drops two rungs. The threshold matters more than the idea: the first cut treated any overrun as evidence, and one dropped frame (28.7 ms) is ordinary jitter on any machine — measured, it walked a single small shape with no effects down two rungs, which would have been a worse bug than the lag. It now only counts a sustained average under about 24fps, and a trivial scene is verified to keep full resolution throughout. **(2) Half of a video scrub's cost was invisible to that same ladder.** Four `seeked` listeners repainted the preview by calling the render function directly rather than going through `FM.requestRender` — so they were neither coalesced (a scrub of a video layer paid for two full renders a frame) nor measured. `js/storage.js` already had its three copies wired the right way; these four were the odd ones out. **(3) Scrubbing re-seeked the video to a time it was already at.** A scrub snaps to the frame grid, so a slow finger produces many frames that all resolve to the same time, and every one re-issued an identical `currentTime` write — which restarts the element's seek and therefore cancels the decode of the very frame being asked for, while emitting no `seeked` and so not even repainting. The exporter has had that guard since #15; the preview never did. Also fixed on the way: one recorded hypothesis in #125 turned out to be wrong and is corrected there — the ladder DOES call resizeCanvas on every tier change, not only when motion starts.
- v7.58 — The same picture is no longer copied twice (queue 125, continued). Every preview render of a video layer also copies the whole frame at FULL SOURCE resolution into a spare canvas, so that if the next frame is still decoding the clip holds its last good picture instead of flashing black. That is worth having — but it was being paid on every single render, including the many that show an identical picture. **Measured rather than assumed** (`tests/_q125hold.html`): on a 2048×2048 clip at 6× CPU throttle one copy is **9.7 ms — 58% of an entire frame's budget at 60fps**, against 2.2 ms at half size and 0.6 ms at quarter. It now skips the copy when the video has not moved since the last one, keyed on the source time, which is what decides the picture. A finger held still on the timeline re-renders continuously at one source time — and since v7.57 stopped the pointless re-seeking, the element genuinely stays put there — so that case was re-copying four megapixels per frame to produce a byte-identical result. Nothing changes when the frame really is new. The test counts the actual copies rather than the bookkeeping around them: the first version watched the recorded timestamp instead and passed against a mutation that copied on every render, because the pre-fix code reuses the same canvas and records the same time either way. **Still not done, and worth naming:** during playback every frame IS new, so the full-resolution copy is still paid per frame there. Shrinking it is the real win (half size is 4.4× cheaper) but is not safe as a one-liner — the composite samples that canvas in SOURCE pixel coordinates for the crop rect, so a smaller canvas would crop the wrong region. The measured payoff and the exact obstacle are recorded in REQUESTS so the next pass is a build rather than a hunt.
- v7.59 — The held video frame is captured small, which finishes the expensive half of queue 125. v7.58 stopped it being copied twice for the same picture; during playback every frame really is new, so the copy was still paid in full every frame — measured at **9.2 ms on a 2048² clip at 6× CPU throttle, 55% of a whole frame's budget**. It is now captured at a quarter of the pixels, **2.3 ms — four times cheaper**. Two things are worth recording. **First, the obstacle written down last time did not exist.** The note said shrinking was unsafe because the composite samples that canvas in source pixel coordinates for the crop rect. It does — and it has always rescaled those coordinates by the source's real size, because the frame cache has produced downscaled bitmaps for years. Grade and key likewise draw the source into their own output box. The composite was already built for this; reading it beat assuming, again. **Second, the obvious implementation was a pessimisation, and only measuring caught it.** Capping the longest side at a flat 960 made the same copy cost **11.9 ms — slower than not shrinking at all** — because 2048→960 is a 2.133:1 resample and the browser's fast path is exact halving. So the rule halves while the result stays at or above 640, which only ever asks for ratios of 2, 4, 8. A test now asserts the ratio is a power of two, because a later tidy-up to a "cleaner" flat cap would otherwise be a silent 5× regression with nothing to catch it. The cost of the whole change is that a held frame is slightly softer for the fraction of a second it is on screen during a seek — when the preview is deliberately soft anyway. The crop correctness is tested directly: a clip cropped to the blue right-hand half of a red/blue source still paints blue while holding a downscaled frame.
- v7.60 — Opening a project answers the tap immediately (queue 128). Ezra: *"the animation of the project layer moving to the left happens instantly, so it feels responsive, then smoothly the project should swoop in too."* Until now it could not: `openProject` awaited the project load and only then started the push, so nothing on screen moved until the load finished — measured at 28 ms here and **113 ms at 6× CPU throttle on a four-layer project**, growing with the project. The animation itself was never the problem; it was measured smooth at 6× throttle (worst frame 18 ms, nothing over 50). So the push is now two phases: the card and the home screen leave on the tap, and the editor swoops in when the load resolves. It is a split rather than simply starting the push earlier, because #app still holds the PREVIOUS project — sliding that in for 113 ms and swapping it underneath you would be worse than the wait. Phase 1 parks the editor off the right edge instead, so the space the home screen vacates shows the theme background. **Three real defects surfaced while building it, all found by the existing test section and all fixed:** `startPush` never cleared a lingering pop, and `fm-pop-out` is an animation with fill-mode:both — a running animation beats a plain declaration, so the park was silently ignored and the editor sat part-way on screen; `onPushEnd` ends the push when the home screen's animation finishes, which on this path is long before the load, so the arriving editor owns the ending instead; and an open that never settles used to strand a pressed card, which had a backstop, but now strands the whole transition, which did not — so the waiting phase got its own deadline. The test section itself was re-specified rather than patched: its premise was "the push waits for the load", which is the thing being removed, so the assertions now ask whether the tapped card is *acknowledged* (pressed **or** leading) rather than which class it carries, and the warm hand-off is tested on the path where it still exists — opening the already-current project, where there is no load to split. Verified at 375px with the load frozen: home pushed left and dimmed, the card leading, the editor a full viewport off-screen, and the vacated strip showing the theme background rather than the last project.
- v7.61 — Leaving a project answers the tap too (queue 128, the closing half; v7.60 did the opening). His words were *"needs to be smoother and less janky when leaving a project also"*, and the measurement came out the same shape as the opening half: not a stutter, a stall. `home.open()` runs straight through to `startPop()`, its very last line, so everything above it happens with the finger already lifted and nothing on screen moving — **81 ms at 6× CPU throttle, and 62 ms of it was one call**: `touchCurrent(true)`, stopping to take a fresh photograph of the project for its card. That call does two jobs. The metadata half — name, size, duration, layer count — is what the grid needs in order to render at all, and is nearly free. The picture is the expensive half, and nothing needs it until you can see it, while the cards are still sliding in. So they are separated: the metadata is taken now, the photograph a moment later. **81 ms → 14 ms**, measured the same way. The capture is deferred past the animation AND into idle time rather than onto a plain timer, because a plain timer put a >50 ms frame right where you land — you arrive on the grid and it hitches under your thumb — and `requestIdleCallback` spends it in a gap instead, with a timeout so it can never be starved. The card keeps its previous thumbnail until then, which is a picture of the project you were looking at seconds ago. The test asserts the control first (the metadata must still be refreshed synchronously, or "it didn't capture" would be good news for the wrong reason), that the capture does not happen synchronously, and that it does still happen — a deferral that never fires is a deletion. Both mutations caught.
- v7.62 — A clip that never shows a picture now says why (queue 129). His words, on a two-second screen recording and for the second time: *"it still has the issue of being on the timeline but not actually showing any video."* There are two ways a clip can be present and blank, and only one of them was covered. If the browser cannot read a video track **at all**, `videoWidth` is 0, and the import already says "no picture — audio only". The other way had nothing watching it: the container parses fine, so the file reports honest dimensions and a real duration and lands on the timeline looking completely normal — and then the **decoder** will not take it, `readyState` never reaches HAVE_CURRENT_DATA, and the compositor (which skips any video below that) draws nothing. Forever, with no error, no toast and no console line. That is a screen recording exactly: iOS records H.265, and a browser without HEVC support parses the container happily and decodes none of it. So a clip that has produced no frame after fifteen seconds is now called out by name, with the actionable half — re-export as H.264, or open the project in Safari — in the console where there is room for it. The source is a `blob:` URL, so there is no network to be slow: nothing after fifteen seconds means the decoder is not going to produce anything, not that it is still trying. **The clip is not thrown away** — the audio may be perfectly good, and something you can still hear and trim is worth more than a refused import. Three things the test refuses to let this become: a clip that decodes normally must never be accused, a clip whose first frame merely arrives late must not be either (on a phone with a big file, that is every import), and a clip that was ready all along but whose event never reached us must not be — the deadline re-checks the element rather than treating silence as failure. That last one is not pedantry: without it the feature degrades into warning about everything, which is worse than the bug, and it is the only one of the three that a mutation could sneak past.
- v7.63 — The export dialog is tidied (queue 141 part 3). His words: *"I want the export screen to be prettied up… Maybe instead of the apple pop up we should have our own pop up so it looks finished and good. As pristine as possible."* "Prettied up" sounded like taste and mostly was not — the thing making it look unfinished was **alignment**. Each row was space-between and a `<select>` shrink-wraps to its longest option, so every control was a different width and started somewhere different: measured at 375px, the five dropdowns began at x=355, 262, 262, 471 and 345. Five ragged left edges down one short column reads as unfinished however nicely each control is styled — the same complaint he made about the frame-rate list being "all over the shop", one level up. They now share one column: label left, control right, every control the same size, both edges true. Getting there took two corrections that only showed up on screen — narrowing the controls first truncated "Same as project — 1080×1920" down to "Same as …", losing the only word that mattered, so the control column takes the room instead of splitting it evenly with a one-word label; and the native chevron then overlapped the text until the arrow got a reserved lane. The **checkbox** is the app's own control now rather than a stark white browser square beside five themed ones — drawn, with an accent tick, its own focus ring restored by hand (`appearance: none` throws the platform's away with its paint), and its long label given the full row width since it is a sentence, not a word. And the card is clamped to the viewport: 330px plus 24px of padding either side overflowed a 320px phone, which is the one width where a dialog must not scroll sideways. The alignment is asserted in the suite rather than admired — a column that drifts again goes red instead of quietly looking cheap. **Parts 3 and 4 of #141 are not both done**: this is the dialog itself. The "our own pop up instead of the apple one" half is still open, and the honest version of it is smaller than it sounds — see REQUESTS.
- v7.64 — An export now ends on **our** card, not the Apple sheet (queue 141 part 4). His words: *"Maybe instead of the apple pop up we should have our own pop up so it looks finished and good."* The honest scope first, because it is smaller than it sounds and I would rather say so than imply otherwise: **the OS share sheet cannot be replaced.** `navigator.share` only opens from a real user gesture and there is no web API that writes to your camera roll without it. But the sheet was never really the problem — the problem was that it arrived unannounced the instant the render stopped, with no sight of what you had just made. So the render now ends on an **Export ready** card: the first frame of your export, the file name, and the three things you would actually check before committing it anywhere — size, length, and shape (`17.6 MB · 1:14 · 1080×1920 · 30 fps`). The Apple sheet appears because you pressed **Save** on that card. **It fixes a real defect as well as a cosmetic one.** `deliver()` has always had to fall back to a plain download when a long render outlived the tap that started it, because sharing needs transient activation and a five-minute export has none left — so the very exports where landing the file in Photos matters most were the ones that quietly did not. Save is a fresh tap, so the sheet can always open. The first frame is used for the poster rather than the last, because the last frame of a video is very often black and a card whose picture is a black rectangle tells you nothing. The poster is contained in a fixed letterbox, never cropped, so a 9:16 export and a 16:9 one give the card the same shape instead of it jumping size with your project's aspect. The exporter **awaits** the card, which is the part worth testing rather than looking at: its `finally` frees the export frame caches and drops the crash-resume data, so a card that resolved early would pull the ground out from under a save that had not happened yet, invisibly. Discard releases it too, or an unwanted export would hang forever holding its caches. GIF and PNG-sequence exports are unchanged and still download straight away — `onReady` is opt-in, which is also what keeps every existing test on the old road.
- v7.65 — The desktop text editor stops taking a slice of the canvas (queue 147, the second half). His words: *"the text edit stuff on pc for some reason covers up the canvas, making it smaller when you could just put it in the add menu, so it doesnt take up real estate on the screen."* v6.96 moved the Aa options panel into the side column and that fixed the half he was looking at — the panel covering the text he was typing. What was left is the one he described in that second sentence: the editor CARD sat at the bottom of the stage and the layout padded the canvas so it would clear it. Measured at 1280×860: **169px of a 552px-tall stage**, nearly a quarter of the picture, spent on a panel that only exists while you type. The card now lives in the side column too, where he said to put it, and the canvas keeps every pixel. **The entry had deferred this on "the column is 286px and the card's minimum is 320px", and that was right without being fatal** — measured, the toolbar really does overflow a 270px column by 45px. But a column has the opposite budget to a bottom bar: width is scarce and height is not (286×552 here), so the toolbar wraps onto two rows and the problem stops being one. The Aa panel now opens *below* the card rather than at the top of the column, since the two share that space for the first time — otherwise the sheet would open straight over the toolbar that summoned it. It falls back to the old floating card whenever the column is too small to hold it, so a narrow window is never worse than it was, and that promise is asserted rather than assumed: the test drives a real 1280px layout, checks the stage reserves nothing and the toolbar is not cut off, then shrinks the window to 760px and checks it goes back. Phone is untouched and was never part of this — there is no side column there and the overlay is the right shape for a phone.
- v7.66 — The edges of an audio clip stop clicking (queue 148, part). Ezra: *"the audio i import is making a realy scratchy popping noise that hurts my ears when im trying to play back stuff."* The measuring pass (v7.65's notes, `tests/_pops.html`) disproved the leading theory — under real load the sync controller makes **zero** corrective seeks and zero rate trims, so it is not the app correcting itself. What that probe could not test is a clip BOUNDARY, because six seconds of continuous playback never crosses one: the element was paused with no ramp and resumed with a seek and a play, cutting the waveform dead at whatever sample it happened to be on. That is the textbook way to make a click, and a loop crosses one every lap. Every edge now has a 45 ms fade. **Anticipatory, not a fade-on-pause** — the envelope is computed from the transport clock and reaches zero *at* the boundary, because easing down only once the boundary has arrived would bleed the source audio past the end of the clip, which is precisely what #1/#8 fixed. **45 ms rather than the textbook 5 ms** for a measured reason: this is evaluated once per sync tick, and under the load in that same measurement the ticks were 100 ms apart, so a 5 ms ramp would be stepped straight over and do nothing at all on exactly the struggling machine that needs it. It multiplies the user's own fades rather than replacing them, so a clip already fading out over a second is unaffected. Pressing play mid-clip gets the same treatment — that is the same click in the place you notice it most, and the element now opens silent and lifts over 45 ms instead of starting at full volume on an arbitrary sample. **This is not claimed to be his bug** — I cannot hear a click from here, and #148 stays open on one question: does it pop at a clip edge or through the middle of one long clip. It is a defect on its own terms either way.
- v7.67 — Speech detection lets you choose what it scans (queue 150, part 2). His words: *"it should have a choice between only detecting where the captions are added in the project or detecting the whole project or detecting a specific audio later then let you select it."* Three choices now sit above the button: **Just this caption clip · The whole project · One audio clip…**, and the clip picker appears only for the third, because showing it beside the other two asks you to answer a question those modes do not have. **The third one is the fix, not a convenience, and #152's measurement is why**: the detector holds up against a music bed 18 dB below the voice and collapses once music comes within 12 dB of it — 2 of 3 utterances at −12 dB, none at −6. Pointing it at the voice track instead of the finished mix is the difference between three cues and zero. "The whole project" is the one with real arithmetic behind it: speech that starts *before* the caption clip used to be silently discarded, and now the clip moves back to meet it — which means every cue has to re-base by the same shift, or they all slide by however far it moved. That is pure maths, so it is a pure exported function with its own test, the same reasoning as the exporter's fit rect: you should not need to decode audio to check where a cue lands. The entry also asked for the source to *"default to the most voice-like"*, and the honest way to know that is the level distribution the detector returns — which means decoding, and decoding every clip up front is the expensive half of the whole operation. So the fallback is **lazy** instead: try the clip you chose, and only if it finds nothing walk the rest. It costs extra exactly when you would otherwise be staring at "no speech found" with no idea another clip was an option, and on "One audio clip…" your choice is respected and nothing else is touched. **Part 1 of #150 — making the button easier to reach — is NOT done**; it still lives in the Aa sheet.
- v7.68 — Speech detection has a door of its own (queue 150, part 1 — the half v7.67 did not do). His words: *"make the auto detect captions button way easier to access and use."* It was reachable only as text layer → text editor → Aa sheet → scroll, inside a 46vh scroller, which is a fair description of unfindable. **Captions** is now a tile on the property grid beside Colouring and Effects, one tap from a selected text layer and the first place anyone looking for captions would look. It opens onto detection at the top — that is the reason to use captions at all — and the cue grid under it. On a layer that is not a caption track yet, the card offers to make it one *and* still shows the detector, because detection converts the layer and fills the grid in one press: asking someone to press "use as caption track" first would be a step for nothing. The card does not own that UI, it hosts it — `js/captions.js` still builds both halves, so this card and the Aa sheet can never drift into two different captions editors. The tile is text-and-caption-only, and that is asserted rather than assumed: every other branch of the category list is built by BLACKLIST, so a new card leaks into all of them unless it is explicitly taken back out, which is exactly how the Camera Options card once turned up on shapes, text, media and groups. Both mutations caught — no card at all, and the card leaking onto every layer kind. #150 is now complete.
- v7.69 — Caption effects can belong to one cue instead of the whole track (queue 151). His words: *"when editing a caption layer you should be able to chose somehow between adding effects to each section or adding effects that effect the whole layer."* The Effects card now shows the track's stack, then a **This cue — "…"** divider naming the cue that is on screen, then that cue's own stack under it. An effect moves between the two with **⋯ → Apply to this cue only** / **Apply to the whole track**. **The costing said ~170 read sites and it was a red herring, which is why this shipped in one pass rather than being a week.** `layer.effects` is read everywhere, but none of it changes: the cue's stack is concatenated onto the track's exactly once, at the top of `drawLayer`, and everything downstream receives an ordinary layer with an ordinary flat array. That is the same shallow-clone trick this file already used twice to render a layer without one of its effects. The `_cueFx` marker is not decoration: `drawLayer` is re-entered constantly — masks, motion blur, blend modes and effect isolation all rebuild the layer and call back in — so without it a cue with one blur would render with four. **You still ADD to the track and then move down, deliberately.** Teaching the effect browser and three other add paths which stack they are aiming at is the version of this that breaks quietly: an add path that guesses wrong does not throw, it puts your effect somewhere you did not look. Moving is one explicit action on a row that already exists. Two things carried over from the costing and both real: the row's ⋯ menu now acts on **its own** stack rather than always on `layer.effects` — Duplicate and Delete at an index belonging to a different array is the exact defect `fxRow`'s `listOf()` was written to fix, one level up — and the import sanitiser now walks cue stacks too, since they arrive from the same hand-edited files and this file's own note calls `layer.effects` the weakest-validated structure on the way in. A cue's array is minted only when something is actually put in it, so scrolling past a cue does not stamp `effects: []` into your project file. Three mutations caught, including the one that leaks a cue's effects onto every other cue.
- v7.70 — The open add-menu tab wears the open-project glint (queue 155) — **and it already did.** Found unticked while working the list oldest-first: the travelling light was wired onto the active Elements / Shape / Media / Audio / Template tab some builds ago, sharing `.hm-glint`'s keyframes with the home card exactly as the entry demanded. Verified rather than assumed before ticking it — the glint is on the active tab and only that one, it follows the selection when you change tab, and it is driven by the same `hm-glint` animation the open project's card uses. That is the third stale entry this list has turned up (after #37 and #147's first half), which is an argument for the list rather than against it: they were all found by working it in order rather than by remembering. What v7.70 actually adds is a **test**, and specifically for the warning attached to the entry — *"Build it from the SAME implementation rather than a second copy, or the two will drift the way the slider glide drifted from the timeline's in #116."* Drift is invisible on the day it happens: someone forks the CSS to tweak one ring, both still look fine in isolation, and only much later does anyone notice the two lights that are supposed to mean the same thing no longer move alike. The test asserts they run the same keyframes, so that day goes red. It also asserts the ring MOVES with the selection, because the tab row is not rebuilt on a click — the active class is swapped in place, so a ring appended once at build time stays on whichever tab opened first and looks perfectly correct until you change tab. Both mutations caught.
- v7.71 — **Try this: the film grain has moved off the project cards and onto the home background** (queue 157). His words: *"I want to try removing the film grain from the projects and instead move it to the background, it might be better if the projects are smooth and shiny with a rough textured background instead of"* — the message ends there, but the instruction did not need the rest of it. The cards keep their glass edge, their light and the OPEN glint; what they lose is the texture over the face, which is what "smooth" means here. The whole screen behind them is now the rough surface. **It is a move, not a rewrite.** Four rounds of tuning went into that grain (queue 76 → 94 → 105 → 133) and every word of it still applies — most of all the linear timing curve, which was the fourth attempt at *"I want a constant flow, not a noticeable start and stop"* and which a forked copy would have quietly lost. The same two cross-fading layers and the same keyframes now drive one surface instead of N, so the per-card phase offset and per-card tile pair are gone with the problem they solved: there is nothing left to de-synchronise when there is one field. It needed a real element rather than a pseudo-one, because `#home-screen`'s `::before` and `::after` are both already the drifting light. **Strength is one number.** The grain sits at `--hm-grain-alpha: .05` on `#hm-grain`, up from the .032 it used on a card, because a card is a small bright surface and the background is the whole screen. Both keyframes read that variable, so making it rougher or subtler is one value, not four. **This is an experiment and it wants a verdict**, which is the honest state of it — say rougher, subtler, or put it back, and any of the three is a small change. The test guards the thing an experiment like this fails at halfway: grain on both surfaces is busier than before it started, and grain on neither is a feature quietly deleted. Both mutations caught.
- v7.72 — New Freehand and Vector Drawing icons, judged to the bar he set (queue 163). His words: *"Make the logos for the pencil drawing and freehand drawing better, get multiple agents with really high standards to not accept it until it's perfect."* Three judges — an icon designer, a first-time user and a pixel engineer — scored them at 24px, which is the only size that decides anything here because it is the add-menu cell. **Round 1: rejected 0/3**, and two of the three said the pair could not even be told apart at a glance. The faults converged: the pencil was a **hollow outline** whose counter turned to mush at 24px ("reads as a paperclip"), both details that made it a pencil died at that size, and — the one that mattered — **a bare pencil is the universal EDIT glyph**, so it said "rename this", not "draw by hand". The vector's lower anchor was broken (the curve entered at its *corner* and ate the counter, rendering as a "G"), its two anchors did not match each other, and in a **video editor** a curve between two square nodes is the standard easing/keyframe-graph mark — the newcomer's first guess was "speed curve". **Round 2: 1/3**, with the pair chosen 2–1 and the new vector unanimous. **Round 3: 3/3, no blocking faults.** What shipped: the pencil keeps its old geometry but **filled**, so there is no counter left to lose, with a deep S-stroke trailing from under the nib — that stroke is the whole difference between an edit pencil and a drawing tool. The vector is a pen tool: 5.2px anchors that survive, the curve **butt-capped** so it stops flat at each edge instead of fusing into it, and a control handle with a solid knob, which is the part that says pen rather than graph. Two dead ends worth recording: my first "solid" pencil redrew the body from scratch and lost the pencil entirely (at 96px it read as a marker), and my first trailing stroke flattened to a dash at 24px because its amplitude was too small for its weight. The judges caught both. `tests/_iconsheet.html` renders every candidate at 24/48/96 on the real cell colour and is committed, because the next icon argument should not start by rebuilding the rig.
- v7.73 — **PC: the notes button finally joined the rest of the buttons on the transport row** (queue 229). His words: *"you still havent moved all the buttons on the pc version to where they should be, like the notepad button."* He is right, and the interesting part is that the code had been *claiming* otherwise since v7.12: the comment sitting beside the button in `index.html` says it lives "LEFT OF THE COG since v7.12 (queue 171)… so the run reads refresh · notes · cog · export". That was true when the cog was in the top bar with it. **v7.52 then moved everything else down** — back to `#t-home`, delete/bind/group to `#t-sel`, the version chip, cog, Export and view options to `#t-far` — and the notes button was simply not in the list. So it stayed behind, alone, in a 50px header whose only other contents are the wordmark and a project-name field that the inspector's own header already duplicates. One name missing from one array, and a stale comment describing an arrangement that had stopped existing. Now `#t-far` reads **v7.73 · notes · cog · Export · ⛶**, exactly what queue 171 wrote down. Measured at 1440×900 with a layer selected: notes is at x 1206 in the row, immediately left of the cog at 1257, and nothing else moved — Export and the view button are at the same pixels they were. The test asserts the button is a descendant of `#transport` and sits left of the cog, so a future migration that forgets it again goes red instead of shipping.
- v7.74 — **PC multi-select: the six small buttons are three big ones, and the playhead trio now means the whole selection** (queue 169, collected by 229). His words: *"in the left massive area where its currently got the six small buttons, just get rid of the buttons that are near the play head and then with the align buttons just make them big and fill up the whole section."* The three he wanted gone are the trim/split trio — the same three that float over the playhead as `#tl-trim`, which is where he asked for them back in v5.25. **But deleting the panel's copy was not safe until today**, and that is the real content of this release: the floating trio and the nudge pair read `FM.selectedLayer` — the PRIMARY layer — so with three clips selected they appeared over the playhead and then edited *one* of the three, while the inspector held a second, selection-aware copy of the same actions.
