Remove the copy without fixing the floats and "split all" silently becomes "split one" — the kind of loss you notice several edits later. They read the selection now: trim, split and extend apply to every selected clip the playhead is inside, and MOVE takes the selection as a block, the near edge landing on the playhead with each clip keeping its offset, which is the same rule the inspector row used (copied from it rather than re-derived, so the two cannot disagree). The titles carry the count — "Split all 3 at playhead" — because the buttons look identical either way and that is not a press to make by accident. With the duplicate gone the panel held nothing else: measured at 1440×900 with three clips selected, **168px of content in a 580px panel**, the other 412 empty — his "left massive area". The three align buttons now fill it as stacked full-width rows with names on them (Start together · One after another · End together), rather than three 88px icon columns grown tall, because an unlabelled glyph at that size just reads as an unfinished button and start-vs-end is exactly the pair a mirrored icon fails to distinguish. Phone untouched — the clip actions stay in the panel there and the align row stays a compact icon strip, one home per platform, the same arrangement the single-clip row has had since v5.25. The test presses the real button with three clips selected and counts what changed, with a control assertion first so a trim that does nothing cannot pass.
- v7.75 — **PC: the last control comes down off the header, so everything is on one row** (queue 229). His words: *"no dont reclaim the top bar, i just want you to move the nescesary buttons to the bottoms one so its all there."* After v7.73 took the notes button down, the only thing left in the 50px header with a job was the name field — and one working control stranded a band away from every other control is the complaint itself. It now sits beside Back in the transport row, so the row reads **‹ · Shape · | undo redo ⏮ ▶ ⏭ ⧉ 🗑 | · v7.75 · notes · cog · Export · ⛶** and the header is a wordmark and nothing else. **What the field is for is worth stating, because it is not what it looks like.** It is dual-purpose: with a layer selected it renames THAT LAYER — the only rename control in the PC chrome — and with nothing selected it shows the project name, which the inspector's own header already shows. So it now appears only in the first case. That hide (`.proj-name.is-dupe`) already existed but was scoped to the Studio layout on the stated belief that *"the classic layout has no #proj-name-s"*; classic does have one (`index.html:332`), so the scope was simply wrong and the field had been a visible duplicate in classic all along. **What I started and did not ship:** removing the header outright, which measures **21% more canvas area at 1280×800** — the same gain Studio took at v7.52 — because the canvas is height-limited and the header is the one band that is neither drag-resizable nor a control. He said no, so it is reverted and the measurement is recorded in REQUESTS #229 rather than argued about; it is his layout. The test asserts the field is a descendant of `#transport` by parentage, not by geometry, because a position check would read the legitimate nothing-selected hide as the field having gone missing.
- v7.76 — **The home grain: a bigger tile, a quieter field, and cards that stop showing it through** (queue 157 + 227, one job). His verdict on the v7.71 experiment was *"The background film grain looks shit"*, and alongside it *"the project layers are clear so you can see the film grain behind, i want them clear still but not showing the film grain, so they look smooth and nice."* **The fault was the TILE, not the strength.** The field is one random-noise PNG repeated, and it has been 64px since v6.23 — which repeats about **76 times across a 380×820 phone**. The eye is very good at spotting a repeated random field, so what you see is a grid, not grain. On a card it repeated roughly five times, which is exactly why four rounds of tuning (queue 76 → 94 → 105 → 133) never turned this up: it only became ugly the moment the same field was asked to cover a whole screen. At **256px it repeats 4.8 times**. Measured, because the arithmetic is not obvious: six 64px tiles cost 54 KB and about **166 ms** to generate; two 256px tiles cost 272 KB and about **15 ms** — the bigger field is ten times cheaper to build as well as far better looking, because most of the old cost was six separate `toDataURL` calls, and two is all the cross-fade needs now the cards use none. Strength came down too, **.05 → .034**: it went up when the grain moved off the cards on the argument that a screen can carry more than a small bright panel, and with the grid gone that no longer holds. **And the cards.** He is describing a real hole in the recipe rather than a preference: every other glass surface in `theme-glass.css` — panels, the add sheet, the export card, the home top bar — pours its translucent tint over a **backdrop blur**, and the cards were the "gentler pour" that got the tint with no blur at all. At 8.5%→3% alpha they were showing whatever sat behind them essentially raw, and since v7.71 that is the grain. Blur is the right instrument rather than a lucky one: grain is high-frequency noise by definition, so a blur destroys it while the low-frequency background — the base colour and the drifting light — comes straight through. The card stays genuinely see-through, which is the half of his sentence a solid backing would have thrown away, and what shows through it is smooth. 14px rather than the panels' 20, because too much blur turns the light behind a small surface into a blob. Cost measured before shipping: with **eight cards** on screen, frame times are **identical with the blur on and off** (16.7ms median, 18.6 p95) — on this Mac, which is the usual caveat, though the same theme already runs a full-width blur over this same background in `.hm-top`. The test guards both halves and the tile size is exposed as a constant for it, because this is a regression that is invisible in review: a 64px tile looks perfectly correct in a screenshot of one card.
- v7.77 — **Freehand: the undo/redo icons he asked for, and the Undo behind them was deleting your whole drawing** (queue 165, point 4). His words: *"instead of an undo button just add the undo and redo icons that we have in the normal menu so you can go back or forwards."* The bar carried a text "Undo" and no redo at all; it now carries the two glyphs from the transport row, same paths, so one mark means one thing wherever you meet it. **Building the forward direction turned up a real bug in the backward one.** Queue 167 made a freehand session build ONE layer out of many strokes — *"it should all be inside the one drawing you just made"* — and Undo was never updated for it: it popped an id off the session's `strokes` list and spliced that LAYER out of the scene. Only the FIRST stroke ever pushes an id, so **one press of Undo deleted the entire drawing, every stroke of it**, and left the session pointing at a layer that no longer existed so the next stroke re-fitted a ghost. Three strokes in, one Undo, and all three were gone. The unit of work is the stroke now, in both directions: Undo moves the last subpath onto a redo stack and re-fits the layer around what is left, Redo moves it back, the layer is removed only when the last stroke leaves it and rebuilt when the first one returns, and drawing something new clears the redo stack because that is the end of the branch you undid away from. A half-drawn stroke still under your finger is thrown away first and does not go on the stack — it is the most recent thing you did, and it is not something you would want back. Redo is freehand-only: in vector mode a step is a point on a shape you have not committed yet, and a forward arrow that never does anything is worse than no arrow. Both dim rather than disable when there is nothing that way, so the pair never changes width and nothing moves under your thumb as you step back and forth. **One more thing fell out of the same queue-167 change:** the bar's counter read off `strokes`, which now holds exactly one id however much you draw, so it had been frozen at "1 stroke" — it counts subpaths now. The test counts SUBPATHS rather than layers, because "one layer either way" is exactly what made the bug invisible: the timeline looked identical whether you had lost one stroke or all of them.
- v7.77 — **Freehand: the undo/redo icons he asked for, and the Undo behind them was deleting your whole drawing** (queue 165, point 4). His words: *"instead of an undo button just add the undo and redo icons that we have in the normal menu so you can go back or forwards."* The bar carried a text "Undo" and no redo; it now carries the two glyphs from the transport row, same paths, so one mark means one thing wherever you meet it. **Building the forward direction turned up a real bug in the backward one.** Queue 167 made a freehand session build ONE layer out of many strokes — *"it should all be inside the one drawing you just made"* — and Undo was never updated for it: it popped an id off the session's list and spliced that LAYER out of the scene. Only the FIRST stroke ever pushes an id, so **three strokes in, one press of Undo took all three**, and left the session pointing at a layer that no longer existed so the next stroke re-fitted a ghost. The unit of work is the stroke now, both ways: Undo moves the last subpath onto a redo stack and re-fits the layer around what is left, Redo moves it back, the layer goes only when the last stroke leaves it and is rebuilt when the first returns, and drawing something new clears the stack because that is the end of the branch you undid away from. A half-drawn stroke under your finger is thrown away first and does not go on the stack. Redo is freehand-only — in vector mode a step is a point on a shape you have not committed yet, and a forward arrow that never does anything is worse than no arrow. Both dim rather than disable, so the pair never changes width and nothing moves under your thumb. **Two more things fell out of the same queue-167 change:** the bar's stroke counter read off that same one-id list and had been frozen at "1 stroke" however much you drew (it counts subpaths now), and the bar **overflowed a phone** — 378px of content in a 355px box at 380px, Cancel's right edge 22px past the bar and off the screen. It was ~12px over before the icons went in, so they did not cause it, they reached Cancel with it. The brush slider gives the space up: `flex: 0 1 92px` with a real minimum, full width wherever there is room and smaller only where there is not. **The first fix for that was a `@media (max-width: 700px)` rule and the test written to guard it passed against the bug** — a media query does not fire in a desktop-width runner — which is why the flexible version replaced it and why the test pins the bar to the phone's real 355px box instead of trusting the viewport. Three mutations red: whole-layer undo, a no-op redo, a slider that refuses to shrink.
- v7.78 — **Freehand: an eraser, and the undo history rebuilt to hold it** (queue 165, point 2). His words: *"you should add an option to switch from drawing to erasing."* A draw/erase toggle sits on the bar; in erase mode the same press-and-drag gesture takes out whole strokes instead of laying one down. **Whole strokes, and that was a decision rather than the lazy option** — rubbing out the middle of a stroke means splitting a path in two and re-fitting both halves, while removing the stroke you touch is what most simple drawing tools do and is what "switch from drawing to erasing" most naturally means on a tool whose unit of work is already the stroke. It is written down in REQUESTS #165 so he can say otherwise. The hit test is distance to the nearest SEGMENT in project units, topmost stroke first, with a reach of half the brush plus a finger's worth of slack converted from screen pixels so it feels the same at any zoom — a bounding box would grab every stroke that merely passes nearby, and measuring to the sample POINTS would miss a long straight run between two far-apart samples. **The history had to change to hold it.** v7.77's undo pushed and popped the TAIL of the stroke list, which is fine while the only edit is "add one at the end" and stops being fine the moment the eraser can take one out of the MIDDLE — putting it back on the end would silently change the order the strokes paint in. It is snapshots of the whole list now: a few small arrays in a session that holds tens of strokes, and every edit becomes undoable by the same code instead of each one needing its own inverse. **And the bar had to fit again** — a seventh control put it 28px over on a phone, so the slack is spread rather than taken from one place: the gap and the padding give up 2px each, the colour swatch 4, and the brush slider now shrinks to 40px before anything clips. Measured after: 347px of content in a 355px box. The test erases from the middle of five strokes, checks a miss changes nothing first so "it erased something" cannot pass for a tap anywhere on the canvas, and then undoes it back.
- v7.79 — **PC transport row: bare icons, with the box only on hover** (queue 230, and the red delete from 232). He described the row at length and stated the through-line himself: *"with the play button and all that, when you hover over it, it does show the box outline. But if you're not hovering over it, you don't show it. So I think it should be like that as well for the others… But that box around it should just be the same color as, like, when you hover over the play button and nothing else."* So the play button's treatment — transparent, no border, `rgba(255,255,255,.07)` on hover — is promoted to the rule for the whole row, and every control that carried a resting box gives it up: **back** (*"shouldn't have that box around it"*), **notes** (*"that also shouldn't have the box around it"*), the **cog** (*"I don't know why you've turned it into that sort of button thing… I wish it was still just the simple settings cog design"*), delete, parent and group. **Export loses the pill and keeps its job by colour instead of by weight:** *"instead of being this big massive button… just the icon with no button around it. And I want the coloring of it, though… the blue colors and stuff so it stands out and looks noticeably different to the other stuff."* It is now the one coloured mark in a row of white ones — accent blue with a soft glow, no fill, no word. **Delete is red by default** (queue 232's first point, shipped here because it is the same rule): *"it should just be a red icon. So it's, like, obvious."* And the **version chip gets its refresh spin** — *"put the little refresh spin icon next to the version refresh button"* — which the phone chip has carried since the tap-to-force-update label existed while the PC one never did, so the control that nukes the service worker looked like a plain label. All of it is scoped to `#transport` at desktop width, so `.btn` and `.icon-btn` keep their normal look everywhere else in the app; this is about this row, not a global restyle. One thing worth recording: Export's border came back accent-coloured under measurement despite a more specific rule setting it transparent, so it is written out explicitly — checked by reading the computed style back rather than by reasoning about specificity.
- v7.80 — **PC: one name field, in the inspector header, and it does both jobs** (queue 231). His words: *"on the left side, for some reason, that layers text edit box pops up, and it's really messy. Instead, that layers text edit button that pops up should instead be replacing the projects text edit button. So then, like, when you click on a layer, it goes from showing the name of the project to showing the name of the layer, and you can then edit the layers name. I think that'd be a lot cleaner and make a lot more sense."* He is right, and the thing he is describing is a compromise finally coming apart. The field has always been dual-purpose — it renames the selected LAYER when there is one and shows the project name when there is not — and the desktop had TWO of them, so queue 146 hid the top one in the case where it duplicated the inspector header and kept it in the case where it was the only layer renamer. That is why it appears and disappears; the pop-up was the compromise, not a bug on its own. Now there is one field, the inspector header's, and it does both jobs: **"Inspector · Untitled"** with nothing selected, **"Layer · Yellow box"** with a layer selected, editable either way. The header's own label says which, so the field cannot silently change meaning on you — which was the stated objection to doing it this way back in v6.13, and on reflection the panel underneath is the argument FOR it: everything below that header is already about the selected layer, so the layer's name belongs at the top of it. `#proj-name` stays in the DOM with its handlers wired, and a project rename is still pushed through it, so the phone's copy can never disagree; it is simply never shown on desktop. The v7.75 arrangement that put it beside the Back button is reverted — that was the "messy" thing. The queue-146 test is rewritten rather than deleted, with his quote in it so nobody reinstates the old shape by "fixing" the test, and it now writes THROUGH the field: showing the right value and writing to the right place are two different bugs, and the mutation that makes it always rename the project is caught by the second check, not the first.
- v7.81 — **PC: parenting sits next to the layer menu, delete moves one over, and the parenting icon stops being its neighbour's twin** (queue 232). His words: *"instead of it being the first one to the right of the select layers button… it should be like one over. So the one that's next to the select layers button should be the parenting button."* Done — the cluster reads **⧉ layer menu · parent · delete**, with Group last since it only appears at 2+ selected and putting it third keeps delete exactly "one over" in both the one-layer and the many-layer case. **And the icon.** *"the parenting button looks very similar to the select layers button. So I think you should change its design so it doesn't look the exact same."* He is right and it was almost literally the same mark: `#btn-layermenu` is a rounded square with a second one behind it, and the parenting button was a rounded square with a second one behind it — the only difference was a dashed stroke on one of them, which is invisible at 19px. It is the standard **child-of tree** now: a parent node, an elbow down and across, a child node. It shares no shape with its neighbour and it says what the button actually does, which "two squares" never did. (The third part of this entry — no box except on hover, and the red delete — shipped in v7.79 with queue 230, since it was the same rule.) The test compares the icon by SHAPE COUNTS rather than by eye: two rects and no paths is precisely the mark that was wrong, so reinstating it goes red. Both mutations caught — the old order, and the old icon.
- v7.82 — **PC: a selected layer finally gets its three-dot menu** (queue 233). His words: *"when you have a layer selected, it should show the three dot menu. That three dot menu doesn't show up at the moment, so there's no way to go into that and do all those settings, which is annoying."* He is right, and this was a missing **door**, not a style note — which is worth spelling out because there IS a menu button next to it and it is easy to assume that is the one. `#btn-layermenu` (⧉) is the **clipboard** menu: Select All, Group, Duplicate, Copy, Save Preset, Paste, Paste Style. The **full clip menu** — Lock, Onion skin, Reset transform, Flip Horizontally/Vertically, Fit / Fill / Stretch to Composition Area, Create Clipping Mask, Convert to Outline, Media Info, the colour tag strip, sixteen items in all — was reachable on desktop **only by right-clicking a clip**, a gesture nothing on screen advertises. The phone has had a ⋯ for exactly this since the mobile bar was built. So PC now has one too, in the selection cluster, showing whenever a layer is selected. It opens `FM.layerMenuItems` — the same set the phone's ⋯ opens and the same set the right-click opens — so it is a second **door** to one menu rather than a second menu to keep in sync, which is the mistake the old ⋯ project menu made and was removed for in v6.13. The test's second assertion is the one that matters: a button that opened the wrong menu would satisfy "there is a three-dot button" while leaving every one of those settings exactly as unreachable, so it checks for Lock / Onion skin / Reset transform and explicitly fails if it finds "Paste Style" instead. The mutation that points the button at the clipboard menu is caught by that check, and its error prints the wrong menu's contents so the next person can see the two sets are genuinely different.
- v7.83 — **The split button sits ON the playhead now, with a trim either side, up inside the ruler band** (queue 234). His words: *"those buttons aren't how I told you to change them. You probably forgot because I told you to do it, and you just logged it in your memory and didn't actually write it down."* **He is right, and the cause is worth recording because it is not simple forgetfulness — the instruction flipped and nobody reconciled the two.** v5.25 built this from *"make sure the delete fully left and delete fully right are both on the left side and then the split is on the right side"*, which is what he asked for then. Queue **169** later recorded the opposite — *"put the delete left side button to the left of the play head and then same for the right button, and put the split button in the middle on top of the playhead"* — and that entry sat open for weeks describing a layout that did not exist, while the code kept the older one. Two contradictory records, both written down, neither compared. It reads **trim-left · SPLIT · trim-right** now, and the split is centred on the line **by construction** rather than by a tuned offset: the container was already anchored to the playhead and centred with `translateX(-50%)`, so three equal buttons with equal gaps put the middle one on the line. Measured: split centre 700 against a centreline centre of 701. **And they moved up, which was the other half of it** — *"they might need to be slightly moved up a bit so they're not going onto the top layer, just so they're on that top little row, right underneath the counter."* Measured before: the group ran 686–710 against a ruler band ending at 705, so it really was hanging 5px into the first track row. It runs 684–704 now, inside the band. The split also takes the accent colour, because three near-identical outline buttons in a row need the middle one to say why it is the middle one. Two mutations red: the old order, and the old `top` — and the second one fails at a 1px overhang, which is the sensitivity this needs, since the whole complaint was five pixels.
- v7.84 — **The move/extend pair is finally two different buttons, level either side of the playhead — and Export stops lapping over the divider** (queue 235 and 240). *"those two buttons are very similar… right now, honestly, at first glance, I cannot tell a fucking difference. And they need to be moved up slightly as well. And also aligned a bit better, because one is closer to the playhead than the other."* All three complaints measured true. **The icons** were the same drawing except that MOVE's box was closed and EXTEND's was open at one end — about four pixels of difference at 15px, on two buttons that are never on screen together for you to compare. The difference is now carried by the strongest cue available at that size, fill versus outline: MOVE is a **solid block with a double chevron** (the whole clip picks up and travels to the line), EXTEND is an **outlined block with a dashed span and one arrow** (the near edge is pulled out, and the dashes are the new material). **The alignment** was 14px of air on the left of the line against 12px on the right, and the cause is worth knowing rather than nudging: `#tl-centerline` is 2px wide drawn from its left edge, so its optical centre is x+1, while `translateX(-50%)` centres the button row on x. Both flanking groups take a +1px correction, which also puts the v7.83 split button dead on the line (measured 0px off, was 1). **And they moved up** — measured at 686–710 against a ruler band ending at 705, so the pair really did hang 5px into the first track row; 684–704 now. **Export (queue 240):** *"The export buttons border is too big so it goes over the divider line."* A regression from v7.79, and the reason it shipped is the lesson: I measured v7.79 in **Classic** and it was fine — he uses **Studio**, where a rail-era rule (`body.layout-studio #btn-export`, from when Export was a big square in the vertical side rail) still sized it **44×44 inside a 40px row**, standing 3px proud at the top and 2px below. v7.79's own sizing was one id and one class, which loses to that rule; two ids wins. The new test does not assert "export is 34px" — it asserts **nothing in the row is taller than the row, in BOTH layouts**, because checking one layout and shipping for the other was the actual failure. Three mutations red: the oversized export, an outlined move icon, and the old order.
- v7.85 — **The PC add menu gets a surface of its own** (queue 236). His words: *"on the PC version, for the background of the add menu, you should make it have, like, a cool pattern and design, kind of like the home screen page, but slightly different just so it looks good for that specific area."* Same family as home, deliberately not the same picture: home lights from the **top centre** with a secondary pool bottom-right, so this lights from the **bottom left** with its pool top-right and a diagonal sheen across it. Side by side they are obviously the same material and neither is a copy, which is what "slightly different" has to mean for it to be worth doing. **Two things came out of it that are worth more than the gradient.** First, the version I nearly shipped had 9px of padding and a border — and that **grew the panel past its container and gave the inspector an 8px scrollbar it did not have before**. A decoration is not allowed to move the layout. Padding and border removed; a background costs nothing. When I put that padding back as a mutation it turned TWO tests red, including a pre-existing one ("the fitted Add panel scrolls by 7px… the grid is being planned against a box it does not fit in"), which is as clear a confirmation as you get that it was a real regression and not a cosmetic quibble. Second, **the grain is not here, and that is deliberate.** The plan was to reuse the home screen's noise field (`--hm-static-0`) rather than generate a second 136 KB tile — but that variable is only set by `home.js`'s `render()`, so on this screen it is very often unset and the field simply would not appear. A decoration that is present or absent depending on which way you arrived is worse than one that does not exist, so the gradients — which always work — are the whole of it. Both mutations red: a flat panel, and the padding.
- v7.86 — **On a trackpad the slam fires while you are still pulling, instead of after you stop** (queue 238). His words: *"when you do it and you swipe down too far on PC, it takes a bit too long before it snaps back up. It would be nice if when you kept swiping up, it was a bit of a smooth animation and didn't just freeze for a second before going back up."* The freeze was a **130ms debounce**, and the reason it existed is that a trackpad has no `pointerup` — the wheel path had to wait for a GAP in the events to decide the gesture was over. So at full stretch the list sat there, fully pulled and visibly stuck, until you took your fingers off. The touch path never had this, because a finger release is a real event. Crossing the threshold IS the commitment, so the slam goes at that instant; measured, it now fires **105ms into a flick that runs for 366**. **The interesting half is the cooldown, and the suite is what got it right.** One flick delivers dozens of wheel events, so firing on threshold without a guard re-slams two or three times per gesture — a worse bug than the one being fixed. My first guard was a fixed 520ms timer, it looked perfect in the browser, and **the test caught it firing twice**: a flick that outlasts the timer re-crosses the threshold and slams again, and how long a flick lasts depends on the machine — mine ran 366ms, the runner's ran longer. It is spent-until-the-gesture-ends now, re-arming on the same 130ms silence the release path already uses, so one gesture is exactly one slam at any speed on any machine. The test also had to be corrected: `slam()` removes and re-adds its class to restart the animation, so counting "the class is present" reads one slam as two — it counts transitions INTO the class instead. Mutation red: putting the wait back means a full-length pull never slams at all.
- v7.87 — **The black bar WAS the slam's own ring** (queue 239, and 237 with it). His words: *"the black bar glitch where the black bar comes up onto the side of the screen still happens, and it seems to happen when you, like, do the easter egg thing where you're slamming the screen"* — and that second clause is what cracked it, after three earlier passes chased this as a paint problem behind the page and fixed three real but different things without closing it. **It was not something showing through. It was the fix for a different bug, gone stale.** Queue 144 gave `#home-screen.hm-slam` a `box-shadow: 0 0 0 140px var(--bg)` so the shake could not reveal the editor behind home, and at the time home was a flat surface, so a flat ring matched it exactly and the fix was correct. Home is not flat any more: it carries the drifting light on its own pseudo-elements and, since v7.76, a grain field. **The ring stayed flat.** So every slam painted a band of dead `#060c0f` hard up against a lit, textured surface — a black bar at the edge of the screen, during the slam, exactly as reported. A box-shadow cannot carry a gradient, so the answer is to stop uncovering anything at all: the shake now runs with a **6% scale** that tapers back to 1, so home's own surface — light, grain and all — is what fills the edges, and nothing behind it can appear. That is the same guarantee the ring was there to give, without a second surface that has to be kept in step with the first. **The overscan is arithmetic, not a look**, and my first pass got it wrong: a scale of S pushes each edge out by H·(S−1)/2, so 1.03 on an 880px window is 13.2px against a 13px translate — 0.2px of margin, before the .34deg twist takes about 8px at the corners of a 1400px width. 1.06 gives 26px against a worst case near 21. Verified by painting the editor bright red and sampling home's box 24 times across the whole 420ms: **the largest uncovered edge is 0px**. The scale also reads as part of the impact rather than as compensation for it.
- v7.88 — **The cog turns when you press it** (queue 241, first of three parts). His words: *"Make the cog do a little turn animation when you click it."* A quarter turn over 280ms, eased out so it arrives rather than stops. A quarter rather than a full rotation because a cog has radial symmetry — 90° reads as a complete, deliberate movement in half the time, and the panel it opens is already on its way in by then. The restart is done by hand (remove the class, force a reflow, add it back) because a class that is already present does not replay its animation, so without that a second press would do nothing at all — which is the failure mode this kind of touch usually ships with. Off under `prefers-reduced-motion`: it is decoration on a control that works without it. **The other two parts of queue 241 are not in this release and the entry says why** — they need the Canvas settings dialog re-anchored to the button and lifted out of its own backdrop blur, which is a layout change rather than a flourish, and it deserves a clean run at it rather than being tacked onto a 280ms animation. Worth recording for whoever picks it up: inside a project the cog does **not** open App settings — `#btn-settings` forwards its click to `#btn-canvas`, so the panel he is describing is the **Canvas settings dialog**, not the `.set-panel` drawer that the same cog opens from the home screen.
- v7.89 — **The three selection buttons wear a ground of their own** (queue 242). His words, with them circled: *"make these three buttons have a different background to signify their difference."* They are parenting, delete and the ⋯ layer menu — the controls that belong to the **selection** rather than to the project, and they appear and vanish with it, which is a real difference worth showing. **The ground goes on the GROUP, not on the buttons**, and that distinction is the whole design: v7.79 stripped the resting box off every button in this row on his own instruction (*"if you're not hovering over it, you don't show it"*), so putting boxes back on three of them would undo what he asked for a fortnight of releases ago. One quiet pill behind all three says "these belong together and they are not like their neighbours" while each button inside keeps exactly the hover behaviour he specified — the test asserts both halves, so a future version that reaches for per-button backgrounds goes red. **And the ground has to leave with them.** Hidden children still leave their wrapper's padding and background behind, which is an empty lit pill sitting in the transport row whenever nothing is selected — caught by measuring rather than by looking, since 10px of stray pill is easy to miss. The wrapper is marked in step with the buttons now, and the test checks the empty case explicitly. Both mutations red: no ground at all, and a ground that never leaves.
- v7.90 — **An export that loses your sound now tells you which clip and why** (queue 215). His words, the first reproduction this entry has ever had: *"I made a fresh project, added some sound effects, pressed export with some pretty normal export settings and got an audioless clip."* **This is not the fix — it is the thing that has been missing for weeks, which is any evidence at all.** `buildAudioMix` skips layers with bare `continue`s: wrong layer type, no `File` on the media record, audio that will not decode. Every one of them is **silent**. Nothing warns, nothing fails, the file is simply mute — so the failure leaves nothing behind to look at, which is exactly why five rounds of asking about this went nowhere. Each skip now records the layer's NAME and the REASON, and they are reported together: a console line listing every one, and a toast that distinguishes the two cases that feel completely different to you — *"Exporting with NO SOUND — 2 audio clips could not be read"* when nothing made it, and *"1 clip had no usable audio"* when the file does have sound and one of your clips is quietly missing from it. That second case is the nastier one, because nothing looks wrong. **Deliberate silence is kept out of it**: a hidden layer, or one suppressed by solo, is correctly silent and is not reported — a diagnostic that cries wolf stops being read, which is how these quietly die. The test asserts the report names the layer AND the reason, and it has a control proving a hidden layer stays unreported. Mutation red: putting the bare `continue` back. **What is still unknown, and it is written into the entry as a question rather than guessed at:** I could not find a distinct sound-effects source in the code — no `sfx`, no `addAudioLayer`, no `'audio'` layer constructor — so I cannot yet say whether he is hitting the type guard or the file guard. Next time it happens the toast will say.
- v7.91 — **The second silent way an export loses its sound, and it fits his report better than the first** (queue 215). v7.90 made the MIXER say when it could not read a clip. This is the other half, and it is the one that matches *"pressed export with some pretty normal export settings and got an audioless clip"* exactly. The exporter probes whether the browser can encode AAC before it declares an audio track — sensibly, because a muxer that commits an empty audio track to the moov produces a file strict players reject. If that probe fails it dropped the entire mix and said so **only in a `console.warn`**. Nothing on screen. **And the mix at that point was built perfectly** — every clip read, every sample in place — so v7.90's reporting stays completely quiet in this case, which is precisely the experience he described: normal settings, no warning, no sound. It now toasts: *"This browser cannot encode AAC — exporting WITHOUT SOUND."* **The detail worth knowing, because the old code comment invites the wrong conclusion:** it reads as an old-iOS caveat, but `AudioEncoder` support is a property of the BROWSER, so the same project can export with sound in one browser and without it in another, on the same machine, with nothing changed. That is exactly why this has resisted diagnosis from a description for five rounds of asking. **Recorded as a known gap rather than papered over: this path has no direct test.** It lives mid-`run()` behind a real `AudioEncoder.isConfigSupported` probe, so reaching it needs a full export with a stubbed encoder, which is a bigger rig than this change earns today. The two states are now distinguishable from the outside, which is what the entry actually needed — a toast naming a clip means the mixer; this toast means the encoder; neither, with a silent file, means the muxer, and that is the last place left to look.
- v7.92 — **The third and last silent way an export loses its sound — and it is the worst of them** (queue 215). v7.90 made the mixer speak, v7.91 the AAC probe. This one is `encodeAudio` throwing, and it matters more than either because **by that point the muxer has already declared an audio track**: `audio: mix ? {…} : undefined` is decided far higher up, while the mix still existed. So a throw here did not merely drop the sound — it shipped a file whose moov **promises an audio track that was never fed**. That is precisely the *"broken/silent track that strict players reject"* the AAC probe upstream exists to prevent, arriving by a route the probe cannot see: the probe answers "can this browser encode AAC at all", not "did this particular encode survive". A file like that can play silently in one player and be refused outright by another, which is a nastier failure than plain silence. It stays a swallow on purpose — a failed soundtrack must never throw away a render that took minutes — but it now says so, and it names itself distinctly from the other two. **With this, all three silent audio losses in the export pipeline report themselves, and the toast alone identifies which half broke:** a toast naming a clip means the mixer could not read it; the AAC toast means the browser cannot encode; this one means the encode started and failed. Ezra's report — *"pressed export with some pretty normal export settings and got an audioless clip"* — had no evidence attached to it for five rounds of asking precisely because every one of these paths was a bare `console.warn`. The next occurrence answers itself. *Same known gap as v7.91: this path has no direct test, because reaching it needs a full export with an encoder stubbed to throw mid-run. Recorded rather than papered over.*
- v7.93 — **Canvas settings hangs off the cog now, and the cog stays out of its own blur** (queue 241, parts b and c). His words: *"on pc make the canvas settings row show up next to where the button is instead of the middle and make it kinda of come out of the button like how ive traced in the image, so the settings button wouldnt be blured like everything else."* The card stops being a centred modal: it hangs from directly under the cog, right-aligned to it, and grows from that top-right corner over 160ms instead of fading in the middle of the screen. **Part (c) is the one that is easy to drop and hard to notice**, and it is why the whole thing works: the scrim carries a backdrop blur across the entire screen, so without lifting the cog above it, the one thing the panel is supposed to be coming out of is the one thing you cannot see clearly. The cog is raised above the scrim — and only the cog, because lifting the whole row would put the transport controls on top of a modal that is meant to own the screen. The two coordinates come from JS as CSS variables because the cog's position is only knowable at runtime: its x moves with the layout and its y with however far the timeline has been dragged. It anchors to **whichever control was actually pressed** rather than to `#btn-settings` by name — on desktop the cog forwards its click here, on the phone this dialog has other doors, and anchoring to the real opener is correct in both cases without special-casing either. **The test found a bug in itself, which is worth recording:** the cog is dual-purpose — on the home screen it opens App settings, and only inside a project does it forward to the canvas dialog. The suite runs with home up, so the first version pressed the wrong door, and the App-settings scrim it left open then failed an unrelated test several cases later. It closes home first and cleans up after itself now. Mutations red: the cog back under the scrim, and the dialog back to centred.
- v7.94 — **The timecode chip goes yellow the instant you add a benchmark** (queue 243). His words: *"when you add a benchmark it doesnt show up as yellow, youve made it so if you add a bench mark, go away from it then go back itll show the timer as yellow but it should also show up straight away."* He read it exactly right, workaround included, and the cause is a clean example of a state that was never wrong — only never looked at again. The `on-mark` class is decided inside `updateReadout`, which runs on **time** changes. Adding a benchmark does not change the time; it changes the **markers**. So you were already standing on the thing that should have lit, the app just never re-checked, and scrubbing away and back was the only way to make it look. Marker changes are a second input to that class, so they poke it now — on the benchmark toggle, on the thumbnail-frame pin, and on the timeline's own "Add marker here", which is in a different module and had the identical omission (`FM.updateReadout` is exported for it). The test **never moves the playhead**, deliberately: moving it is the bug's own workaround and would hide the defect completely. It also checks the reverse — removing a benchmark puts the chip out without a scrub — because the same staleness in the other direction is just as wrong and would have been easy to leave behind. Mutation red: dropping the refresh.
- v7.95 — **The home tabs stop showing the grain, and the grain stops looking dead** (queue 245). Two halves, and the second is my own regression. **(a)** *"I also want all of the buttons like the one to open up all your projects or elements or tutorials. Those buttons should also be clear but not show the film grain."* Identical hole to the one #227 found in the cards, and the identical fix: these are glass surfaces with a translucent tint and **no backdrop blur**, so the field behind them comes through raw. 12px rather than the cards' 14, because they are small pills and too much blur turns the light behind one into a blob. **(b)** *"the film grain is seemingly still and not moving. It looks kind of cheap… it's okay to just have it fast moving like actual film grain on the TV."* He is right, and this is **a v7.76 regression I introduced**: that release cut the tile count from six to two — correct at the time, since the cards had stopped using them and two is all a cross-fade needs — but **two tiles can only ever go A→B→A→B**. Your eye learns both patterns inside a second and the field reads as one still image breathing. The fix costs no bytes: the tiles are *random* noise, so sliding one by a non-repeating offset yields a visually unrelated field, and stepping through six offsets on each layer turns two images into twelve apparent frames. `steps()` and not a smooth slide, deliberately — v6.62 removed a smooth drift because it read as fabric moving past a window, and the app's own filmgrain effect re-rolls per frame for the same reason. The two layers run at different rates so they cannot fall into step and re-create the flicker. **An existing test had to be widened rather than deleted, which is the interesting part:** it demanded `animationName === 'hm-grain-a'` to catch a forked copy losing the tuned linear curve (#116, #155). A second animation on the same layer is a legitimate addition, not a fork — so it now checks that `hm-grain-a` is *present* and *still linear*, which catches a real fork either way, and asserts the boil directly on top. Three mutations red: no boil, tabs without blur, and a forked cross-fade.
- v7.96 — **The add-menu background reaches the menu's borders** (queue 246). His words: *"the background you added behind everything on the add menu on pc looks whack, it needs to fully go to the borders on that menu."* A fault in v7.85's own work: the gradient went on `.addmenu--panel`, which is only the CONTENT box — measured 307×210 inside a panel taller than that — so it read as a floating rectangle of light sitting *inside* the menu rather than as the menu's surface. Exactly what he is describing. It is on the bordered region now: measured after, the painted surface is the whole 264px panel against a 210px content box, so 54px that used to be bare panel is part of the material, and the gradient ends where the border is. Anchored with `:has(.addmenu--panel)` rather than a body class, deliberately — the add menu appears when nothing is selected and there is no existing marker for that state, so a class would be a second writer for something the DOM already says plainly, and this app has paid for duplicated state before. Still no padding anywhere: v7.85 established that padding here costs the inspector a scrollbar, and two tests catch it. **The test had to be re-anchored, not just re-run**, and that is the part worth noting: it measured the gradient on the *content box*, so it would have gone on passing while the bug he reported was fully present — a gradient on the inner box satisfies "there is a gradient" and looks precisely like the thing he complained about. It measures the panel now, and carries a guard that fails if the two boxes ever become the same size, since at that point it could no longer tell them apart. Mutation red: putting the paint back on the content box.
- v7.97 — **Opening the export dialog stops the transport** (queue 247). His words: *"when you open the export menu playback should pause."* The pause goes in **before the notepad confirm**, which is itself a modal — leaving the transport running through that would keep the exact thing he is complaining about, just behind one more sheet — and before the dialog builds, since the resolution presets are read off the project while the playhead may still be moving. It is guarded on `FM.playing` rather than called unconditionally: every `FM.pause()` bumps the play generation and invalidates an in-flight `requestPlay` (see the note at the top of app.js), and firing that on a dialog open for a transport that was already stopped is precisely the kind of thing that shows up much later as "I pressed play and nothing happened". **The entry asked me to check the sibling doors rather than fix one and leave them inconsistent, so I measured all three** with real playback running: export now pauses; **Canvas settings does not; App settings does not.** Neither of those is changed here, deliberately — he asked for the export menu, and the other two are a judgement call with an argument on each side (Canvas settings changes resolution and fps live, so pausing is arguably right; App settings is appearance, and stopping playback to toggle a theme would be irritating). The measurement is recorded in the entry so it is a decision he can make in one word rather than something I guessed at overnight. The test drives the real `showExportDialog`, so it covers the notepad path too, and it asserts the transport was genuinely running first — otherwise "it is paused afterwards" is true of a transport that never started. Mutation red: removing the pause.
- v7.98 — **The flaky microphone test stops being flaky, by waiting on the real signal** (queue 226). It polled `readyState` on a 6-second budget and went red roughly one run in twenty on a busy machine — and it timed out during a release earlier in this very session, which is exactly the cost a flaky test imposes: **it made a green run stop meaning anything for a minute.** That is the whole argument against them; a test that is red one run in twenty trains you to re-run instead of read. The fix is not a bigger timeout, it is a **different kind of waiting**. `openMic()` now publishes its acquisition promise (`FM.voiceRec._micPending()`), and the test awaits that instead of polling for its side effects. A promise cannot be raced by a slow machine, and — the part that matters for not trading one problem for another — **a genuinely dead microphone still rejects rather than hanging**, so this does not swap a flake for a freeze. The old poll survives as a fallback for a build where the hook is missing, with a generous budget now that it is no longer the primary path. The rejected-promise branch gets its own `.catch` so a caller that never awaits it cannot raise an unhandled rejection — a small thing, but publishing a promise that nobody is obliged to consume is exactly how those appear. **#222 (`key/cold-actually-shrinks`, ~1 in 5) is still open** and is a different shape — an entrance animation delay, diagnosed in its entry — so the suite is not yet trustworthy on a single run. One down.
- v7.99 — **The last flaky test: `unstampIntro` now stays unstamped** (queue 222). `key/cold-actually-shrinks` went red about one run in five, and its entry had already captured the diagnostic that solved it: at the moment of failure the animation reported `state=paused ct=280` while the card **still carried `hm-in`**, its entrance class, with the transform still at identity. So the lead animation had inherited an entrance DELAY — up to 0.82s of it — and at 280ms had simply not begun. `unstampIntro` exists to prevent exactly that and was being called correctly; the problem is that **removing the class is not enough on its own**, because `stampIntro` re-stamps every card in the grid whenever the list is re-entered. A card that has already begun a push can have `hm-in` — and its delay — put straight back on top of it. The smallest honest fix is a flag on the node: the element itself is the thing that must not be re-stamped, so the element carries the answer, and `stampIntro` skips anything already pushed off this screen. **Both flaky tests are now closed** (#226 in v7.98, this one here), which matters more than either fix on its own: a suite that is red one run in five trains you to re-run instead of read, and it cost this session real doubt about a green result during a release earlier today. **Evidence, stated precisely rather than overclaimed: SIX consecutive clean runs, plus a mechanism that is understood and directly addressed.** (Three were in hand when this was first written; the remaining three landed after and the number is corrected here rather than left flattering-but-stale.) Six passes is still not proof — against a one-in-five flake it is about a 26% outcome on chance alone — so the reason to believe this one remains the diagnostic rather than the count: the failure mode was a re-attached entrance delay, and re-attachment is now impossible. If it ever comes back, that assumption is the thing to re-check first.
- v8.00 — **The drawing overlay survives a zoomed canvas** (queue 165, point 3 — the half that was actually broken). The drawing tool has always thrown your zoom away on entry, with a code comment blaming the overlay: *"overlay lays out in screen px — a zoomed viewport double-scales it"*. Measured, that was true — at 2× the overlay was **984×1501 against a 492×751 canvas, exactly double**. `#draw-overlay` lives inside `#canvas-wrap`, which is the element the viewport transform is applied to, so its CSS box is in the wrapper's **local** coordinates while `getBoundingClientRect()` answers in **screen** coordinates; feeding one into the other applies the zoom twice. It is placed in the wrapper's own space now, and the scale is read off the **wrapper** (rendered width against layout width) rather than from `FM.viewport.scale`, so it stays right whatever applies the transform and cannot drift from a second source of truth. Measured after: overlay 492×751 against a canvas of 492×751 — identical. **Nothing else needed touching, and that is worth stating because it is not obvious:** the backing store stays at screen resolution (so it is sharp when zoomed), and a CSS box of `cr.width/k` renders back to exactly `cr.width` on screen — so one unit under the `dpr` transform is still one *screen* pixel, which is the unit `dispScale()` and `redraw()` already work in. **The part I got wrong is more useful than the part I fixed.** I first recorded, confidently, that the stroke coordinate path was 102.8px out under zoom — the bug #97 spent four rounds on. It is not: `toProject` returns 540.0, 1062.8 where the crop says it should return 540.0, 1062.8, a **0.0px** error. My probe had compared the canvas centre against the *project* centre, when at 2× the canvas shows a **crop** whose centre is a different point. So the frightening half of this job never existed, and I would have handed the next session a much bigger, scarier task than the truth. That is the fourth time this file has caught one of my own recorded conclusions being wrong. Mutation red: reverting to screen space puts the overlay back to double size. **Still open in 165.3: the reset on entry and the hand toggle** — the coordinate obstacle they were blocked behind is now gone.
- v8.01 — **Your zoom now survives picking up the pencil** (queue 165, point 3 — the zoom half). His words: *"another option that lets you grab the screen and zoom in or out so you can do more detailed drawing."* The drawing tool used to throw the viewport away the instant you entered it, and that was the correct thing to do while it stood — the overlay laid out in screen pixels inside a wrapper the viewport scales, so a zoomed canvas doubled it (measured: 984×1501 over a 492×751 canvas). v8.00 removed that hazard, so the reset had nothing left to protect and it is gone. **What it buys immediately, without a single new control:** the zoom you already set is kept, and the ⛶ view bar's existing zoom buttons keep working while you draw — which is the "zoom in or out" half of the request, answered with the controls that were already there rather than a second set inside the drawing bar. **Verified on ink, not on maths**, because everything else in this area measures coordinates and the only thing that matters is where the line appears: entering the tool at 2× reports `scale 2` (it reported 1 before), and a stroke drawn through project y=700, x 300…700 produces a layer centred at **500, 702** against a wanted 500, 700 — **2.0px**, which is the freehand curve-smoothing rounding a three-point line, not a coordinate error. **Still open in 165.3: the "grab the screen" half.** Panning is trivially available now (`FM.viewport` has `x`, `y` and `apply()`), but it needs its GESTURE chosen rather than guessed — one finger already means draw, the drawing bar is already full at 380px, and two-finger drag may be the right answer on touch with something else on desktop. That is a design call, and it is recorded in the entry rather than decided overnight.
- v8.02 — **You can grab the canvas and move it while drawing** (queue 165, point 3 — the last half). His words: *"another option that lets you grab the screen and zoom in or out so you can do more detailed drawing."* **The gesture chose itself once the constraints were written down**, which is why this needed no decision from him: one finger already means DRAW and cannot be reassigned; the drawing bar is already full at 380px (measured, 347px of content in a 355px box — an eighth toggle overflows it); and a mode you have to switch into is a worse answer than one you simply do. A **two-finger scroll** — the gesture people already use to move a canvas in every other tool — needs no control at all and cannot collide with drawing, because a wheel event is not a pointer. Shift+wheel pans horizontally so a one-wheel mouse is not stuck on one axis. Measured: a scroll while drawing moves the viewport by 120px and the drawing surface stays exactly on the canvas. **The honest part: my first version of this claimed the overlay stays put because the code re-syncs it, and the mutation proved that wrong** — removing the re-sync changed nothing, because `#draw-overlay` is a *child* of the wrapper the viewport transforms, so a pure translate carries it along with no JavaScript at all. The call is kept for a *scale* change, which does alter the local box, and both the comment and the probe now say which is which rather than taking credit for something the browser does. That is the fifth wrong conclusion this session's own checks have caught, and every one of them was caught by insisting the test fail when the code is broken. **Queue 165 is now complete**: centring (v7.35), erase (v7.78), undo/redo (v7.77), zoom (v8.01) and pan (v8.02).
- v8.03 — **The pan can no longer strand you off your own canvas** (queue 165.3, fixing a trap v8.02 shipped four hours earlier). v8.02 gave the drawing tool a two-finger pan and no limit on it. Measured afterwards: twenty-five flicks put the canvas at **top −5025, bottom −4377 — entirely off screen** — and while drawing there is no way to bring it back, because the ⛶ view bar that owns the zoom and fit controls is hidden in that mode. The only escape was Done or Cancel: commit or lose your drawing blind. **Nobody hit it; I found it by measuring after the fact**, which is the argument for verifying a thing you have already shipped rather than only the thing you are about to. The clamp keeps a **90px corner** of the canvas inside the viewport, and it is applied as a *correction* rather than by pre-computing limits — the canvas rect already knows where it ended up, whatever the zoom and crop are doing, so there is no second model of the geometry to drift out of step with the first. Verified in all three directions at 380px: pan down hard, up hard, and sideways hard all leave exactly 90px visible, and a normal small pan still moves. **Worth noting what this cost me to find: it was invisible to the suite** — 368 green tests, none of which knew a user could pan into nothing — and invisible in a screenshot, because the screenshot I took was of the state *before* the hard pan. It took deliberately asking "what happens if someone does this a lot?" of a feature I had already called done. **And it shipped without a suite test, which is its own lesson:** the mutation I ran at the time went through the probe, and the probe reported the same result with the clamp present and absent — a vacuous check that I read as a pass. There is a real regression test now (`draw-pan-clamp`): it shoves the canvas thirty flicks in each of three directions and fails if fewer than 20px survive on either axis, with a control first proving the pan is actually running so "it stayed on screen" cannot pass for a dead handler. Removing the clamp now fails it with the sentence that matters — *panning down pushed the canvas off screen, and while drawing there is no way to bring it back*.
- v8.04 — **Subtle shading on the notes button** (queue 225). His words, in full: *"Add subtle shading to the notes button."* SUBTLE is the whole instruction, so this is depth rather than a redraw: a soft top-to-bottom gradient down the page so it reads as a lit sheet instead of a flat rectangle, and one faint shadow line under the top edge where a real page would catch its own curl. Both are drawn INSIDE the existing page path, so the silhouette at 24px is byte-identical to before — the mark is unchanged, it has just stopped being flat. The gradient id is namespaced (`fm-note-sheet`) because this SVG is inlined into a document that holds others. Verified in place: the button is 34×34 in the transport row with a 19px icon, the gradient resolves, and the yellow edge and dark rules are both intact. **This one is a lesson about the queue rather than about shading.** It was logged at the very start of the session, on 15 Aug, as the first thing in the list — and then his long PC-layout message arrived, jumped the queue with his explicit permission, and ran for seventeen releases. #225 sat undone behind it and I twice reported the list as "everything done except #244", which was wrong. It surfaced from an integrity sweep of the tick marks, not from memory — which is the entire argument for that file existing, and for auditing it rather than trusting a summary.
- v8.05 — **"Export just this layer" is a picker now, not a tick** (queue 174). His words: *"With the export just this clip tick at the bottom, make it say export just this layer, and also make it so when you press it, it isn't a tick but it's a button and it lets you select what layer."* Both halves, and the second is the one that mattered: the tick could only ever isolate whatever happened to be **selected**, so exporting a different layer meant closing the dialog, selecting that layer, and reopening. The button carries the choice itself — which is exactly why it also had to stop depending on the selection. It opens on **All layers**, lists the project's layers top-down in the order they read on the timeline, and once you pick one it wears its name and takes the accent so the dialog cannot quietly be set to export a fraction of your project without saying so. **It opens on "All layers" every time rather than remembering**, and that is deliberate: soloing a layer is a one-off thing to do to an export, and one that silently repeated last week's choice would hand you a file with most of the project missing and no clue why. For the same reason it is kept out of the remembered prefs. **The old checkbox is deleted rather than hidden**, which the dialog's own alignment test insisted on: a hidden one still got laid out (a stylesheet rule outranks the `hidden` attribute here) and sat adrift of the column — measured, ending at x=499 against x=590 for everything above it. Deleting it also left one source of truth, so there is no second way to ask for this and nothing to keep in step. Verified in the real dialog: opens "All layers", the menu offers All layers / Teal dot / Yellow box, and picking one relabels and accents the button. **This was the first genuinely un-built thing an audit of the list turned up** — #172 and #173 beside it were both already done and merely un-ticked. **Two corrections landed on top of it, both worth recording.** First, I pushed this with a RED suite: an older guard asserts the export dialog has exactly one solo entry point and it checked for the tick by id, so removing the tick failed it. The guard was right and its assertion was stale — it now accepts either shape, because what it has always been about is that soloing has one door and it lives here. Second, and worse: **nothing tested that the picker actually DOES anything.** With the export ignoring it entirely, all 370 tests still passed — the label was guarded and the effect was not, which is the exact shape of a control that looks right and does nothing. There is a test for the effect now (`export-solo-pick`): it asserts the state the exporter reads, not the text on the button, and it asserts the reset between exports too, because a solo leaking from one export into the next ships a file with most of the project missing and no clue why. Both halves mutation-verified.
- v8.06 — **Swipe UP on Recents opens your Faves** (queue 204). His words, the second time of asking: *"it still needs to be added that swiping up on the recents menu in effects opens the faves menu."* This resolves the ambiguity #124 recorded honestly at the time — he had said UP to open and also *"swipe back up and cancel"*, and it was built as pull-DOWN because down was self-consistent with the cancel. He has now said UP plainly and unprompted, so **up opens and reversing (down) cancels**, and the affordance reads "Pull up (or tap) for your faves". **The direction is the easy half. The gate is the one that could have hurt.** The down-pull was legal because it was gated on `scrollTop <= 0`: down at the *top* of a scroller is free, since there is nothing left to scroll up to. Up is only free at the OTHER end. **Negating that gate instead of mirroring it would have claimed the scroll direction in the middle of the list and made the effects browser unscrollable** — a far worse regression than the gesture being the wrong way round, on the screen he uses most. It is `scrollTop + clientHeight >= scrollHeight - 1` now, with 1px of slack because an exact equality never lands on a zoomed or high-dpr viewport and the gesture would simply never arm. Everything else is mirrored rather than reinvented: the reversal-cancel still measures distance back from the PEAK, the damping is still `pow(dy, 0.78)`, and the commit point is untouched at 34 damped pixels — re-tuning constants in the same pass would have made a mis-tune and a mis-claim indistinguishable. The suite's gesture test is mirrored with its assertions unchanged (arm, cancel, cancel-sticks, clean-pull-opens are the same contract either way) and **gains a new one for the gate**: an up-pull away from the end must NOT be claimed. Both mutations red — and the dangerous one is worth quoting, because it reports "scroller at 602/602": at the end, and the negated gate rejects it anyway. Verified at 380px that the browser still scrolls freely, which is the thing that must not break.
- v8.07 — **The app says when an effect cannot do anything to a layer** (queue 180). His words: *"lots of effects don't work on text."* **They all work. The layer is white.** Measured 16 Aug: text defaults to pure white, and on white, Saturation, Grayscale and Hue Shift can *never* do anything — there is no colour to strengthen, drain or rotate — while Brightness and Contrast default to **1.3, i.e. UP**, which can do nothing to a colour already at the ceiling. That is five of the eight colour effects looking broken on the most ordinary text layer there is, two of them at their own default value, with nothing on screen explaining why. **The measurement also killed the one thing that could have made this a real bug:** on COLOURED text (red, teal) every single one of them works — so there is no second defect hiding behind his report, which is the question his entry had been holding open for a word from him. It got answered by measuring instead of asking. **The fix is the app saying so**, which is the same answer #31b's effects half got in v7.50 and for the same reason: nothing is broken, the app was just silent about arithmetic the user cannot see. Two places, because they answer different moments — a **toast when you add it** (you tap Saturation, the picture does not change, and until now the app's only reply was silence) and a **"does nothing here" tag on the inspector row**, which is what answers reopening the project a week later. The effect is still added either way: it is not wrong, the layer just has no colour yet, and deleting someone's choice for them is the ruder half of being right. **The check MEASURES rather than reimplements.** It runs the shipped filter string over one pixel of the layer's own flat colour, so it cannot drift from what the compositor actually does and stays correct if a default changes later — and it asks the question the right way round, not "is white special?" but "does adding this to what is already there change the pixel?", so a Saturation stacked above a Sepia is correctly left alone while the same Saturation underneath it is flagged. It makes **no claim it cannot prove**: blur and glow read neighbouring pixels, so one pixel says nothing about them and they are never judged; a media layer's pixels are unknown, so no claim; a switched-off effect already has the eye to explain it. **A 380px screenshot caught a regression the DOM check missed** — with both "always first" and "does nothing here" on one row, the **eye button was pushed off the right edge**, so the control for switching the effect off became unreachable on exactly the row you would want to switch off. One tag now, never two, and the dead hint takes the slot. The test asserts the EYE, not the tag, because the tag was never the thing that broke. Five tests, four mutations red.
- v8.08 — **"Save project as preset" in Canvas settings** (queue 183). His words: *"This settings menu shall have an option that says save project as preset"*, with a screenshot of the **Canvas settings** dialog — so a preset here is that dialog's own contents: aspect, size, frame rate, background. Deliberately nothing else. Not the layers, not the duration, not the effects: the dialog he pointed at sets up an empty canvas, and a "preset" that quietly dragged a copy of the project along would be a different feature wearing the same word. **It is offered in two places, because saving one is pointless without somewhere to spend it.** In Canvas settings you save the current setup and your saved ones list underneath, each with its size and fps so you can tell them apart, and an × to delete. On the **New project** screen they appear as chips under the name — which is the whole reason to keep a canvas setup, starting the *next* project from it. **Picking one writes the controls, not the project.** You still press Apply (or Create); a mis-tap is undone by Cancel exactly like any other change in that dialog. Applying it straight to the project would have given the dialog a second, hidden Apply button. Two details worth naming: a preset whose named aspect no longer reproduces its saved pixels **falls back to Custom on the exact saved size** rather than quietly resizing someone's preset, and saving under an existing name **replaces** it instead of leaving two rows you cannot tell apart. **The store is treated as hostile, because it is user-editable text on disk.** Every record is validated on the way OUT as well as in — a width of 0, 999999, a junk fps, `null`, a bare number, a `javascript:` background: all dropped or replaced, and unparseable storage comes back as an empty list rather than throwing on the way to the New project dialog. And preset names are user text on screen: every one goes in by `textContent`, never `innerHTML`. There is a test that saves a name containing an `<img onerror>` and asserts no element is created and no script runs; swapping that one line to `innerHTML` turns it red. Three tests, two mutations red, verified at 380px on both screens with Create and Apply still reachable.
- v8.09 — **Speed to the playhead, and the speed cap lifted** (queue 184). His words: *"let's say your clip is slightly too short for what you need, then you can go on the timeline to exactly where you want it to last to, then press a button and it will change the speed to go exactly to that point."* Two buttons above the speed ruler: **park the playhead and the speed is SOLVED**, not nudged. The invariant is the source span — duration × speed, the footage in the clip, which re-timing never changes — so a clip that should end at time T with its start fixed needs speed = span ÷ (T − start). One divide, and the whole value of the feature is that you never work it out yourself: a 4-second clip told to end 2 seconds after it starts comes out at exactly 2.000× and lands on the playhead to the millisecond. One button holds the start and moves the end; the other holds the end and moves the start. **They refuse the cases with no answer** rather than doing something arbitrary — a playhead before the clip starts can't be where it *ends*, and a clip carrying a **speed ramp** has no single speed to solve for, so solving one would silently throw the ramp away; both say so instead. **And the cap he hit is gone** (part 2 — *"alight motion lets you speed up and slow down unlimitedly, you can have something 1000x speed"*): it was 0.25×–4×, it is now **0.01×–1000×**. Widening it doesn't wreck the control because that row is a scrub ruler *plus a typed box*, not a fixed-width slider — the ruler still moves 5% a step so ordinary speeds feel identical, and 1000× is typed. A linear slider over that range would have put 1× a pixel from the left end and made every normal speed unreachable. Worth knowing at the extremes: a `<video>` element's playbackRate is capped by the browser, so *live preview* of a 900× clip can't keep up; rendering and export seek per frame and are unaffected. **Two of my own assertions were dead and only mutation testing found them** — the "held edge stays put" check passed against a version that aimed at the playhead instead, because with no clamping the two produce the identical number. It took a case where the duration floor actually bites to tell them apart, and the test now asserts its own control first so it can never silently stop testing anything again. Five tests, four mutations red, verified at 380px.
- v8.10 — **The settings cog really is out of its own blur now, so you can see it turn** (queue 241, reopened). His words: *"you didnt even give the settings cog an animation like i asked and left it blury. like what i said was so simple to understand and you somehow missed it."* He was right, and it was **one cause with two symptoms**. The quarter-turn animation had been shipping since v7.88 and was running correctly all along — *underneath a blur scrim*, where it could not be seen. **`#t-far`, the far-right cluster the PC transport row puts the cog in, carried `transform: translateY(-50%)` purely to centre itself vertically — and a transform creates a STACKING CONTEXT.** So `body.cv-anchored #btn-settings { z-index: 101 }` was trapped inside that context and could never rise above the dialog at z-index 100. Measured: the cog computed z-index 101 exactly as designed, and `elementFromPoint` over the middle of it returned `#canvas-dialog` — covered, blurred, and not even clickable. The centring is now `top: 0; bottom: 0` plus flex, which centres identically with no transform and no stacking context; the row's alignment is unchanged to the pixel. **The test is the real lesson.** It compared the two z-index NUMBERS, which is precisely the assertion that cannot see this failure — 101 > 100 was true throughout, so it passed while the feature was broken on the layout he actually uses. It now asks what is **actually painted on top** of the cog, which no stacking context can lie about; it checks that only the cog was lifted (the notes and export buttons must stay under the blur); and it walks every ancestor and fails if any of them creates a stacking context, so this cannot be quietly undone later. Putting the transform back turns it red. Verified in **Studio at 1440px** — the layout he uses — which is where the original check went wrong: the same v7.79 lesson, *measure the layout you ship to, not the one you have open*.
- v8.11 — **The canvas settings menu opens into the empty space, fits without scrolling, and closes when you tap outside** (queue 252). His words: *"the way i said the settings cog to open up does not open up like how i showed, look at my screenshot and replicate it. you dont have to follow the sizing strictly as i want it to all fit on screen nicely"*, *"so you dont have to scroll through the menu"*, and *"make it so you press anywhere on the screen out side of it it wil close the menu"*. All three. **The measurement is the argument:** at 1440×900 the card needed **473px** of content and was given **206**, because the cog lives on the transport row low on the screen and the panel hung *downward* into whatever was left — while **634px sat completely empty above it**, which is the region he traced. It now anchors by its bottom edge and grows up into that space: everything visible at once, nothing to scroll. **It picks a side rather than always going up**, which matters — "always upward" is just the old bug mirrored. In the other desktop layout the cog rides near the top of the frame, and anchoring upward there would push the card off screen; the suite caught exactly that, with the cog at y=126. So the side is chosen by measuring the room on each, and the card is clamped to the viewport either way. **Tapping the backdrop closes it without applying** — on the backdrop only, so a press on the card is untouched, and on `pointerdown` rather than `click` so a drag that starts inside the card and releases outside cannot shut it mid-gesture. A stray tap must never silently resize a project, so there is a test that changes the aspect, dismisses by the backdrop, and asserts the project is unchanged. *(Two of my own mistakes are worth recording. The older anchoring test names the cog `cr` and the card `kr`; I read them the other way round while updating it and chased the resulting nonsense for several runs. And I first asserted that it always takes the roomier side — that kept failing on a difference nobody can see, because the app picks its side from whatever OPENED the dialog and in a short test frame neither side has real room. Over-fitting a test to an implementation detail is worse than not having it, so what is asserted now is what he actually asked for: attached to the button, on screen, not scrolling.)*
- v8.12 — **Volume goes to 1000%, on the same scrub ruler the effects use** (queue 195). His words: *"The volume slider needs to be like the effects slider and not a dot on a line, because I want to be able adjust the volume up to like 1000%."* Those are one requirement, not two: an `<input type=range>` maps its whole travel onto its range, so at 0–1000% every ordinary level would be crushed into the first tenth of the bar and 100% would sit a few pixels from the left end. A scrub ruler has no ends to run out of. **The reason this took a gain stage and not a bigger number** is measured in the entry: `el.volume` *cannot* exceed 1 — assigning 2 throws `IndexSizeError` and the value stays 1 — while the export's `GainNode` amplifies happily. Widening the slider alone would have meant hearing nothing while you dragged and then getting a loud, distorted file. So above unity now routes through Web Audio, and **`el.volume` still carries everything up to unity**, which is where fades, solo, mute and the edge de-click already live and are left completely untouched; the two multiply back to the number you asked for. **The irreversible risk was handled by not writing a second router.** Creating a `MediaElementSource` cannot be undone — a second call throws, and once one exists the element's audio flows *only* through the graph, so a dangling source is a permanently silent clip. `js/audio-fx-live.js` already owns that rule for audio effects, so a boost is simply another reason to route: one place that can get it wrong instead of two. **A layer at or below 100% is never routed at all** and keeps today's exact native path — that negative is the load-bearing test here, and routing everything turns three tests red.
