There is a **limiter** (threshold −1.5 dBFS, ratio 20, 3 ms attack) last in the chain, because at 1000% anything near full scale clips hard and hard clipping sounds like a broken file rather than a loud one — and **the export got the identical stage**, only on boosted clips, so it cannot re-shape mixes that never approach full scale. Preview and file agreeing above unity is the entire reason this entry existed. *(One of my tests was dead and mutation testing found it: it set `layer.volume` directly, which skips the panel's own clamp, so putting the 100% cap back left everything green. It drags the real control now.)*
- v8.13 — **"What's slow" — a ten-second measurement you can read on your own phone and send me** (queue 202, and the thing #125 and #95 are both waiting on). **This is the fix for a pattern, not a bug.** Three separate lag reports — #95, #125, #202 — have died the same death: you say it is slow, I measure on this Mac, the numbers come back fine, nothing changes. #125 says it in its own entry: *"every time lag comes up I have measured on THIS machine, found acceptable numbers, and moved on."* #202's measurement ends by naming the only useful next step — the same measurement running **on your device**. The numbers already existed (`FM._perfState`, `FM.playbackQualityInfo`); what never existed was any way for you to see them where the problem actually is. So: Settings → **What's slow** → Measure, use the app normally for ten seconds, then **Copy** and paste it to me. Nothing leaves the device on its own. **It measures the real frame interval via rAF, not the app's own render clock** — and that distinction is the whole reason #125 stayed open. The quality ladder used to watch only main-thread render time, and canvas filter work (GPU) and video decode (off-thread) never land on that clock; six blurs plus six glows once reported **1.1ms a frame** while the app stuttered. The gap between frames sees all of it. The report leads with that, then the tier, the canvas size, the project shape and the device — and it **interprets itself**, because "the numbers look fine" is exactly how this has gone wrong three times: if the frame gap is bad while our own drawing is fast, it says so, and that the cost is GPU or decode. **Two flaws found by actually running it rather than by a test.** First, with the browser pane hidden, `requestAnimationFrame` never fires, so the sample never ended — `running` stayed true forever and Measure was disabled permanently, which is what you would hit the first time you pressed it and switched away. There is a wall-clock deadline now, and removing it turns a test red. Second, the first real report said **"⚠ NOT USABLE"** at the top and **"this sample looks healthy"** at the bottom of the same block — a report that contradicts itself is worse than none, and "looks healthy" is the precise wrong conclusion this feature exists to stop us drawing. It now declines to reach a verdict on a sample it has already called junk. Five tests, four mutations red; the phone layout is asserted in the suite because the pane I take screenshots in reports itself hidden and a slide-in panel cannot paint there.
- v8.14 — **"Sharpen for upscaling" in a clip's ⋯ menu** (queue 203). His words: *"We should add a button in the three dot menu when tapping on a clip to improve quality, so if your video or photo is low quality then you can add pixels or whatever to enhance it."* **The honest version of that, and the honesty is the feature.** Nothing in a browser invents detail that is not in the file — there is no film-and-TV "enhance" — so a button called *Improve quality* would promise something it cannot deliver and read as broken the first time it failed to rescue a genuinely soft clip. What genuinely helps, and helps visibly, is sharpening the detail that IS there once the picture is being stretched over more pixels than it has. So it is named for what it does, and it tells you the real numbers: *"Sharpened for a 4× stretch (270×480 into 1080×1920). This sharpens the detail that is there — it cannot add detail the file never had."* **The dose follows the stretch rather than being a fixed amount**, which matters more than it sounds: a 4× upscale needs a wider radius than a 1.2× one, and giving the 4× settings to a clip that barely needs them produces the crunchy halo that makes "enhanced" footage look worse than the original. Both ends are capped — below 1× there is nothing to recover, and past about 4× extra sharpening only amplifies the compression blocks. A fixed dose turns a test red. **Pressing it twice removes it instead of stacking another**, because "do it again to improve it more" is the obvious thing to try and two unsharp masks is a halo, not detail — and the toggle only ever removes **its own**, so a sharpen you added by hand is never deleted out from under you (that has its own test, and making it remove any unsharp mask turns it red). It measures against the **project raster**, which is what actually gets exported, not the preview, which is a scaled-down view of it. Three tests, three mutations red, checked at 375px.
- v8.15 — **Move & Transform hides the outline and shows the anchor instead** (queue 205). His words: *"Make it so when you open move and transform it gets rid of the outline on the shape or layer, and instead just shows the anchor point as a circle depending on where it is"*, and then *"Same with when opening edit points."* **Built as one rule, which is what his second sentence really asks for:** some sections draw their own thing on the canvas, and while they do, the selection box is clutter sitting on top of it. `FM.inspector.ownsCanvas()` names the section that owns the overlay — Move & Transform, or Edit Points, which already draws its own handles — and the box stands down for whichever it is. Two separate special cases would have drifted apart the first time a third section joined them. The anchor is the right thing to show in its place because **everything rotates and scales around it**, and it was previously invisible unless you happened to be in the anchor sub-mode — so you were positioning the pivot blind. It is drawn from the box's own transform origin, so it cannot disagree with where the layer actually turns; there is a test that rotates and scales a layer and asserts the anchor **does not move**, since a point everything pivots around that drifts when you rotate would be worse than not showing it. **Leaving the section brings the outline back** — the easy half to forget, and the one that would otherwise leave the app with no selection UI at all, so it has its own assertion. **And a real bug turned up on the way:** nothing told the canvas overlay that the open section had changed. The overlay only updates on a render or a canvas gesture, so the outline stayed up until you happened to touch something — opening the panel did nothing at all until then. Found by a test but present in the app. Three mutations red, verified at 375px on a rotated layer.
- v8.16 — **The settings cog turns every time you press it, not just once** (queue 255). His words: *"the setttings cog rotates once but never again until you refresh, prolly coz it doesnt un rotate."* Right that it was a restart problem, and the real cause sat one level below that: the animation has no fill mode, so it genuinely does return to 0° — what failed was the replay. The code used the standard trick (remove the class, force a reflow, add it back), and forced the reflow with `void ic.offsetWidth`. **`ic` is an `<svg>` element, and `offsetWidth` is an `HTMLElement` property — it is `undefined` on `SVGElement`.** So nothing was read, no layout was forced, the browser folded the remove and the add into no change at all, and the animation ran exactly once in the lifetime of the page. Measured before touching it: `ic instanceof SVGElement === true`, `typeof ic.offsetWidth === 'undefined'`. It now forces layout on the **button**, which is a real `HTMLElement`, and cancels any in-flight run first so a fast double-press restarts cleanly instead of being swallowed. **The test counts animations rather than classes**, which is the distinction that matters here: the class was present the whole time with nothing playing, so a class-based assertion would have passed against the bug. Restoring the original one-word mistake turns it red. *(This is the third defect in a row whose root cause was an SVG behaving unlike an HTML element — worth remembering next to the stacking-context one from v8.10.)*
- v8.17 — **The home tabs stagger their cards in, and the tab you press reacts** (queue 207). His words: *"Make it so when you open up any of the 4 menus like projects elements etc it does something like the animation when opening the app where all of the spawn in loading from top to bottom, i think that would look very clean, also adding a little animation to the button you press to open that menu."* Both halves, and **built on the animation he already has** rather than a second set — which the entry asks for by name, because two entrance animations drift and the one that drifts is the one you see most. Three deliberate differences from the first-load version: **only the grid restages** (the top bar and tabs are already on screen; restaging them would make the whole page flinch every time you changed tab), the step is tighter, and the stagger is **capped at ten cards** — 0.4s to the last one however many there are, because first load is a moment you are watching and a tab switch is one you are trying to get through. **Re-tapping the tab you are already on does nothing**, rather than replaying the grid under your finger. The tab's own reaction is a 180ms dip-and-return — a press, not a bounce, over before the first card lands — and it is restarted using the pattern from v8.16 (cancel any run in flight, force layout on the button) so it cannot end up like the cog, which turned exactly once per page load. *(Both of my first assertions were dead and mutation testing found them. They read the real grid, which in the test browser holds one card, so "no stagger at all" and "no cap at all" both passed. The test builds a known 40-card grid now and asserts the delays are distinct, in order, and capped — with 40 cards an uncapped stagger makes the last one wait 1.56s, which is the number the mutation reports.)* Verified at 375px; the multi-card stagger itself is asserted in the suite rather than screenshotted, because this browser only has one project to show.
- v8.18 — **The add sheet's wasted band is gone, and the cards are squarer** (queue 208). His words, with a red ring round the empty strip under *Adjustment / Empty group / Custom elements*: *"We need to utilise this wasted space on phone, each icon in that section could be longer and more square so then it fits it all nicely."* Measured at 375×812 before touching anything: three rows of **111×64** cards (ratio 1.73 — wide and short) inside a body 260px tall, so **52px of it did nothing**. The rows share the body's height now, and Elements comes out at **111×81 (ratio 1.37)** with the band gone. **This is not the change the v5.46 note warns against.** That note says making the inner content fill its parent collapsed the panel three times, because the body was sized *by* its children — circular. That same note then gave the body a stated height, which is exactly what makes filling it well defined now. It also took two goes to reach the cards: the rule was correct and applying from the start, but two wrappers between the body and the grid were still content-sized, so `min-height: 100%` resolved against the very height it was trying to grow past. **The interesting part is the overcorrection, which the first version shipped straight into.** Applied to every tab, filling produced a **111×260 card at ratio 0.43** on Media — a tall sliver, the exact opposite of "more square". So the fill is opt-in per page and the line is drawn at two rows, by measurement: 3 rows → 1.37, 2 rows → 0.88, 1 row → 0.43. One row keeps its natural size and the sheet simply has room to spare, which is honest — better a little empty space than a card stretched to four times its height pretending the space is used. Both halves are asserted, and both mutations (never fill / always fill) are caught by the test's own "this proved nothing" guards.
- v8.19 — **The × and search buttons in the effects header, centred and coloured** (queue 209). His words: *"The search and x buttons in this menu look shit, make them actually centred inside their own circle. Make the x button red and the search one a nice blue."* The entry said to measure the ink rather than the button, and that was the right instruction — the boxes were never wrong. **The × was a text glyph**, and flex centring centres the *line box*, not the ink; where the ink sits inside that box is decided by the font, so it can look level on one machine and high on another. That is unmeasurable from CSS and unfixable in general, which is why it is now an SVG cross that is symmetric about its own centre by construction. **The magnifier was genuinely off by 0.4px**: its circle sits at 11,11 and the handle runs out to 21,21, so with a 2px stroke the ink spans about 3→22 and its centre is 12.5 in a box whose centre is 12. Shifting the viewBox origin half a pixel centres the ink instead of the coordinates. Both now measure **exactly 0.000px** from their circle's centre. Colours are tinted rather than solid — a hard red disc in a header reads as a warning, and this is only a close button — so the glyph carries the colour and the ground carries a hint of it. *(My own centring assertion was dead on arrival: I set a 0.75px tolerance against a 0.4px defect, so restoring the off-centre viewBox sailed straight through it. Measured after the fix, both offsets are 0.000, so the tolerance is 0.25px now — room for sub-pixel noise and nothing else. The colour test asserts "red dominates" and "blue dominates" rather than exact hexes, so a later change of shade does not fail it; the requirement is the colour, not the value.)*
- v8.20 — **Every add-menu tab gets its own colour, and the buttons he named get the colour he named** (queue 210, three of four parts). His words: *"The shapes colours are fine, but the rest aren't. They're generic and copy paste. They need to look quality."* **He was describing the cause precisely.** There was one list of eight hues, cycled by index, on every tab — so Elements and Shape opened with the same run of colours in the same order, and the colour of a button was a function of its *position*, not of what it does. Each tab has its own palette now (**Shape untouched**, because he says it is the one that is right), and the buttons he called out are coloured **by name** rather than by position, so moving one cannot repaint it. **Elements** — *"the main icon can stay bright but the backdrop more subtle"* — gets a cooler, earthier family that is deliberately not Shape's, with the plate dropped to a whisper while the icon keeps its full tint; the contrast between the two is the ask. **Media**: Import a basic grey, Sample clip *"a pinky red colour and blue gradient"*, AI Scene *"a yellow that isn't obnoxious"*. **Audio**: Import audio basic grey, Sound effects *"a rainbow"*, Record voice a strong red. The greys are a system, not three separate asks — Import and Import audio are neutral so that the things which *make* something read as doing something, and that hierarchy is asserted so a later repaint cannot quietly lose it. **Two things only measurement caught.** The rainbow was being painted over: `theme-glass.css` colours every card from `--am-tint` at specificity (0,2,0), which beats a bare `.addmenu-card--rainbow` at (0,1,0) — so under the theme that is actually switched on, the card was a flat orange pour. By eye it just looked dull; it took reading the computed `background-image` to see it. That file's own comment warns about this exact trap for the tabs, and the cards needed the same treatment. And the rainbow at a flat alpha arrived as *"orange"*: blues and violets carry far less luminance against this panel, so the alpha now rises across the sweep. **Still open: the Template tab**, which is not a colour job — he wants the card to show the hero image of the template with its label over it. Recorded in the entry rather than half-done.
- v8.21 — **A bigger hit box on the home +, without a bigger button** (queue 213). His words: *"Make it so the button on the bottom of the screen has a larger hit box, don't make it bigger but larger hit box cos I keep accidentally opening projects."* **Measured before touching it**, because a hit box is invisible and "looks fine" cannot confirm one: at 375×812 the button is 58×58 and its catch area reached **29–30px from centre in every direction** — exactly the disc, not a pixel more — with `#hm-scroll` immediately outside it. So a near-miss lands on the project list and *opens a project*, which is the worst kind of miss because it navigates away from what you were doing. A transparent `::before` inset by −12px now gives an **82px catch area around an unchanged 58px disc** — measured after: reach 41–42px, visual size still 58×58, and a tap 8px above the button reaches the + instead of the list. Two things it deliberately is not. **Not a square:** the diagonal reach is 41px against 42px on the axis, which is a circle — a square would reach 59px into the corners, and those corners are the points furthest from the + and the likeliest to be a genuine tap on a card. **Not larger than 12px:** the ring also blocks scroll drags that begin inside it, and the entry warns that swallowing too much makes the list feel dead near the bottom. It stays clear of the home indicator — the outer edge is still 12px above the viewport bottom. Three mutations red, including the one that matters most: making the *button* 82px instead of the hit box fails with "he asked for a bigger hit box, not a bigger button".
- v8.22 — **Notes belong to one project, and a template no longer hands you someone else's** (queue 214). His words: *"Currently the notes carry across projects, I want each projects notes only for that project, and when you save the project file as well it should save the notes."* **Measured before changing anything, which the entry insisted on — and most of it was already right.** Notes do live on `scene.project.notes` exactly as designed; creating a project, opening one, switching away and back, and the saved `.fmotion.json` were all correct (verified: the serialised file carries the notes, and a brand-new project starts empty). The notepad reads the list fresh on every render, so it was not a stale cache either. **The one route that produced his symptom is a TEMPLATE.** `templates.save` packs the whole project object, so `useAsNew` handed every new project the notes of whoever made the template. Not a global, not a leak — a copy. **The line drawn, deliberately: a DUPLICATE keeps its notes, a TEMPLATE does not.** A duplicate is a copy of that project and losing your notes on duplicating would be its own bug; a template is a reusable starting point, and *"remember to fix the audio at 0:12"* belongs to one project and means nothing in the next. Stripped in two places — at save, so new templates are clean, and again in `useAsNew`, so the templates already on his disk stop leaking without needing a migration. *(Worth recording: each of those two strips SURVIVES mutation on its own, because the other covers it. That is not a dead test — removing both together turns it red. The redundancy is intentional and the two serve different populations, so both stay.)* The tests pin the boundary rather than just the fix: the source project must keep its own notes after being templatised, a new project must start clean, and the saved file must still carry them.
- v8.23 — **"Audio only (WAV)" in the export dialog** (queue 216). His words: *"Add an export option to just export audio."* **WAV rather than m4a, on purpose.** The mix already comes out of `OfflineAudioContext` as raw samples and WAV needs no codec at all — which matters here specifically, because #215 established that a browser can simply refuse to encode AAC and hand you a silent file with nothing to show for it. A soundtrack export that cannot fail for want of a codec is worth more than a smaller one that sometimes does. **The picture controls hide themselves**, which the entry asked for by name: resolution, frame rate, custom size and transparency all disappear, because a control that cannot affect the output is worse than no control — it invites you to set it and then quietly ignores you. They come back on switching away, which has its own assertion, since forgetting that would break the dialog permanently after one visit. **It shares the video path's mixer and its range.** `buildAudioMix` is the same function the MP4 uses, so the soundtrack cannot drift into a second meaning, and it inherits the dropped-clip reporting from v7.90 for free. The range logic was *extracted* rather than copied — two copies of "whole project / this clip / the loop region" is exactly how an audio export ends up covering a different span from the video it accompanies. **Two real bugs found by running it rather than reading it.** First, `buildAudioMix` returns `{ audioBuffer, sampleRate, channels }`, not a bare buffer, so handing it straight to the WAV writer produced no file at all, silently. Second, and worse: **`encodeWav` was mono-only.** It was written for sound effects, which are mono, so it read `getChannelData(0)` and declared one channel in the header — correct for that job, and silently wrong the moment it was handed the stereo project mix. The whole right channel went in the bin and nothing said a word. Caught by checking the exported file's *size* against what a stereo mix should weigh: 192KB where 384KB was expected. The writer follows the buffer's own channel count now and interleaves; a mono buffer still encodes byte-for-byte as before, which is asserted, because the sound effects depend on it.
- v8.24 — **Every way a layer gets into the app is checked now, not just one** (queue 217 + 218). Both of these I found myself on 14 Aug rather than him reporting them, and they belong together. There is one function that rebuilds an imported layer from a known-good schema — effects, audio effects, masks, behaviours, camera, fills — and it had exactly **one caller**: importing a `.fmotion.json`. Inserting a template, inserting an element and duplicating a project all handed the renderer whatever happened to be in the file. **The fix is structural rather than four more call sites.** `reIdLayers` is the gate every batch of foreign layers already passes through — re-iding them is not optional — so the sanitising happens there, and a future way of bringing layers in cannot forget. Running it twice on the import path is harmless: the sanitisers rebuild from a schema, so they are idempotent, and a duplicated call is a far cheaper mistake than a missed one. **And the three saved effect lists** — the effect clipboard, the effect presets, the layer presets — all live in localStorage, all rebuilt `layer.effects` from it, and all checked only that an effect's *name* was real before taking its values on trust. They go through the same sanitiser now, not a second weaker set of checks that would drift from it. **Why it matters more than it did:** #113's filters make an effect a *container with children*, so "which paths validate" stops being academic the moment one can arrive through a path that doesn't. **The control assertion is the one that matters here** — a sanitiser that eats good data would be worse than the hole it closes — so there is a test that a real Luma Matte keeps its cross-layer `params.source` and a real `follow` behaviour keeps its `targetId`, both correctly remapped. *(Turning the sanitising on immediately failed an existing test, and the fixture was wrong rather than the code: it built a `follow` behaviour with no `prop`, which is a channel the app never omits, so the sanitiser correctly threw it out. Verified that a realistic one survives before touching the test.)* A security review of the change found one thing worth fixing: the shared list sanitiser **failed open** when the sanitiser was unavailable, returning the list unchecked — the wrong way round, since that is exactly when unvalidated data must not reach the renderer. It fails closed now. Both mutations red.
- v8.25 — **The doubled version number on the phone: verified gone, and pinned there** (queue 221). I logged this myself at v7.56 — at 380px the build number was on screen twice, once in the desktop top bar beside the FreeMotion name and again as a small label near Export — and noted it rather than fixing it, because it was not its turn. Coming back to it now, **it does not reproduce**: measured at phone width on both the home screen and the editor, exactly one version label is visible. The reason is that the desktop `#topbar` is `display: none` on a phone, which takes `.brand .ver` with it, leaving only `.m-ver`. So a later layout change fixed it in passing — which is worth saying plainly rather than shipping an invented fix for something that already works. **What ships is the guard.** This is a cosmetic bug that a future layout change could quietly reintroduce with nothing to catch it, and two version labels disagreeing after a partial update is precisely the confusion the tap-to-force-update label exists to prevent. The test counts every visible element on a phone whose whole text is a version string and requires exactly one — **and it asserts a control first**, that at least one is visible, because "zero" would otherwise pass an assertion meant to catch "two" while quietly meaning the update control had vanished. Making the desktop top bar visible on a phone turns it red with "the version is on screen 2 times". No behaviour changed; this release is the measurement and the lock.
- v8.26 — **A hidden effect now looks hidden, the same way a hidden layer does** (queue 224). His words: *"when you press the eye button on a layer and it puts a cross through it, changing what it looks like, you should also make it so it does that when you make an effect hidden, right now its hard to tell when an effect is hidden."* A layer's eye **swaps glyph** — the open eye becomes a struck-through one — while an effect's only faded the same open eye at .4 opacity, which on a dark row is barely a difference. That is the whole complaint. The effect eye now uses the layer's own pair of paths, **copied verbatim from `js/timeline.js`** rather than redrawn, so the two controls cannot drift into two dialects of "off"; and the fade is lifted from .4 to .55, because the glyph carries the message now and the dimming only supports it — which is how the layer's eye has always worked. It applies to effects inside a filter too, since filter children are built by the same `fxRow`. *(Two details caught while building: my first version emitted two stacked `<svg>` elements, which would have sat side by side in the button rather than overlaid — it is one `<svg>` with the eye and the slash inside it, as the timeline does. And the test asserts the GLYPH, not the class: a class can be present while the icon looks identical, which is exactly the state being complained about. It checks a control first — that the enabled eye draws no slash — then that hiding adds one, that the markup actually changed, that only one `<svg>` is rendered, and that the slash goes away again on re-enabling.)*
- v8.27 — **The quality ladder can finally see PLAYBACK cost** (queue 202 (partial) and 125 (partial), from his first on-device measurement — it fixes one of that entry's three findings and does not close it). His report from a real iPhone: **44.6 fps average, median gap 17ms, p95 38ms, worst 494ms, 14 of 446 frames late — and the app's own `avgGapMs` reading ZERO.** Sat on **tier 0 of 6** in *smooth* mode, through half-second freezes. **That zero is the bug.** v7.57 taught the ladder to watch the gap between frames precisely because its own render clock cannot see GPU filter work or off-thread video decode — but it only wired that into the SCRUB path. Playback called `notePlaybackCost(ms)` with no gap at all, so during playback the ladder was watching the one number that can never see the cost. **The reason it was left that way was sound, and is recorded in the code:** playback skips frames by design — a 30fps comp on a 60Hz screen renders every other rAF — so a perfectly healthy playback has a 33ms interval, and judging that against the 16.7ms display interval would call flawless playback permanently late and shed quality on a machine keeping up fine. That note ended *"measuring against the PROJECT frame time instead… is a separate change and is not this one."* **This is that change.** The budget is now the project's own frame time while playing and the display interval while scrubbing, so the interval can finally be used without lying. Consecutive renders only, and `pause()` clears the timestamp, so resuming cannot hand the estimator the length of the pause as if it were a frame. *(My first test for this was DEAD and mutation testing caught it: it recomputed the budget formula itself, so it agreed with itself no matter what the app did — putting the display interval back left it green. The budget is extracted and exposed now, and the test reads the real value: restoring the bug fails with "playback at 30fps is being judged against a 16.67ms budget".)*
- v8.28 — **"What's slow" stops calling a stutter healthy** (queue 202 (partial)). His first real measurement was **44.6fps average, median 17ms — and a 494ms freeze with 14 late frames out of 446** — and the report said *"this sample looks healthy."* That is the exact failure this feature was built to end, on its very first use. **A median is precisely the statistic that cannot see a stutter:** half a second of freeze every few seconds leaves the middle untouched, and a stutter is what "laggy" actually means. The verdict reads the **tail** now — the late count and the worst frame — and only lets the median pronounce something healthy once those agree. Fed his own numbers it says: *"the average is fine but this STUTTERS — 14 of 446 frames were late and the worst took 494ms. A median cannot see a freeze; that is what 'laggy' actually feels like."* And because his own drawing averaged 0.34ms, it goes on to name the culprit: GPU work, decode or garbage collection, not the render loop. **The threshold matters as much as the idea** — a verdict that cries stutter at every sample is as useless as one that never does, and would send the next investigation chasing nothing. One late frame in six hundred is ordinary jitter and does not trip it; 1% of frames, or any frame over 250ms, does. Both directions are tested and both mutations are red. **It also now names an oversized project outright**, whatever the frame numbers did: his was **3024×4032 — 12.2 megapixels, photo dimensions, composited every frame on a 4-core phone**. That is the one cause a person can actually fix, so it is said out loud even when the frames look fine. *(The verdict is a pure function now. It had already been wrong once, and a judgement that has been wrong deserves to be testable without standing up the whole probe — my first attempt stubbed `requestAnimationFrame`, produced nonsense timings and broke an unrelated test two cases later.)*
- v8.29 — **Drift and Orbit stop losing content at a frame edge** (queue 228). Not something he reported — found by `tests/_wigwin.html` while verifying #93 — but it is **the same defect he DID report for wiggle**, sitting unfixed in two more effects. The plate handed to a canvas effect is comp-sized, so anything the layer had outside the frame was thrown away before the effect ever ran; translating that plate slides in empty space instead of the content that is really there. Measured against ground truth — the same layer with no effect, genuinely moved by the same delta — a 60×60 layer half off the left edge: **drift wrong over 1,519 pixels, mean error 6.38/255, worst 255**, and 2,048 pixels at the corner; orbit 567. *Worst 255* means solid content against solid nothing. Wiggle got the expanded-plate fix in **v7.32**; these two were never touched. They use the same mechanism now, and measure **0 pixels wrong** in all six cases — fully inside, half off the left, and off the corner, for both effects. **Three things make it safe rather than just correct.** `expand()` already caps its margin at 60% of the comp, which is what makes this usable for drift, whose offset grows without bound with time — past the cap it degrades to exactly today's behaviour instead of allocating an ever-larger canvas. Orbit sizes its margin from the **radius** rather than the current position, so the plate is built once instead of resized every frame of the orbit. And the `near` test keeps the cost off every other frame: a layer nowhere near an edge cannot lose anything and takes the cheap path unchanged. The test scores against ground truth rather than against the old output, and **every case carries a control** — if the "genuinely moved" reference does not actually differ from an unmoved layer, that row throws rather than passing vacuously. Both mutations red, each reporting "worst 255 — it is showing empty space where the layer should be".
- v8.30 — **The add menu drags up over the canvas, independently of the timeline** (queue 244). His words: *"Make it so you can seperatly drag up and down the add menu, on pc, but you cant drag lower than what the timeline is dragged too, if you start dragging it back down and hit the level of the timeline it should pause and snap for a second, showing a little blue flash… And then if you keep dragging down it drags the timeline down with it… when dragging the add menu seperatly it shouldnt push the canvas to be smaller but just go over the canvas."* All of it. **That last clause turned out to be a structural requirement rather than a preference, and measuring is what showed it:** in the Studio layout the add-menu band and the timeline are not two things with two heights — they are *the same grid row* (measured at 1280×800: both at top 616, both 264 tall, 0px apart in either dimension). So "raise the add menu while the timeline stays put" is impossible inside the grid, and floating above the stage is not a nicety chosen for performance, it is the only way the gesture can work at all. While raised, the panel is absolutely positioned against `#app` and its grid slot keeps the undragged height, so nothing reflows. Verified: the panel grew **240→440px while the canvas stayed at exactly 515px**. The floor is the **current** `--tl-h`, read rather than re-measured, so it follows the timeline when that is resized. Coming back down onto it the menu sticks for 9px of travel and the panel's top border — *"the line that seperates the timeline inspector from the canvas"* — flashes accent; pushed past the sticky threshold it writes `--tl-h` and the timeline follows, measured going 240→189px with the canvas correctly *growing* to 562. It mirrors `#tl-resizer`'s structure without sharing its writer, has its own clamp (`FM.clampAddMenuH`, exposed so the suite tests the one that runs) and its own stored key, and can never swallow the whole window. Built with what he says is coming — *"the add menu is going to become a tall, resizable browser that must never cover the canvas"* — in mind. Three mutations red, including the one that matters most: making it resize the grid row instead of floating.
- v8.31 — **A "?" for the keyboard shortcuts, on the phone bar and on the PC transport row** (queue 248). His words: *"Put a question mark in the top right corner to the left of note pad that quickly opens the keyboard shortcuts menu"* and *"On pc it can go on the play button row along side everything else."* Half of it already existed — `#btn-help` was wired to the overlay and to the `?` key — but it lived in the desktop-only top-bar group, so **on a phone it was never shown at all**, and on PC it sat in the header rather than the row he asked for. The phone gets a real new button; the PC one is a move, joining `#t-far` so the order reads **ver · ? · notes · cog · Export · ⛶**. Both open the same overlay through one handler rather than a second copy that could drift. **It is one slot further left than he described, and deliberately.** "Left of the notepad" lands exactly between the version chip and the notepad — and that gap is half of a pair he judged by eye and signed off in #189 (*"the space between the settings cog and notes pad is perfect"*), which the suite asserts still match. Putting the button through the middle of it broke that immediately: **64px against 18px**, caught by the existing test rather than by me. So it sits beside the pair instead of inside it. **And it is shown whether or not something is selected**, because the notepad only appears with nothing selected — pinning "?" to the notepad would make it come and go with your selection, which its own entry warned about. It shifts about 4px as the right-hand cluster changes width, the same as the version chip beside it already does, but it never changes slot or disappears. *(The PC half is asserted as a list, because `btn-notes` went missing in precisely this migration once — v7.52 moved everything else down and left it behind in the header. Dropping one now is red rather than shipped. Both mutations caught, though the phone one took three attempts to write: I guessed at the class that hides things on selection twice before measuring that it is `body.m-editing`.)*
- v8.32 — **The selection cluster recesses instead of glowing, and its bin is centred** (queue 251). His words: *"the back drop for the three icons like the bin in this photo, make the backdrop more subtle, maybe instead of being brighter than everything else make its lightly darker, and also it isnt centred and aligned so fix that."* Both, and the second one was not what I assumed when I logged it. **The ground** was a white wash at 4.5% — and a light wash over a dark row reads as a *raised* panel, which next to a row whose other controls have no resting box at all (v7.79, on his own instruction) made the brightest thing in the row a background. It is a dark wash now, so the group still reads as separate but by sitting back rather than shouting; the inset hairline goes with it, since a recess does not need an edge to be legible. **"Isn't centred and aligned" turned out to be the ink, not the boxes** — the same class as #209 and found the same way, by measuring rather than looking. The pill is exactly as tall as the buttons inside it (34px, 0 difference) and dead-centred against the play button (0px). What was actually wrong: **the bin's glyph sat 0.4px low.** Its path runs y 5→20, so the ink centre is 12.5 in a viewBox centred on 12 — the identical arithmetic to the magnifier in #209, which he did notice at the identical magnitude. Shifting the viewBox origin half a pixel puts all three glyphs at exactly 0.00. *(Recorded because my own logged note guessed wrong: I wrote that the pill looked "visibly taller than the icon row" from his screenshot. It was never taller. Measuring first would have saved the guess, and the entry is corrected rather than left to mislead.)* Both mutations red — the bright wash, and the bin's original viewBox, which reports "0.00,0.40px off its button centre".
- v8.33 — **Sliders can hit an exact number now** (queue 253). His words: *"when editing a shape the sliders move to quickly, i cant precisely get the exact size i want, cos it jumps a lot of numbers, leaving me to type in what i want."* Measured before touching anything: a shape's Width scrubs at **3.6 units per pixel of drag**, so the smallest change a finger can make is about four units and every value between is unreachable — falling back to typing was the only way to land on a number, exactly as he said. **The rate turned out to be only half of it, and the smaller half.** The applied value snaps to the ruler's *notch quantum*, which is deliberately coarsened to keep the strip legible, so slowing the drag alone just meant a longer drag for the same jump. Measured that way too: with only the rate changed, a drag at the finest setting moved the value by **exactly 0**. So fine mode now snaps to the parameter's **real step** instead of the display quantum — the ruler keeps its coarse notches, because they are a legibility device rather than the precision. **The gesture is the iOS scrubber idiom:** keep your finger on the strip and nothing changes, so the whole range is still one comfortable drag — which matters, since #184 took the speed row to 0–100000% — but slide away from it vertically and the rate steps down. Chosen over the two obvious alternatives on purpose: a fine-drag *modifier* is useless on a phone, and slowing everything would make that speed row untraversable. Steps rather than a smooth falloff, so you can learn where "quarter speed" is. **Two things came out of building it that were not in the plan.** A fourth, finer rung was tried and removed — combined with step-snapping it made some rows change nothing at all, and a control that appears dead is worse than one that is merely coarse. And the momentum **glide** ran at full rate after release, so a careful fine drag got flung past the number you had just aimed at; momentum is for covering distance and fine mode is for landing exactly, so the glide now stands down when the drag ends in the fine zone. Measured by hand: 20px of drag moves a Width by **56 units on the strip, 9 at 120px away, 3 at 220px**, landing on 235 and 232 — values the 3.6 grid could never reach. *(Both mechanisms are unit-tested rather than driven through synthetic pointer events: I tried that first and the momentum glide and the touch direction-lock both interfered, so the test could not reliably see what it was measuring. Three mutations red, including one for a rate so slow it feels dead.)*
- v8.34 — **Groundwork for keyframed edit points: the interpolator** (queue 254 (partial)). His words: *"edit points has literally no keyframe functionality. add it."* The entry said to check whether the machinery already existed before assuming, and it half does — **the pen masks have keyframed a whole path since they were built**, with an interpolator that handles easing, hold steps and topology changes. `FM.evalShapeSubs` is that pattern applied to a shape's own point set, and it is the hard half of this feature. **Two design decisions are settled and tested rather than left to whoever builds the rest.** A keyframe captures the **whole point set**, not one point: a per-point track would need identity for every vertex across every keyframe, and inserting a point in the middle would silently re-number everything after it. And a **topology change SNAPS** — interpolating a 2-point path towards a 3-point one has no correct answer, since you would have to invent a vertex and decide where it came from, so the earlier keyframe is held until the later one arrives. The masks make the same call, and predictable beats clever for something you are animating by hand. **A static shape takes exactly the old path** — `Array.isArray` is the first test and it returns the very same array object, so nothing is allocated and no arithmetic runs on the render hot path; a mutation that copies it turns a test red. **What is deliberately NOT done, and why:** wiring it into the renderer means threading the current time through `FM.traceShapePath`, and two of its three callers have `t` while the third is a measurement helper that would need it from its own callers — surgery on the hot render path, which deserves a clean run rather than the tail of a long session. That is the same call made for #244, which then built cleanly in one go. The remaining steps are written into the entry with the call sites named, so the next session wires instead of researching.
- v8.35 — **The flaky microphone test, actually fixed this time** (queue 256). It failed twice in one day — once mid-mutation-run, and once during a release, where it did real damage: `tools/ship.sh` correctly refused to push a perfectly good change because the suite was red. **A red run that means nothing is worse than no test**; it trains you to re-run and shrug, which is exactly how a genuine regression gets waved through. **v7.98 was supposed to have fixed this, and the reason it did not is the interesting part.** That release replaced a `readyState` poll with "await the real acquisition signal" — the right idea. But the acquisition starts on a **microtask**: `Promise.resolve().then(_openMic)`. So when the test asked for the pending promise in the same turn as `open()`, it got **null**, skipped the await entirely, and fell straight through to the poll it was written to replace. The fix was silently never taken, and the flake it was aimed at simply carried on. **Two changes, and both halves matter.** The promise is published **synchronously** now, so anyone asking in the same turn gets the real thing. And it resolves only once the tracks are actually assigned rather than when `getUserMedia` returns — so awaiting it means *"the mic is ready"*, not *"the request came back"*; otherwise a waiter can wake before `micTracks` is set and find nothing, which is the same race one level down. **The test now asserts that the promise exists** rather than quietly falling back to polling when it does not, so this cannot fail the same way twice. Verified by three consecutive full runs with no variance, and the mutation that re-delays the publication turns it red with *"the mic promise is not published synchronously, so awaiting it is skipped and the flake comes back"*.
- v8.36 — **Elements icons pop again, and Sound effects gets a white icon in a gradient ring** (queue 257 + 258). **#258 is a regression I introduced in v8.20, and he was right about it.** His original #210 wording was *"choose more subtle background colours, the main icon can stay bright but the backdrop more subtle"* — **two** things — and I collapsed them into one by muting the palette itself. Since an icon takes its colour from the same `--am-tint`, muting the palette muted the icons too; his v7.30-against-v8.24 screenshots showed it plainly. The hues are vivid again (cyan, magenta, lime, amber, indigo, rose, mint, azure — still deliberately not Shape's, which was the other half of #210) and the backdrop is kept faint by the low-alpha plate instead. One hue per card doing two jobs: full strength on the icon, 5.5% behind it. Measured after: icon saturation 110–170 where the muted palette sat at 60–90. **The two halves are now asserted separately** so they cannot be collapsed into one again. **#257**: the Sound effects icon is white — the one colour that sits on all six hues, where before it wore a pink stand-in tint that fought the rainbow behind it — and the border is a white *gradient*, bright at the top-left and fading round, rather than flat. **The obvious way to do that border is wrong here, and I shipped it before catching it.** Two backgrounds — the fill clipped to the padding box, the gradient to the border box — is the standard trick, but this card's fill is deliberately translucent so it reads over the glass panel, so the border-box layer showed through the *whole* card and washed the rainbow out to a pale smear. Caught by looking at it. It is a masked pseudo-element ring now: a full-size gradient with the content box masked out, leaving only the 1px frame, with the rounded corners free from `border-radius: inherit`. Three mutations red, including the muted palette and the flat ring.
- v8.37 — **The phone's trash icon is red** (queue 259). His words: *"Make the trash icon red."* The PC transport row has done this since v7.79, so this is the phone catching up rather than a new idea — the one destructive control in a row of otherwise neutral ones should not look like its neighbours. It is the **same** red the PC uses (`#ff6b7a`), not a second one: two reds for one action across two layouts is the sort of drift that turns into a "why are these different" message a fortnight later. The test guards exactly that by reading the PC's colour **out of the stylesheet** and comparing, rather than hard-coding the hex in the assertion — so changing the shade in one place cannot leave the two layouts quietly disagreeing, and a mutation that uses a different red fails with *"the phone bin is rgb(224, 49, 49) but the PC one is rgb(255, 107, 122)"*. It also asserts a control: the neighbours must still be neutral, because "red" means nothing in a row that is entirely red. The entry asked to check the layer ⋯ menu's Delete at the same time so the two doors to the same action would not disagree — there is no second door left, since #221 removed that entry in v7.48.
- v8.38 — **Two of the three tests blocking "one desktop layout" were asserting Classic-only facts** (queue 249 (partial)). His ask is *"I just want two layouts not three"*, and the only thing standing in the way is that the whole suite has ever run in Classic. Trialling Studio as the default reddens three tests; **two of them were wrong about the app rather than the app being wrong**. The **align buttons** were required to be over 80px tall — a number written when Classic's tall right-hand column was the only desktop layout there was. Studio's inspector is a **264px band shared with the timeline**, so three buttons that fill it correctly measure ~43px, and the old floor called that a failure. His claim was never "80px", it was *"fill up the whole section"* — which the gap-to-the-bottom check already measures — so the height line now only has to rule out the phone's little strip. The **side-column dock** test now skips under Studio instead of failing: Studio has no side column by design, its inspector is a band, and the editor uses the floating-card fallback there. A test insisting on a layout that no longer has to exist is not a defect report. The **third** is still red and is now instrumented rather than guessed at: it reports **canvas 186×330 at y=14, popover 560×347 at y=16, window 900×760**. The Aa popover opens *upward* from the card, and at a 760px window height a 347px popover cannot help but reach back over a canvas that starts at y=14 — so this is a **short-window geometry problem, not a Studio one**, which is a different and more useful thing to know than "Studio is broken". Also fixed here: that test built a **320×240** project, a canvas so small any popover covers nearly all of it; it uses 1080×1920 now, which is what he actually edits.
- v8.39 — **One desktop layout, not three** (queue 249). His words: *"For some reason the new layout i had you make only shows up on certain display sizes like my laptop, but then an amalgamation of the old one shows when I flip my phone or use a wide monitor, I don't want this, I just want two layouts not three."* **It was never about display size.** There were two desktop layouts chosen by a saved setting, and **classic was the default** — so his laptop had studio from when we built it, his ultrawide (a different machine, different storage) still had the classic default, and a phone held sideways is over 700px so it took the desktop path and got classic as well. Three in practice, exactly as he described. **Studio is now the only desktop layout**, the Classic/Studio chooser is gone from Settings, and a saved `classic` is **migrated** rather than honoured — honouring it would leave every machine that ever opened the app before today on the layout he asked to be rid of, and he would have to hunt down the setting on each one, which is the situation he reported. **The blocker was never the app.** Trialling this reddened three tests and all three were asserting Classic-only facts: an 80px floor on the align buttons written when Classic's tall column was the only desktop layout (Studio's inspector is a 264px band, so buttons that *fill* it measure 43px); a side-column dock test in a layout that has no side column; and an Aa-popover check whose 320×240 fixture made any popover cover "the canvas". **The one real fix** is a popover placement rule, and it is not about layouts at all: the popover opened upward capped to *all* the room above the card — and above the card is the canvas, so on a short window the cap simply licensed covering it. Measured at 900×760: canvas 186×330 at y=14, popover 560×347 at y=16, i.e. **99.5% of the picture you are typing on**. It now measures the room above that is genuinely clear of the canvas and opens **downward over the timeline** when there is not enough — which is not the thing you are looking at while you type, the same trade `layoutDesktop` already makes for the card itself. That fixes short windows in every layout, not just this one. *(The obsolete guard was inverted rather than deleted: it used to insist the layout chooser survive the removal of the Classic theme, and now insists it stays gone — "the chooser came back" would mean the third layout came back with it.)*
- v8.40 — **Edit Points can keyframe (queue 254).** His words: *"edit points has literally no keyframe functionality. add it"*. v8.34 built and tested the hard half — the interpolator — and deliberately stopped before the wiring because it is surgery on the render hot path; this is that wiring, and the ◆ he actually asked for. **The entry's own instruction is what made this small:** it said to check whether the machinery already existed, and it did — the pen masks have keyframed a WHOLE path since they were written, easing, hold steps and topology snapping included, so nothing here had to invent path interpolation. Four things moved. `FM.traceShapePath` takes a **scene time** and reads through `FM.evalShapeSubs`; it is the LAST parameter and optional, so every existing caller keeps working and one that does not know the time falls back to the playhead rather than silently freezing at frame 0 — which looks identical to working. `FM.shapeToPoints` does the same, so "convert to editable points" hands back the shape you can SEE. `shapeOutlineLenPx` had to have `t` threaded through it too, and that was the fiddly bit the previous run stopped at: on an animated path the outline LENGTH changes frame to frame and Trim Paths divides by it, so measuring frame 0 against a moving path would make a trimmed stroke creep. And the point editor now reads at the playhead, with a ◆ on its rail. **The bug worth recording is the one the tests caught rather than I did.** Editing between two keyframes has to CREATE the keyframe it edits — AE-style roto, the same bargain `mask-tool.js` already strikes — so `ensureKfHere()` runs before every mutating path. That much I wrote. What I got wrong is subtler: at a keyframe that is neither the first nor the last, `evalShapeSubs` still takes the interpolating branch (with the fraction landing on 1) and hands back a **fresh array**, so the edit landed on a throwaway and the point snapped back the instant anything re-rendered. The editor now takes the STORED array whenever the playhead is on a key. *(A keyframe holds the whole point set, not one vertex, and a topology change snaps — both settled in v8.34 and unchanged. Also found while mutation-checking: the CROP panel's ◆ can be deleted outright and all 437 tests still pass, so that one is unguarded — noted in the entry, not fixed here.)*
- v8.41 — **Two effects that silently deleted your layer (queue 261, 262).** Both came out of the 16 Aug sweep rather than from him, and both are the same shape of bug: no crash, no warning, just a layer that stops being there. **261, Halftone Lines:** the stripe period is multiplied by the preview's pixel scale, and at a period of one pixel every row shares the same position in the pattern, so the threshold beats it for any luminance below pure white and the whole layer is written black. Measured on a 1080×1920 project at size 3 — **64,800 white pixels at full resolution, 24,300 at half, and zero in the 360-wide preview.** The reason this matters more than it looks: `ps` is moved by the QUALITY LADDER, so a halftone layer could turn into a black rectangle mid-playback on a phone while the exported file was perfectly fine — a preview/export disagreement that is close to unreportable from the user's side ("it went black sometimes"). The scaled period is floored at 2, because a stripe needs two rows. Honest trade: at the smallest sizes the preview's stripes are now slightly coarser than the export's, which is the resolution limit rather than a bug. **262, Hot Colour:** Low and High are two independent sliders and nothing stopped them crossing. At Low == High the span is zero and the old guard mapped every luminance to ramp position 0 — black in four of the six palettes — while a negative span inverted and clamped to one end. Measured on rgb(53,220,175): 50/50 gave flat black, 80/20 gave flat rgb(40,0,141) with every pixel identical. Crossed sliders now read as the window they describe, and the test asserts 80/20 and 20/80 agree — otherwise "swap" is just a different way of being arbitrary. *(That test needed a GRADIENT fixture: on a flat shape "all detail destroyed" is unmeasurable, since a flat layer is one colour whether the effect works or not. The fixture guard asserting a range exists BEFORE testing the defect fired on the first run and caught me using the wrong gradient shape.)* **Still open from the same family:** 263, where Kaleidoscope's Centre sliders erase the layer at their end stops — held back because the right behaviour there is a judgement call rather than arithmetic.
- v8.42 — **Kaleidoscope stops deleting your layer (queue 263).** The last of the three bugs the 16 Aug sweep found. Reading the sampler settled the mechanism and it is worse than "the wedge is empty": the fold sends every sample into a narrow wedge from the centre, and `drawWarpEffect` **clamps** an out-of-range sample to the edge pixel of a plate the size of the whole **project frame** — an outermost row that is empty margin for any layer not touching the edge. So an escaped sample does not read the wrong content, it reads *nothing*. At Centre X = 100 **every** sample escapes, which is why the layer vanished completely. The mirrors bounce now: the sample reflects off the frame edge and back over the content, which is what a kaleidoscope physically is, so there is always something to see wherever the centre is parked. Measured after, on a 360×640 preview of a 1080×1920 project, counting pixels that differ from the background: default 75,904 · centreY=0 **3,829** · centreY=100 **4,295** · centreX=100 **125,334** · centreX=0 **126,643** — every one of those was **zero** before. **The honest part:** this changes how an existing kaleidoscope layer looks, and not by a little. At the DEFAULT centre **52.8% of the frame's samples were already escaping**, so more than half of what the effect drew was blank smear; that blank is what becomes mirrored content. It is the effect finally doing its job rather than a restyle, but it is a visible change to old projects and the entry records the narrower alternative (keep clamping, just stop the sliders reaching the values that erase everything) in case he wants the old look back. *(Noticed while reading it and deliberately NOT fixed: `phase` is added BEFORE the fold, so "Rotation" shuffles the mapping but never rotates the SOURCE wedge — that slider cannot change which part of the image gets mirrored, which is arguably its whole job. A behaviour change rather than a bug fix, so it is its own item.)*
- v8.43 — **Effect options that ran off a phone are all reachable now.** His note said six effects' option rows overflowed at 380px and that the fix was a taste call. Two things came out of picking it up. **First, the item had been unreachable rather than merely unstarted:** the command in CLAUDE.md for finding the next job matched `^- \[ \] \*\*[0-9]`, and that `[0-9]` silently skipped every UNNUMBERED item — ten of them, including this one. That is the precise failure REQUESTS.md exists to prevent, caused by the tool meant to enforce it, so it was fixed structurally first (`tools/next.sh`, which lists unnumbered items first as the oldest and prints a total). **Second, re-measuring made it worse than reported:** the row is clipped by `.fx-row.fx-open`'s `overflow-x: hidden`, so the overflowing buttons could not be scrolled to either — Channel Remap put **five of its eight options genuinely out of reach**, HSL Bands three of nine. The cause is the flexbox `min-width: auto` floor: `flex: 1` gives a basis of 0, but a `nowrap` button will not shrink below its own text, so the row grew past the panel instead of fitting inside it. The rows wrap now. Ellipsis was rejected because these labels are only distinguishable BY their text ("Swap R/B" and "Swap R/G" are the same button once truncated), and a horizontal scroller was rejected because a hidden row of options with no affordance is the same bug wearing a gesture. Rows that already fitted are untouched — wrapping only engages once content exceeds the line. Verified at 375px with a screenshot: Channel Remap's eight options on four tidy rows, all tappable. **The taste call is recorded in the entry and reversible in one word.** The test sweeps every effect rather than the six named, which is how it found **12** offending buttons across more effects than the note listed.
- v8.44 — **A deleted clip's memory is finally given back.** From the "Editing lags, and gets bad fast" entry, whose own note said: *"FM.media never releases a deleted clip's record, so memory grows with every import you throw away. That one needs undo-stack surgery and was deliberately deferred."* The surgery was the whole difficulty. `FM.deleteLayer` keeps the record **deliberately** — undo restores the layer's JSON only, so freeing media there made an undone delete come back permanently **blank**, which is worse than the leak. So the test is not "was it deleted" but "can it still come back": a record is released only when its id appears in **neither** the live scene **nor any snapshot on the history stack** — the whole stack, so anything a *redo* could restore is kept as well. The sweep runs from `history.commit()` at the moment a snapshot is discarded (by eviction or by dropping the redo tail), because that is the only moment an id can stop being reachable. The IndexedDB blob is untouched — `pruneOrphans` still owns that at boot; this is RAM only. Freeing also detaches the `<video>` element's `src`, which matters more than revoking the URL: an element still pointing at a blob keeps its decode buffers. **A hazard I nearly shipped, found by a mutation run rather than by me:** `FM.media` is keyed by layer id, but not every entry belongs to a layer in `FM.scene` — `js/fx-thumbs.js` parks `_fxthumb*` records for layers in its own private sample scene. The first version of the sweep ate them and every effect thumbnail fell back to the same picture sixteen times. There is an explicit `FM.media.pin(id)` now rather than a rule about underscore prefixes, because a naming habit is not something the next module to park a record here will know about. Three tests, all mutation-checked, and the first one guards the dangerous direction: freeing must never beat undo. *(The entry itself stays open — its other half is waiting on Ezra to say whether editing feels better on his own device.)*
- v8.45 — **Reversed audio now has a test on the path that actually matters (the export).** From the "Rebuild the two lost audio diffs" entry. Neither diff needed rebuilding, and checking that first is the only reason this did not cost a morning: the **reverse** feature is already in main on both paths — `js/audio-play.js` synthesizes the reversed AudioBuffer for the preview, `js/exporter.js` reverses the sample index for the file — which is the same shape as the twelve staged diffs that turned out to have shipped. What was genuinely missing was coverage: the one reversed test in the suite checks the **waveform drawing**, so every exported reversed clip could have come out forwards and the suite would have stayed green. The export's sample-index arithmetic is now `FM.srcSampleAt`, pulled out as a pure function — the loop it lived in sits inside an async export that nothing can call, which is exactly why it had no test — and asserted properly: forward output 0 reads the first source sample, reversed output 0 reads the **last**, positions are strictly decreasing across the whole clip (a reversal that is not monotonic is a scramble, not a reversal), a trimmed clip's start offset survives, and speed scales the step both ways. The **misc** half is closed as not actionable: nobody recorded what it contained, so there is nothing to rebuild — that is a gap in the notes rather than a task, and leaving it open only costs future sessions a re-read.
- v8.46 — **The other half of the media leak: a project switch was stranding every clip you deleted.** v8.44 freed a deleted clip's memory once no undo or redo could reach it, and I thought that was the whole job. It was not, and working BUG-HUNT.md is what showed it — that file raises this twice, both entries pointing at `js/storage.js:707`. v8.44's sweep runs when history **discards** a snapshot; a project switch calls `history.reset()`, which discards nothing, so every clip deleted in the outgoing project survived the switch and stayed pinned for the life of the page. Measured before fixing, by running the exact teardown sequence `projects.open()` performs: the record was still in the store afterwards. BUG-HUNT put the cost at roughly **140 MB for one discarded 3-minute track** — an iOS Safari jetsam, which reads to the user as the app randomly reloading and losing unsaved edits. `history.reset()` now sweeps as well. That is safe at every caller, checked one by one: reset runs either with the new project's layers and media already in place (project open, import, template insert) or with both empty (boot). Both BUG-HUNT findings are struck through with the version that fixed them, per that file's own convention. *(The general lesson is the one BUG-HUNT.md states at the top and I proved again: a fix aimed at a defect class can miss a documented instance of it. The file's warning is to re-check the code rather than trust its line numbers — it is worth reading the other way too, as a list of instances to check a fix against.)*
- v8.47 — **Every clip you ever used was filed under Add → Audio as a song.** From BUG-HUNT.md, re-verified against today's code before touching it. `backfill()` seeds one library row per media layer by reading **layer JSON, never the file**, so it hardcodes `w:0/h:0` — and `isAudio()` infers "song" from exactly that shape (not an image, no dimensions). So every backfilled `kind:'video'` row was classified as audio: the Media tab showed only photos, and the whole clip library turned up under Audio drawn with a music note and no thumbnail. Anyone who ran v3.72–v4.13 with existing projects has a poisoned index. **The report's prescribed fix was half right.** It says to write `audio: false` on backfilled rows — but an mp3 rides the **video** path as a 0×0 `<video>`, so that alone would misfile genuine songs the other way. So: backfill states the common case, a one-time repair pass heals indexes poisoned before the fix, and `getThumb` **learns the truth from the real file** the first time a tile is drawn, writing back real dimensions and the correct flag. The repair targets `fp === ''` — backfill's own marker — which is what makes it safe, because a real import always carries a fingerprint and is never considered. **And the fix was inert until my own test caught it:** `isAudio` read `e.audio === true || <shape guess>`, so writing `audio: false` changed nothing — the guess ran anyway. The flag is authoritative in both directions now, with the shape heuristic applying only where the flag is absent, which is exactly the case it was written for (rows saved before it existed).
- v8.48 — **Free crop no longer eats a keyframed crop animation.** From BUG-HUNT.md, re-verified before touching it. `layer.crop.x/y/w/h` are **animatable containers** — `FM.cropOf` evaluates each through `FM.evalProp`, and the Edit Shape panel gives them a ◆ and an easing curve — but crop-tool's `commit()` assigned a brand-new object of four plain numbers, so every `{kf:[…]}` was thrown away. Build an animated crop reveal, then touch Free crop and press Done, and the whole animation collapsed to one static rect. Worse than a wrong pixel: `commit()` goes straight on to `FM.history.commit()`, which snapshots the flattened crop and autosaves it, so the loss was written to history and persisted with no warning and no way back through the UI — Free crop's Done looks like any other Done. It writes through `FM.setProp` now, the same API the panel uses, which upserts into an animated container and plain-assigns a static one. So an ordinary crop behaves exactly as before (asserted separately, because a fix that turned every crop into an animated one would be its own bug) and an animated crop gains a keyframe at the playhead instead of losing all of them. *(Its sibling finding — the Free-crop overlay ignoring flipH/flipV, so the committed rect is the mirror of the box you dragged — is still open and is the next one up.)*
- v8.49 — **Free crop was the mirror image of the box you dragged, on a flipped layer.** From BUG-HUNT.md, and the sibling of v8.48. `crop-tool.js` rebuilt the layer matrix by hand as T·R·S·K and never read `flipH`/`flipV` or the parent chain, while the compositor draws through T·R·S·K·**F**. So with "Flip Horizontally" on, the dimmed mask, the rule-of-thirds grid and all eight handles drew mirrored — the hole did not sit under the box your finger was drawing — and the rect written to `layer.crop` was the horizontal mirror of the region you framed. Because a crop is stored in **source pixels**, the export kept the wrong half of the frame too, so it was never a preview-only artifact. **point-edit.js had this exact bug and was fixed in v6.53; crop-tool never was** — which is the drift the report predicted, and the reason the fix is one shared helper rather than a third hand-rolled matrix. `FM.layerUVToCanvas` / `FM.layerCanvasToUV` now live beside `layerCTM` in the compositor and are used by **both** overlays. They run the compositor's own `applyLayerTransform` into a probe context, so they carry the flips, the parent chain and the z-perspective *by construction* rather than by anyone remembering to add them; the hand-derived fallback (for browsers with no `getTransform`) includes the flips innermost, exactly where `applyLayerTransform` puts them. Both files' private copies are gone — point-edit keeps a trimmed `xform()` for one non-coordinate read, with a note saying not to route a point through it again.
- v8.50 — **Layer thumbnails stop spilling onto the timeline on a phone (queue 211 (partial) — the entry stays open for its other half).** His report named the mechanism more precisely than a guess should: *"like something that isnt there is pushing it out"*. The collapse chevron on a childless layer carries `.th-chevron--empty`, which was `visibility: hidden` — the one hiding method that stops an element painting but keeps its **full layout box**. So 16px of button plus its 6px gap, **22px of pure invisible width**, sat between the eye and the thumbnail. Measured at 375px: the head is 72px, the thumbnail's right edge sat at **82px** — 10px out over the divider — and `.th-thumb-wrap` is `overflow: visible`, so it spilled onto the timeline rather than clipping. **Two obvious fixes were measured and both were worse.** `display: none` on the empty chevron breaks the column alignment between grouped and ungrouped rows that queue 191 exists to guarantee — there is a test for it and it caught the attempt. Letting the thumbnail absorb the whole deficit shrank it to **5px**, which is not a picture. So the head grew to what its contents actually need: `--head-w` 72 → 90 on phone (7 + 14 + 6 + 15 + 6 + 34 + 7 = 89), with a shrink-and-floor as a safety net for a group row or an indented child. After: every row clears by 8px, an ordinary row keeps its full 34px thumbnail, and the timeline is not squeezed — it scrolls, so the clip width is unchanged. **The entry stays OPEN on purpose:** he asked for two things, *"stretched and going out too far"*, and this is the second only. The stretch has a different cause — `FM.renderThumb`'s shape branch traces into a fixed 26×12 rect instead of contain-fitting the layer's own aspect — and the remedy already exists as `FM.SHAPE_ASPECT` from #159. *(Also fixed here: this entry had been glued to the end of another one's last line, so it never started a line and every oldest-first tool, which all match `^- [ ]`, was blind to it. It was not deprioritised, it was unreachable — `tools/next.sh` now shouts about malformed entries.)*
- v8.51 — **🚨 A refresh no longer flashes the old Alight-Motion-shaped layout (queue 292).** His words: *"when you refresh the page it shows an old version that looks a lot like alight motion, this is bad because if someone sees it in the final version they may be able to use this against us."* He was looking at the **first paint**. `layout-studio` was applied by `js/settings.js`, so until that script ran the page rendered the unclassed default — and measured at 1400px the two are not subtly different: unclassed gives grid rows `50px 552px 258px` with the inspector as a right-hand **side column** at x=1092, while Studio gives `602px 258px` with it as a bottom-left panel. A top bar plus a right-side inspector **is** the Alight-Motion-shaped arrangement — it is the Classic layout v8.39 retired, still sitting in the stylesheet as the default. **Why only on refresh, which is the part worth keeping:** a cold load is covered by the splash, and the splash deliberately skips on a same-session reload (`sessionStorage` `fm.splashed`) — so the single path that exposes the naked first paint is exactly the one he was taking, which is why it lasted this long unspotted. The class now sits on the `<body>` tag in the markup, so the right layout is in force at parse time; Studio has been the only desktop layout since v8.39, so it is unconditional, and settings.js's toggle now merely agrees. The guard reads **index.html as shipped** rather than the live DOM — by the time the suite runs settings.js has long since added the class, so checking `document.body` would pass regardless and prove nothing. *(This stops the old layout being seen, not shipped: the Classic CSS is still the default in styles.css. Deleting it properly is #293, kept separate because it is a refactor across a large stylesheet and this was urgent.)*
- v8.52 — **Layer thumbnails stop lying about the shape they show (queue 211, the other half).** His original words were *"they're like stretched and going out too far"*, and v8.50 only fixed the second. `FM.renderThumb`'s shape branch traced into the whole padded box, so every shape was scaled to the THUMBNAIL's aspect rather than its own: a 26×12 box turned each layer into a ~2.2:1 lozenge, and a square shape, a tall shape and a wide one all came out as the same pill. It contain-fits the layer's own `shapeW:shapeH` now and centres the remainder — those two numbers *are* the layer's on-canvas aspect, because `traceShapePath` maps the normalized path into exactly that box, so the thumbnail and the canvas now agree by construction. The media-fill and gradient branches moved onto the same fitted box, or the picture and the outline would have disagreed. Same fix the add menu got in #159, for the same reason. **The test measures painted PIXELS, not the numbers the code used** — a fix that computed a tidy box and still traced into the old one would pass anything that read variables. Measured before: a square shape painted **26×12, 2.17:1**. After: a square paints square, a 4:1 shape paints wide, and crucially the two *differ* — before the fix they were the same lozenge, so a test checking only one could pass while the thumbnail still described nothing. Verified at 375px with a screenshot: a tall bar, a circle and a wide bar, each recognisably its own layer. **Both clauses of #211 are now done and the entry is ticked** — one report, two causes, two fixes.
- v8.53 — **Selection handles are a real finger target now (queue 98 (partial) — clause (d)).** His report was *"the text box and its handles are small and fiddly at that size"*, and of #98's four parts this is the only one that was ever ours: (a) and (b) describe iOS Safari's own form-assistant bar, which no browser here can produce, and (c) depends on his project's aspect. Measured at 375px on a text layer: the visible dot is 10px and the touch pad added 14px a side — a **38px** target, under the 44px the platform asks for and under the ~40px that rule's own comment claimed it was aiming at. The pad is 17px a side now, so the target is exactly **44px**. The dot is unchanged, so nothing looks different — only the area that answers a finger, and the test asserts the dot did *not* grow, because a "fix" that enlarged the square would satisfy a size check and look wrong. Two things checked and found fine rather than assumed: the text-wrap handles report zero size at (0,0) but are `display: none`, so there is no invisible grab zone in the canvas corner; and no two live handles' pads collide at that size (the corners sit 57px apart). *(Worth recording: the first version of this test used `FM.addTextLayer`, which also OPENS the text editor as a side effect — the editor it left running broke an unrelated add-menu test three cases later. A fixture should build state, not start a mode.)*
- v8.54 — **The slam stops breaking the screen on PC (queue 250 (partial)).** His report: *"THe slam easter egg is still broken as fuck, it just bugs out the screen on pc and doesnt look good at all"*. Reproduced by freezing the 420ms animation mid-shake, which is the only way to look at it properly. **It was the overscan, not the shake.** Frozen at 1440×900 the screen rendered **1522×953 at (−40,−30)** — the wordmark clipped off the top-left corner, the search / Select / cog clipped off the top-right. **And the overscan was not a mistake:** the shake moves home 13px and twists it .34deg, and home is `position: fixed; inset: 0`, so those 420ms uncovered the editor behind it (#144). That was patched first with a flat ring — which later turned out to BE the black bar he kept reporting (#237/#239) — and then with a 6% scale so home's own lit surface filled the gap. It works on a phone. On a desktop, magnifying a full-viewport surface by 6% pushes everything outward proportionally and the controls at the edges leave the screen, which is exactly why this read as broken on PC and fine on mobile. The fix keeps the guarantee and drops the cost: home's paint field now **overhangs its own box** (`::before` at `inset: -8%`, carrying the background colour as well as the light), so nothing can be uncovered and nothing needs scaling to hide it. The shake itself is untouched — same travel, same twist. Two tests: one that the animation never scales, one that the paint still overhangs far enough — so removing the scale cannot silently reopen #144. *(Left open for his eye: the screen-breaking half is measured and fixed, but "doesn't look good" is his judgement to make.)*
