My next suspect, written up in the request so it is not lost, is the service worker: it falls back to a cached copy of the page whenever a refresh's network request fails, that cache is never rotated, and a stale page would then pull in stale scripts — handing you a genuinely old build. If it happens again, look at the version chip: if it shows an OLD number, that confirms it and I can stop guessing.
- v9.12 — **The three matte cleanup tools stop needing each other (EFFECTS-PLAN round 25).** All three exist to tidy up a key, and each one forced you to stack a second effect to finish the job. Matte Choker eroded on whole pixels with a hard square kernel and no softening, so a choked matte kept stair-stepped edges — it feathers in the same pass now, with an edge-contrast control to re-harden the ramp so a feathered matte does not go milky. Matte Fringe painted an opaque flat band with hard inner and outer edges, which always read as a pasted-on sticker rather than a rim light; it has strength and softness, taking it from 2 distinct colours to 57. Smooth Edges could only shrink the matte into the artwork as you softened it, with a visibly banded straight ramp — there is a choke to push the edge back out and a smoother falloff option that widens the ramp by over half. One thing worth recording from building it: most effect kernels in this file are a single very long line, and adding an explanatory // comment in the middle of one silently comments out the rest of the function. It broke Smooth Edges the moment I did it, and I reverted and redid it with the function expanded.
- v9.13 — **Four more colour effects stop being one setting each (EFFECTS-PLAN round 26).** Solarize was a hard per-channel flip at full strength — always a 100% harsh colour break — and now softens, mixes back, and can flip on brightness instead of tearing the channels apart. Spot Colour snapped everything outside the kept hue to 100% grey and never boosted the colour it kept, so the one hard sin-city look was all it did; it drains partially now, boosts the kept hue, and can invert to grey out the chosen colour instead. Four-Colour Gradient lerped straight toward the gradient, so at any usable amount it painted over your footage — it can Screen, Multiply, Overlay or Soft-light against the picture now, which is what a colour wash actually is, plus a spread control. And Night Vision was hardcoded green with fixed grain, so amber, white-hot and blue scope looks were impossible.
- v9.14 — **Dots, Hexagon Array, Mosaic and Dissolve stop being one pattern each (EFFECTS-PLAN round 27).** Dots had its dot-to-cell ratio and its opacity both hardcoded, so the only pattern available was "medium dots at 85%" with hard aliased edges. Hexagon Array locked its line weight to 12% of the cell, so scaling the hexes always dragged the line thickness with them and a large cell with a fine line was impossible. Mosaic could only make square, averaged, gapless blocks — it does wide scanline cells, a tile grid with gaps, and crisp nearest-neighbour retro sampling now. And Dissolve was one static salt-and-pepper pattern, identical in every project, that ate the frame uniformly with hard-edged holes and never moved; it can sweep in from any side, soften its holes, and boil over time. Two of my own mistakes are worth recording because the tests caught both: the sweep was written BACKWARDS — "from the left" ate the right-hand side, and the total number of holes was identical either way, so only counting them per half of the frame showed it. And Mosaic's block geometry could not be measured at all on the standard test image, because it varies so fast that every block averages to the same colour; it needed a picture that changes smoothly in both directions.
- v9.15 — **Border Frame, Contour Lines and Contour Strips get their controls (EFFECTS-PLAN round 28) — and that is the effects plan's whole table done bar two.** The border was welded flush to the very edge of the layer with hard square corners at full opacity, so a matte-style inset frame, a rounded one, and a subtle translucent one were all unreachable; it insets, rounds and blends now. Contour Strips had its alternating strong/weak opacities hardcoded, so the recolour was all-or-nothing and always landed in the same places. And Contour Lines had a real problem rather than a missing knob: it traced its bands off RAW brightness, so any real footage shredded into confetti instead of forming contours. Smoothing the brightness BEFORE banding it is what turns noise into contours — 30% fewer stray marks on a noisy image — and it can now draw as a clean topographic map on white paper rather than only scribbling over the picture. **What is left of that plan:** Spin and Flip Layer, which work on whole canvases rather than pixels and so need different handling, and Gaussian Blur, which needs a compositing path. Five more the plan itself says to skip. Everything else in it is built.
- v9.16 — **Spin and Flip Layer get their controls — and that is the whole effects plan's table finished.** Spin started at exactly 0° at every clip's start, so two spinning layers could never be offset from each other, and it always turned about the layer's centre; it takes a start angle and a pivot now. Flip Layer flipped in place and threw the original away, so all it could ever make was "the same layer, backwards" — it can hinge the reflection out to one side and keep the original, which is how you build a symmetry composite. **Where the plan stands: 105 proposed upgrades, all built except Gaussian Blur (which needs a compositing path rather than a slider) and five the plan itself says to skip.** The only genuinely unbuilt features named anywhere in it are Corner Pin, LUT import and Curves — each needs a new kind of control (on-canvas drag handles, a file picker, a curve editor) rather than another parameter, which is why they were always last.
- v9.17 — **The AI could not switch on any tick-box or pick any source layer — it silently wrote garbage instead (BUG-HUNT backlog).** When the assistant added an effect, it ran every parameter through a clamp against that parameter's minimum and maximum — but a tick-box and a layer-picker have neither, and clamping against nothing produces NaN. So the registry's perfectly good default was thrown away exactly WHEN the AI supplied a value. It then failed silently in both directions: NaN is not null, so every downstream "use the default if missing" check sailed straight past it, and NaN saves into the project file as null. In practice: ask for Apple-style rounded corners and you got a plain rounded rectangle, and every effect that points at another layer — Displacement Map, Luma Matte, Compound Blur, Match Grade, Polar Displace — came out with no source at all and did nothing. Six parameters across the catalogue, all of them broken, none of them complaining. Fixed, plus the capability list the model reads now describes a tick-box as "0|1" rather than "undefined..undefined", which is what invited a number into a control with no numeric range in the first place. The guard is written over the WHOLE catalogue rather than one effect, because the defect belonged to the parameter TYPE.
- v9.18 — **Exporting a transparent GIF or PNG could permanently delete your project's background colour (BUG-HUNT backlog).** To render with transparency the exporter set the project's background to nothing for the duration of the export — but that is the SAME object the app saves to disk, and the app saves automatically the moment you switch to another app or lock your phone. So: tick Transparent, start a long export, put the phone down — and the save that fires writes "no background" into your project. The exporter put the colour back in memory afterwards but never re-saved, so the file on disk kept the null until some later save happened to run. If the app was force-quit first — most likely right after an export, which is the most memory-hungry thing it does — the project reopened with no background at all, and every render and export after that was missing it. The same window could also bake a background-less thumbnail into your project list. Fixed by the general rule rather than the one case: a render option is now a temporary flag the renderer reads, and never a change to saved state. Worth noting the test needed two halves — driving the flag proved the mechanism was safe, but putting the old line back still passed, because nothing here runs a real export. It now also checks the shipped exporter contains no assignment to the project background at all.
- v9.19 — **Dragging several clips at once no longer silently changes the spacing between them (BUG-HUNT backlog).** Each clip in a multi-selection was stopped independently at its own left limit, so the moment a SHORT clip could go no further it froze while the longer ones kept travelling with your finger — and letting go made that permanent, keyframes and all. The worked example: a 6-second clip at 4s and a 1-second clip at 0.5s, dragged 3 seconds left, and the gap between them quietly goes from 3.5s to 1.9s. Nothing warns you; each bar just stops on its own. The selection now works out a single limit for the whole group before it moves, so it stops as one the instant its most-constrained clip would run out of room, and everything keeps its spacing. Dragging one clip on its own is unchanged — with nothing else selected the shared limit works out to exactly the old one. The comment a thousand lines further up the same file already stated the rule this broke: a hold-move on one selected clip must not silently break the others' relative sync.
- v9.20 — **Copying a keyframe from one effect could paste it into a completely different effect (BUG-HUNT backlog).** An effect keyframe remembered its position — "the first effect's amount" — which is fine on one layer and meaningless on another. Copying onto another layer is a feature the app advertises, and dozens of effects share the parameter name "amount" with wildly different ranges: Twirl runs -360 to 360, Grayscale runs 0 to 1. So copying a Twirl keyframe of 140 and pasting it onto a layer whose first effect was Grayscale drove that 0-to-1 control to 140 — fully desaturated from the paste point onward — and saved it. Worse, if the target effect had no parameter of that name at all, the paste INVENTED one and wrote the junk into the project file, while you saw no keyframe appear and got no error. The clipboard now remembers which kind of effect the keyframe came from and refuses to paste into a different one, and it will never create a parameter an effect does not have. Pasting between two layers that DO share the effect still works exactly as before — that is the point of the feature, and the test checks it, because a fix that simply refused every cross-layer paste would have passed everything else.
- v9.21 — **"Template saved" could be a lie, and the space it took was never coming back (BUG-HUNT backlog).** Saving a template writes the heavy part — the layers plus full copies of every video and image in the project — into the big storage area, then writes a small index entry into the small one. If that second write failed because storage was full, the app said "Template saved" anyway: the code returned success without ever checking. So the template never appeared in your list and could not be recovered, while its copy of all your media sat in storage with nothing pointing at it — and the boot-time cleanup was written to skip exactly that kind of key, so the space was gone for good. The same shape of mistake was in Save as element, Save selection as element, and Import font. All four now check, roll the heavy part back, and tell you the truth; the cleanup also collects packs nothing references instead of skipping them blind. One thing worth admitting: I spent a diagnosis cycle convinced normal saving was broken, because I had been calling it with its two arguments the wrong way round — it takes the NAME first, then the project. The A/B against the previous version is what showed me it failed there too, i.e. that it was my test and not the app.
- v9.22 — **The eye that hides a layer is finally big enough to tap (BUG-HUNT backlog).** It was a 15x15 pixel icon — about 4mm, against the 44px that counts as a touch target — sitting inside the track head, which selects the layer when you tap it. So missing the eye by 8px did not miss harmlessly: it selected the layer and slid the inspector sheet up over the timeline instead of toggling visibility. The icon has not moved or changed size; only the invisible area that responds to a finger has, and the thumbnail beside it still selects as before. Every other small control in the stylesheet had already been given this exact treatment — the trim handles, the keyframe dots, the favourite stars — the eye was simply the one that got missed. **Also struck a second finding as STALE rather than fixed:** the report that the floating + button covered the row-reorder handles at the bottom of the screen. Re-measured in exactly the state it describes, that button no longer renders at all — the Add-layer row replaced it — so its proposed fix would have reserved empty space for a control that is not there.
- v9.23 — **Keyframes on trim-path, the repeater, the dash offset, mask paths and audio effects can finally be copied (BUG-HUNT backlog).** The timeline drew a diamond for all of them, but copy kept its own shorter list of which properties it knew about — so Copy on those diamonds returned nothing at all, and because it returned nothing the "Paste keyframe at playhead" item never even appeared. Your copy silently vanished. Delete had the same gap and had been patched by hand at some point; copy never was, which is exactly what keeping two hand-written lists guarantees over time. There is now ONE list that delete, copy and paste all read, and a test that fails the moment they could drift again: every property that draws a diamond must be addressable, so adding a new animated thing to the app and forgetting the list is caught immediately rather than in six months by a user whose copy did nothing.
- v9.24 — **The X and Y boxes in Move & Transform go dead the moment they touch a snap line (BUG-HUNT backlog).** Drag the X number and the instant it reached the middle of the frame — or either edge, or any X the layer sits at on one of its own keyframes — it stopped, and no amount of further dragging moved it. Measured on the phone: X walked 600 → 552, snapped to 540, then sat on 540 for the remaining ~190px of the drag. The box re-read the value before every step and the snap had already rewritten it, so each 1-6px of movement was rubbed out before the next event arrived; only a single jump bigger than the 8-unit snap radius escaped. A scrub gesture now remembers where it started and how far it has travelled — which is exactly what the Move trackpad beside it always did, and why only the number boxes were stuck — so a snap grabs on the way in and releases on the way out. It is one shared `scrubGesture` used by BOTH number scrubbers, so a third cannot be added later without it. Two faults the bug report had not spotted, fixed by the same change: the identical read-back made Motion Blur **Samples** completely immovable (0.08 per pixel into a whole-number round is always the number it started from) and killed **Crop Width** on any project narrower than ~1400. Dragging far past a hard limit no longer banks travel you have to un-drag before the value moves again. Verified live at 375px, before and after, with the snap itself confirmed still working; two mutation checks.
- v9.25 — **Undo inside the mask editor was being silently thrown away the moment you touched a point (BUG-HUNT backlog).** Editing a mask path, pressing undo, then dragging any point put the whole pre-undo shape straight back and committed it — the step back was gone, and nothing said so. Before that even happened the teal overlay stopped agreeing with the picture: it kept drawing the old shape while the layer itself snapped back, so the handles sat visibly off the mask. The editor took one copy of the point list when it opened and worked from that copy forever; undo replaces every layer with a fresh object parsed from the snapshot, so the mask the editor was aimed at became a different object holding different coordinates while the copy stayed behind. It never noticed, because it finds its layer and its mask by id and both ids survive. Now it follows the model: it re-points itself whenever the mask it was seeded from is no longer the one the scene holds — checked by the undo itself the instant it swaps the scene (a drag starting before the next animation frame would otherwise still be too early) and again every frame by the overlay's own draw loop, which covers loading a project. **A second bug fell out of writing the test:** the double-tap-to-delete timer outlived the editor, so closing and reopening it within a third of a second and tapping the point that happened to share an index with the last one you touched deleted that point. Checked the other on-canvas tools while in there — point-edit re-reads the live layer on every operation, and crop, fill-drag and text-edit hold no geometry, so this was the only one. Verified live at 375px with the overlay photographed back on the restored mask; two mutation checks, one per test.
- v9.26 — **A group was being drawn at full project resolution and then thrown away down to the preview's size — 16x the pixels, every frame, on the project he measured (queue 125 (partial), queue 202 (partial), queue 95 (partial)).** Every other buffer in the compositor is allocated on the TARGET's pixel grid: effect plates, the camera plate, the manual-blend plate. Exactly two were not — the plate a group is flattened onto, and the plate a mask blend mode rasterises through. Both were built at project size and never stamped with the render scale, so the drawing code inside them ran at 1:1 no matter how small the preview actually was. On the phone report in that entry — a **3024x4032 project, 12.2 megapixels**, inherited from an imported photo, previewing into a 762k-pixel canvas — that is **16.0x the pixels needed**, allocated, cleared and drawn per group per frame. An ordinary 1080x1920 comp on a phone previews at about 0.48, so it was **4.3x** there, measured live. Export is untouched by construction: an export canvas is exactly project-sized, so the scale is 1 and every number is what it always was, and there is a test asserting that. **This is asserted as a SIZE, not a timing** — that entry records four passes that measured this area on a fast Mac, found acceptable milliseconds and moved on, and a pixel count is the same number on his phone as it is here. A third test compares a quarter-scale render against a full-scale one pixel by pixel, because a saving that changes the picture is not a saving. **Being straight about what this is not:** the scene in his own measurement had no group in it, so this is a real cost removed, not proof his lag is fixed. The 12.2-megapixel project itself is still the biggest thing in that report and still needs answering.
- v9.27 — **Importing a phone photo into an empty project was creating a 12.2-megapixel composition — that is where his slow project came from (queue 125 (partial), queue 202 (partial), queue 95 (partial)).** The first file you import sets the project's size, and it took the file's pixel dimensions verbatim with no limit at all. A stock iPhone still is 3024x4032, so dropping one into an empty project built a **12.2 MP** comp — bigger than anything the app's own Canvas settings picker offers, whose top entry is 2160p. That is exactly the project in his own measurement: 8 layers, 2 effects, 4 cores, half-second frame stalls. It is capped now at the short side the picker's largest preset means — 3024x4032 becomes 2160x2880 — with the aspect ratio kept, both sides even (H.264 refuses an odd dimension, so an uncapped import could produce a project that would not export), and a message saying it happened and where to change it. Nothing at or under the top preset is touched, which is tested for four common sizes. **Anyone who genuinely wants bigger can still type it into Canvas settings** — this governs only the size the app picks unasked. **Second fix, same shape as v9.26:** the onion-skin ghost plate was project-sized AND reassigned every frame, and assigning width/height reallocates the whole backing store — a 48 MB allocation thrown away twice per frame on his project. It is on the preview's pixel grid now and only reallocated when the size really changes; measured live at 380px, 518x921 instead of 1080x1920 on an ordinary comp, with the ghosts photographed still landing in the right places either side. **What this does not do:** it cannot shrink a project he has already made — Canvas settings does not rescale existing layer geometry, which is its own job — so this stops it happening again rather than repairing his current file.
- v9.28 — **Changing a project's size no longer scatters everything in it — which is also what lets an oversized project finally be repaired (queue 125 (partial), queue 202 (partial), queue 95 (partial)).** Canvas settings has always let you change the width and height, and it has always done only that: every layer kept the pixel coordinates it had, so resizing a finished composition threw the work wherever those old numbers happened to land, silently. That is a bug on its own, and it is also why v9.27's import cap could not rescue the 12.2-megapixel project it was written for — shrinking it would have wrecked the layout. There is a **Scale the layers to fit** switch under Size now, on by default, shown only when there is actually something to move. **The list of things that have to move is short, and short for a reason:** nearly everything lives inside a layer's own transform and follows its scale for free — a shape's width, a font size, a stroke, a repeater's offsets. Only three kinds of value are in absolute project pixels: a ROOT layer's position and scale (root only — a child's numbers are already parent-local, and scaling both ends applies the factor twice, which is a mutation check of its own), pen-mask paths (canvas-space points with no layer transform at all), and drop-shadow blur and offset (`shadowBlur` is device pixels by specification and the current transform does not touch it). Pixel-measured effect parameters move too, read from the registry's own `unit: 'px'` rather than a hand-written list, and clamped back into each slider's declared range. A different aspect ratio uses the smaller factor and re-centres, so the work stays whole instead of stretching. Undo puts back the size AND the geometry, verified. **The main test renders both sizes and compares where the pixels land** rather than checking arithmetic — and the cheap count assertion deliberately runs after the render, because first it short-circuited every mutation and left the picture untested.
- v9.29 — **THE TIMELINE WAS DRAWING EVERY CLIP 18px LEFT OF THE TIME IT WAS AT, in every project, since v8.55 (queue 327).** His words: *"the timeline is actually broken and is starting early"*, *"every project is broken the same way"*, *"refreshing page didn't fix"* — and it could not be fixed by refreshing, because nothing was wrong with the saved file. The playhead is a fixed line at the middle of the screen with the timeline scrolled underneath it, so the entire geometry rests on one number: how wide the track-head column is. That number was read with `getComputedStyle(document.body)` — and since v8.55 the narrow head has been declared as `#tl-tracks.tl-no-groups { --head-w: 72px }`. A custom property cascades DOWN, never up, so body went on reporting the 90px default while the column rendered at 72. **A project without a group — which is nearly every project — therefore drew t=0 eighteen pixels away from the line that means t=0.** Measured at 375px: clip at x=169.5, playhead line at x=187.5. It now MEASURES the column that actually rendered instead of reading a constant off some other element and hoping the two agree, so a future rule can move that variable anywhere and the geometry cannot come apart from the layout again. The test asserts the thing the geometry exists for — a clip at t=0 lands on the playhead line — in both the group and no-group layouts, and checks the two layouts really do differ so neither case can quietly stop covering the other. The whole suite was green throughout the ten months this was broken, because nothing had ever asserted it. **Also in this release: the Presets card shows each preset rendered ON YOUR LAYER** (the unnumbered "Check I changed the right Presets" item, which his answer today finally settled — he meant the inspector's Presets card, and wanted it to work the way the effects browser already does). Every row is a live tile of this layer with that preset applied. The tile is produced by running the REAL apply on a throwaway clone, not by a second description of what applying does — a preview that could disagree with the button beside it would be worse than no preview, and that seam is now a test. A preset this build cannot apply gets no tile, because there is nothing it could honestly show.
- v9.30 — **Auto-tracking froze the whole app for its entire run, and could then tell you it worked when it had not (BUG-HUNT backlog, two findings that had to be fixed together).** Press Track and everything after the decode ran as one uninterrupted block — both match passes, the template adaptation, the simplification and the keyframe write — so the browser could not draw until the whole thing returned. The app locked solid, nothing scrolled, and the "Tracking… 40%" toast the code goes to the trouble of computing was written to the page and never once appeared on screen. On a phone a multi-second block like that also risks the OS killing the tab. It stands aside every eighth frame now. **The yield is a MessageChannel post, not requestAnimationFrame:** rAF stops firing in a hidden tab, so switching apps mid-track would park the tracker forever, and a 0ms timer is floored to a second or more when backgrounded — the exporter learned both the hard way and its note says so. **And that fix makes the second bug easier to hit, which is why both are here.** A live editor means the clip can be undone or deleted while the track runs, and the tracker was holding the layer object it started with: after an undo that object is detached from the scene, so the keyframes went into an orphan, were silently discarded, and it still reported "Tracked — 47 keyframes added". You were told a minute of work had succeeded and found nothing on the clip and nothing to undo. It re-checks the layer is still the one in the scene. **A mutation check earned its keep here:** deleting the write-site guard survived the whole suite, because on a long clip the walk bails first and the write is never reached — the assertion was dead. It is only the last defence on a SHORT clip, which is now its own test.
- v9.31 — **Dragging a song that drives an audio-reactive layer recomputed the whole track's loudness sixty times a second, and kept every copy (BUG-HUNT backlog, two findings, one root cause).** Computing one of those envelopes is a pass over the clip's ENTIRE decoded audio — roughly eight million float operations on a three-minute track — and the key it is cached under folds in the source clip's start, trim, duration and speed as well as the behaviour's gain, attack and release. Every one of those moves continuously under a gesture: dragging the song rewrites its start on each pointermove, and the Smoothing slider derives attack and release from its position. The accessor runs per layer per frame. So the drag itself stuttered to unusability on a phone, and each pass permanently retained a ~100 KB array pair in a cache with no cap and no eviction anywhere in the file — a ten-second drag stranded tens of megabytes that nothing ever freed, ending in a mobile-Safari reload. Now: the cache is a bounded LRU, and **a signature must be seen twice before anything is computed**. During a gesture every frame produces a key that is never seen again, so nothing is computed at all; the moment you stop, one compute runs. Counting sightings rather than comparing with the previous key is deliberate — two behaviours on different layers reading the same song alternate keys every frame, and a previous-value check would have starved both forever, which is its own assertion. Export is untouched: it prewarms explicitly before its frame loop. The second finding was filed as *unverified* and it was not — same cause, and the test measures it: 40 settled looks used to leave 40 envelopes resident.
- v9.32 — **Cancel during "Decoding frames…" was a dead button (BUG-HUNT backlog).** The decode is up to 900 sequential seek-and-capture operations, and it had no cancellation hook of any kind — the exporter only checked its cancel flag BETWEEN layers. So pressing Cancel while the overlay read "Decoding frames… 12%" set a flag that nothing read: the app went on seeking and decoding for the whole of that clip (tens of seconds at 1080p, minutes at 4K, since each frame waits up to half a second), allocating up to 1.5GB of bitmaps you had just said you did not want, and only then noticed. On a phone that is both an app that feels dead and a real out-of-memory risk. The decode takes an abort signal now and reads it every frame; the frames already made are freed and NO cache is stored, because a half-length cache is worse than none — the compositor would play the clip from it as though it were complete. The export also stops immediately afterwards instead of carrying on into the audio mix, the AAC probe and the muxer before its next check. **The signal is opt-in**, so every other caller of that decode — the preview, the tracker, the reverse cache — is untouched, and that is one of the three assertions. **A second finding in the same area was struck as STALE rather than fixed:** the report that MP4 export crawls when the tab is backgrounded had already been fixed under queue 47 (partial), and the code carries the measurement in a comment. Re-checked by reading the live code rather than trusting the entry.
